| Harness binding doesn't stop a process the agent starts inside the harness session, or the agent's user starting its own instance of the registered harness. | Both are legitimately below, or are, the registered program, running as the agent's user. | Run the harness under another user, or use a mandatory gateway. | [FAQ](faq.md#what-does-harness-binding-not-stop) |
| The LLM gateway's record is complete only when the agent can't reach the model around it. | The gateway sees only requests sent to it. | Let only the gateway reach the provider (network policy) and set `gateway_mandatory: true`. | [gateway](gateway.md#calling-it) |
| A tool the policy's `structured_output:` names gets no L3 cross-check: a decide for it needs no tool use in the model's response, and a tool use of it needs no decide, even under `gateway_mandatory`, so any tool of that name runs with no decide unseen. The browser pack names Browser Use's actions (`browser_use:*`) only; its output tool with Anthropic models (`AgentOutput`) is the deployment's to name. A child policy can't remove a name its parent declares. | Browser Use asks the model for its actions as structured output, which carries no tool use id to match a decide with. | Read Browser Use's records as decided by the signer, not as proven asked for by the model; declare only names whose calls really come as structured output and that no other tool of yours uses. | [gateway](gateway.md#reconciliation), [Browser Use](integrations/browser-use.md#the-browser-policy-pack) |
| **(1.2)** Tools the provider runs itself (web search, code execution, hosted MCP, ...) can't be held for a decision: they run mid-response. The gateway records their input where the provider returns it (not for image generation) and can only refuse a whole request that declares one (`provider_tools`). OpenAI `shell` and `tool_search` tools count as provider-run, since the provider may host them: with `allow`, list them if your client runs them. | No client-side step happens between the model asking and the provider running them, and a request doesn't always say who runs those two. | Deny provider tools you can't accept in gateway.yaml `provider_tools` (`mcp` covers every hosted MCP server, `mcp:<label>` one); the client gets a 403 naming them. | [gateway](gateway.md#provider-run-tools-12) |
| **(1.3)** Verified tool results catch an edited tool result for a call the signer already completed, and record a result sent before its call completed. They don't catch text the agent adds outside tool results; a tool call the agent writes into the history itself with its result (reconciliation flags it at run.final, `result_without_call` or, under `gateway_mandatory`, `fabricated`, unless the agent also forges a decide, a result and a model report with `gateway_mandatory` off); results past a request's newest 512 or past the 1 MiB of them the gateway digests (newest first), which the agent chooses by adding results of its own: each such call is a signed `capture.gap{result_unchecked}`, and past the newest 1024 not even that; a request under the token of a run the agent registered itself; a result OpenAI Responses doesn't resend (`previous_response_id`) after its first send; and, with the signer unreachable and `model` failing open, a request the gateway forwards unchecked. | The check compares what the gateway sees against the signed results of the same run, by tool_call_id: content outside a tool result, a call with no result in that run, and a request never sent or never checked have nothing to compare with. | Set `gateway_mandatory` and let only the gateway reach the provider; keep the `model` class failing closed; read a run's `result_without_call` and `fabricated` records with its mismatches. | [gateway](gateway.md#verified-tool-results) |
| **(1.3)** Verified tool results are off unless signer.yaml sets `verify_tool_results`. A client whose recorded result isn't the value the model is sent mismatches on honest runs, and `refuse` then stops the agent with a 403: every result of the Claude Code hooks (a digest of the raw output) and the Claude Agent SDK integration (the structured tool response); in Pydantic AI a tool returning a list, which Anthropic gets as one text block per item; in Google ADK under `AnthropicLlm` every result but a dict (and a dict with a `content` key or only `result`), under `LiteLlm` a model or dataclass return (ADK sends its Python repr); and a result of several content blocks (Agent Framework, Strands). Gemini requests (ADK's default) don't pass through the gateway, so nothing is checked. Calls decided under ids of the client's own (the MCP integration, the MCP gateway, LlamaIndex's `ReActAgent` and `CodeActAgent`) are unchecked, and so are calls completed with no result (a Pydantic AI output function's: the model reads `Final result processed.`), error results and results whose content redaction changed. A match says the output was passed on unchanged, not that it is true. | The signer compares commitments: equal values, or a value and its digest, match; two renderings of the same output don't, and a commitment over redacted or absent content opens nothing to compare. | Run `record` first; set `refuse` only once `tracekit verify` shows no `result_mismatch` on honest runs of your own agent, model and tools. | [gateway](gateway.md#verified-tool-results) |
| **(1.3)** Verified tool results: `refuse` refuses a request only for an edited result of a completed call. A result sent before its call completed is a signed `result_mismatch` and the request is forwarded, so an agent can send a result of its own for an allowed call it never completes. A request's results past 1 MiB (as JSON, newest first; a result the run's previous request also sent counts for nothing) are not compared, and neither is any one result over 1 MiB: each such call is a signed `capture.gap{result_unchecked}` (a `gaps` warning in `tracekit verify`), never refused. | A complete can be lost on an honest run (the client gives up after its retries) and every later request sends the result again: refusing would stop the run for good. The gateway digests results in pure Python before forwarding (15 MB of numbers took 3.4 s); the cap bounds that work per request, and a gateway that restarted has no digests cached, so refusing unchecked results would stop honest long runs. | Read a `result_mismatch` whose detail says the result was sent before the call's was recorded with that call's `decided_not_completed` on `run.final`, and a `result_unchecked` gap as a result nothing compared; keep the tool results a model reads under 1 MiB. | [gateway](gateway.md#verified-tool-results) |
| With `durability: ack-on-write` (the default), a power loss can drop records already acknowledged. | Acknowledging before fsync is faster. | Set `durability: ack-on-fsync` in production. | [doctor](doctor.md#v2-checks) |
| A data directory on NFS, SMB, FUSE or similar can't be trusted for locking and fsync. | Those file systems don't give the guarantees the store needs. | Put `data_dir` on a local disk (`D-DATA-FS`). | [doctor](doctor.md#v2-checks) |
| Gemini CLI tool calls carry no call id; Tracekit pairs them by tool and arguments, first in first out. | The harness doesn't send one. | None needed; be aware when reading paired results. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| Reasoning capture reads only Claude Code's transcript, and the v1 model proxy fronts only the Anthropic API. | Other harnesses use other formats and APIs. | Use the v2 gateway or OpenTelemetry for model-side cross-checks. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| Tracekit doesn't see whether Claude Code's sandbox started for a command, what it blocked inside one beyond what Claude Code writes into the result, or the sandbox settings (they aren't signed into the run). | The sandbox runs inside Claude Code's process and keeps no record; hooks see the call and its result. | Set `sandbox.failIfUnavailable: true` so Claude Code never falls back to running unsandboxed; `tracekit doctor` warns when it isn't set (`D-CLAUDE-SANDBOX`). | [Claude Code's sandbox](integrations/claude-code-sandbox.md) |
| Tracekit doesn't see whether Codex ran a command in its sandbox: a request to run outside it (`sandbox_permissions: "require_escalated"`), Codex's answer, or a command an execpolicy `allow` rule let run outside it. Codex's sandbox and approval settings aren't signed into the run, and no rule holds an escalation as `TK-A003` holds Claude Code's unsandboxed retry. Nor does it see the shell an `exec_command` names (`shell`: bash, zsh, sh, pwsh or cmd; the policy reads every command as a POSIX shell's) or what is typed into a running command (`write_stdin`, which fires no hook), and with its in-development `features.exec_permission_approvals` on, Codex can run some calls with no hook at all. A relative path in a shell command is decided as typed, not as a path in the folder the call names for itself (`workdir`). | Codex's `PreToolUse` hook sends a shell call's command only; the sandbox and its approvals run inside Codex. Codex sends `write_stdin` no hook, as input to a command that already had one, and its in-development permission-approvals path builds the hook in a way some calls don't pass; such a call runs unhooked. The policy reads a relative path as typed, by its certain suffix: its path rules read no working directory. | Keep escalations with a person (`approval_policy = "on-request"`, `approvals_reviewer = "user"`), or set `approval_policy = "never"` so Codex refuses them; keep `allow` rules narrow. The policy still decides every command before Codex's approval. Leave `exec_permission_approvals` off, and read a command that starts a shell or interpreter with nothing to run (`bash`, `python3`) as one that can run anything typed into it. Write path rules that also work on a relative path's suffix. | [Codex's sandbox](integrations/codex-sandbox.md) |
| **(1.5.1)** Codex runs Tracekit's hooks only once a person has trusted them as they are, and again after they change (a new init can change them, as one run from another install does). Interactive `codex` asks at startup; `codex exec`, or a person who chose to continue without trusting, skips them without a word, so no Codex call is recorded or decided and the signer sees no gap, because no hook ever ran. `tracekit doctor` (`D-CODEX-HOOK-TRUST`) recomputes Codex's hash of each of Tracekit's hooks, v1 or v2, as codex-rs computes it (October 2026), and checks that Codex's `config.toml` (`$CODEX_HOME`'s, by its real path as Codex reads it, else `~/.codex`'s) records trust for that hash and that none is turned off. A Codex that hashes differently reads as changed, never as trusted. For a hook with a setting Tracekit doesn't write (`async`, `statusMessage`, `commandWindows` or `additionalContextLimit`, added by hand) it says trust is recorded but it can't confirm it's for that hook; with Python 3.9 or 3.10 it can't read the file and says it can't confirm. In system mode init and doctor use the agent user's `~/.codex`: that user's `CODEX_HOME` isn't known to them. | Codex keys a hook's trust to a hash of its own normal form of the hook, so a person, not init, must review a hook before it runs. | After `tracekit init --agent codex`, run `codex` and trust Tracekit's hooks (at its startup prompt, or type `/hooks`); init ends by saying so, and doctor warns while they aren't trusted or have changed since. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| A process's `CODEX_TOOL_CALL_ID` names the Codex call that started it, the `tool_call_id` of that call's records, but the process and its children can change or remove it. | Codex sets it as a label for correlation, not authorization. | Use it to find the signed call behind an effect; read a missing or unknown id as unexplained, and a match as a pointer, not proof. | [Codex's sandbox](integrations/codex-sandbox.md#codex_tool_call_id) |
| Gemini CLI's whole-CLI sandbox (`tools.sandbox`, `GEMINI_SANDBOX`, `-s`) runs without Tracekit: in a container the hooks `tracekit init` writes don't run and the session isn't recorded, with no gap; under Seatbelt the hook can't keep its state and blocks every call. | Gemini runs the whole CLI, its hooks included, inside that sandbox: a container gets your user settings without their hooks, and Seatbelt lets a process write only the workspace, temporary and cache directories. | Use Gemini's tool sandbox (`security.toolSandboxing: true`) instead. `tracekit doctor` warns when Gemini's settings turn the whole-CLI sandbox on (`D-GEMINI-SANDBOX`), but can't see `GEMINI_SANDBOX` or `-s` at launch. | [Gemini CLI's sandbox](integrations/gemini-sandbox.md#not-the-whole-cli-sandbox) |
| Tracekit doesn't see sandbox permissions Gemini CLI grants outside a call's arguments (what its approval mode allows, an expansion Gemini proposes itself, one allowed for good), and Gemini's sandbox settings aren't signed into the run. | Gemini's `BeforeTool` hook sends the call's arguments; the sandbox and its approvals run inside Gemini. | Avoid YOLO mode and answer expansion requests once, not for good; `TK-A004` holds each expansion a call's `additional_permissions` asks for. | [Gemini CLI's sandbox](integrations/gemini-sandbox.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |
| `tracekit doctor` doesn't see Gemini CLI's folder trust: Gemini ignores an untrusted project's settings, so `D-GEMINI-SANDBOX` can read a project setting Gemini doesn't apply, or miss what Gemini does. | Folder trust is Gemini's own state, kept outside the settings files doctor reads. | Put the sandbox settings that matter in your user settings (`~/.gemini/settings.json`), which folder trust doesn't switch off. | [Gemini CLI's sandbox](integrations/gemini-sandbox.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |
| Qwen Code runs a project's hooks only in a trusted folder: in an untrusted one the hooks `tracekit init --project` wrote don't run, so nothing gates or records that session's calls. A `disableAllHooks: true` in any Qwen Code settings file turns every hook off, Tracekit's included, and so do `--safe-mode` and `--bare` at launch. With `QWEN_HOME` set, Qwen Code reads its user settings from that directory, not from `~/.qwen/settings.json`, where `tracekit init --agent qwen` writes. | Qwen Code skips an untrusted workspace's hooks and honours its own switches without telling them; `tracekit doctor` reads neither its folder trust nor its settings layers. | Install in the user's settings (`tracekit init --dev --agent qwen`), trust the folder, leave `disableAllHooks` unset, don't launch with `--safe-mode` or `--bare`, unset `QWEN_HOME` (or copy the hooks into `$QWEN_HOME/settings.json`), and check with `tracekit view --dev` that calls are recorded. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| Qwen Code's model text and reasoning are not recorded: its transcript is hashed at each hook when the payload names it, but not read, so `tracekit analyze --v2` has only the hook's prompt and no reasoning for its runs. | Its transcript is not in a format Tracekit parses, and its hooks carry no model text. | Route model calls through the LLM gateway for text the agent can't write; read Qwen Code runs by their calls. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#what-was-asked-and-said) |
| The Claude Code hook records as typed every part of a prompt that isn't in a wrapper it recognises (such as a paste block, a skill's body, a message from another session, agent, teammate, plugin, observer or channel, a task or system notification, a scheduled task, a message Claude Code marks as from a non-user source, uploads, command output, system reminders and fetched web content). Wrapper text it doesn't recognise therefore counts as the person's: a value it holds isn't untrusted (a `from: untrusted` rule allows a call that uses it), its constraints are live, and `tracekit analyze --v2` reads it as a request. Claude Code 2.1.286 sends three kinds of turn no person typed to the hook with no wrapper at all, so they count as typed too: a prompt a plugin submits, a notice from another Claude Code session, and a poll event (a custom element such as `<ci-status>`). Once Claude Code sends its payload's `source` field, a turn whose source isn't `user` or `sdk` is recorded whole as untrusted (`claude-code:<source>`), and none of its constraints are live. The Gemini CLI hook recognises only its referenced files block; the Codex, Cursor, Qwen Code and Factory Droid hooks recognise no wrapper. | A hook gets the prompt as one string, with no signed boundary between what was typed and what the harness added; the wrapper spellings are the ones seen in each harness, and a harness release can add one. Claude Code adds its wrappers to those three kinds of turn only after the hooks run; its hook input schema defines `source` (`user`, `sdk`, `system`, `loop_wakeup`, `schedule_wakeup`, `poll_event`), but 2.1.286 leaves it out. | Report wrapper text Tracekit records as typed. Where a value must not be used, put a deny or ask rule on the destination, not only `from: untrusted`. Install only plugins and poll sources you trust to speak for you, and update Claude Code: a release that sends `source` closes the three bare paths with no change on your side. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#what-was-asked-and-said) |
| A paste Claude Code sends inline (its default) is recorded as typed, and its constraints are live. A recognised wrapper inside it, or typed text that looks like one, makes the rest of the prompt untrusted from there, text typed after it included: a value in it isn't trusted, and `tracekit analyze --v2` reads none of it as a request. When the wrapper starts a line, constraints after it aren't live, and Claude Code shows a notice naming them; when it follows other text on its line (a tag's name in a sentence), they are live up to the next wrapper that starts a line, and analyze checks them. Constraints in a wrapped part that starts a line (a paste block, a slash command's arguments, a scheduled prompt, a relayed message) are recorded but not live. | The hook can't tell a typed tag from one the harness added, or a pasted line from a typed one; wrapped parts are text the harness added or relayed, which the person didn't type. Claude Code starts a line with each wrapper it adds, so one mid-line may be typed, and a constraint only holds more calls. | Type constraints before a large paste or in a prompt of their own; restating one after a wrapper at the start of a line doesn't make it live. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#what-was-asked-and-said) |
| Untrusted text in one prompt is recorded up to 50,134,272 characters, shared equally across the wrapper kinds in it. Past that, the text isn't recorded: the hook leaves one event number unsent before a `claude-code:harness-cut` note naming the cut kinds, so the signer writes a signed `client_counter_gap` and marks the run's provenance index partial, and Claude Code shows a notice. For the rest of the run, every `from: untrusted` rule applies to each call its pattern matches, whatever the value's source. | Each observation the hook sends the signer is one frame of at most 1 MiB. The cap is 48 frames' worth of characters, so the hook doesn't hold an ASCII prompt long (about 7 s at the cap); text that isn't ASCII takes more frames, up to about four times as many, since each frame holds 1 MiB of UTF-8. A cut value never reaches the signer's untrusted-value index, so the gap marks the index incomplete rather than let such a value pass. | Keep pasted and attached text in one prompt under the cap. After a cut, expect `from: untrusted` rules to ask about (or deny) every call they match until the run ends; start a new session to return to normal. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#what-was-asked-and-said) |
| Qwen Code records as the person's prompt only a `UserPromptSubmit` that carries `submitted_prompt` (Qwen Code 0.21.1 on in its TUI, 0.21.12 on for `qwen -p`), and records that text, not `prompt`, into which Qwen Code expands `@file` contents and a large paste (over 1,000 characters or 10 lines: `submitted_prompt` holds its `[Pasted Content N chars]` placeholder). The rest are not recorded: its continuations after tool results (an empty `prompt`), a Stop hook's reason it sends back to the model, and typed prompts Qwen Code sends without the field: Qwen Code before those releases; the TUI with Vim mode on (or input it can't trace to the composer); ACP clients and daemon hosts that send no `qwen.submittedPrompt` declaration (Web Shell sends one; realtime voice handoffs and channel messages, a person's included, never carry it). The live constraints the hook hands the signer are read from the same text, so a Stop hook's reason, a referenced file's contents or a large paste's text never state one. | Qwen Code fires `UserPromptSubmit` for those sends too, and `submitted_prompt` is the only field that tells a submission from them. | Use Qwen Code 0.21.1 or later (0.21.12 for `qwen -p`) with Vim mode off, and an ACP client that declares `qwen.submittedPrompt`; type a constraint you want held into the prompt itself, not into a large paste or a referenced file; read a prompt it didn't record from Qwen Code's own session history. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#what-was-asked-and-said) |
| On native Windows, `tracekit init --agent qwen` writes `"shell": "powershell"` into each hook, so Qwen Code runs it under Windows PowerShell whether it was started from cmd.exe or Git Bash, with the command in PowerShell's `& "..."` form (bare when the Python path needs no quotes) ending in `; exit $LASTEXITCODE` (PowerShell hands a native exit 2 back as 1, and Qwen Code blocks a call only at 2); a `PreToolUse` hook whose Python didn't run or failed exits 2, so the call is blocked. Qwen Code starts PowerShell with `-Command` and no `-NoProfile`, so the user's PowerShell profile runs before each hook. Qwen Code before 0.15.0 ignores a hook's `"shell"`: under cmd.exe the bare form runs (the tail reaches the hook as arguments: the v2 hook takes the event its command names, the v1 hook, which names none, the payload's) but a quoted Python path can't start (Node escapes its quotes), so every call runs unchecked and unrecorded; under Git Bash a quoted path is a syntax error, so every event exits 2 and every call and prompt is blocked, and with a bare path 0.14.5 sent no request to the model in our test, so no call ran. An earlier init wrote no `"shell"`, so its hook ran under the shell Qwen Code was started from, and with a Python path that needed quotes it couldn't start under cmd.exe. On another event, a hook that can't start (its Python moved or was uninstalled) or crashes isn't recorded. Tested with Qwen Code 0.26.5 and 0.14.5 on Windows in runs on the contributor's fork ([run](https://github.com/khushi-git-7/Tracekit/actions/runs/41506179637), [run](https://github.com/khushi-git-7/Tracekit/actions/runs/41647694734)). | Windows has no POSIX shell to wrap the hook in, and Qwen Code picks its shell from how it was started unless the hook names one. | Use Qwen Code 0.15.0 or later; run `tracekit init --dev --v2 --agent qwen` again after upgrading Tracekit and after moving or reinstalling Python, or use WSL; check with `tracekit view --dev` that calls are recorded. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| `tracekit srt-decider` sees only requests to the hosts in its `--allow-domain` list; srt-proxy refuses the rest itself and Tracekit records nothing of them. | srt-proxy's decider protocol has the decider name its hosts before the first request, and no entry matches every host. | List every host the workload needs; a missing one shows in the workload as `403` with `X-Deny-Reason: host_not_allowed`. | [sandbox-runtime](integrations/sandbox-runtime.md#setup) |
| A request `tracekit srt-decider` allowed is recorded as allowed, not as sent: Tracekit doesn't see the response, nor whether srt-proxy forwarded it (it still refuses a host that resolves to a private address, and an answer that comes after its 5 s wait). | The protocol carries requests and verdicts, nothing after. | Keep the signer close to the decider so answers come in time; route calls whose result matters through the [tool executor](executor.md), which records it. | [sandbox-runtime](integrations/sandbox-runtime.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |
| An `ask` through `tracekit srt-decider` refuses the request at once (`403`); the request is allowed only when the workload sends it again after a person approved it. | srt-proxy waits 5 s for each answer. | Have the workload retry a `403` that names an approval, or send the request again yourself once it is approved. | [sandbox-runtime](integrations/sandbox-runtime.md#what-is-decided) |
| A `tracekit srt-decider` approval binds the method, URL and body digest: not the headers, nor the body of a GET, HEAD or OPTIONS. A body over 32 MiB is refused. | srt-proxy shows a body only when asked, up to 32 MiB, and not for those methods. | Where a header or such a body changes what a request does, deny that host by policy rather than ask. | [sandbox-runtime](integrations/sandbox-runtime.md#what-is-decided) |
| One `tracekit srt-decider` is one run, whatever process sent a request, and the approvals it waits on live in its memory: after a restart the same request asks again. When the signer closes the run (no request for `idle_s`, an hour by default), the decider refuses the next request and stops srt-proxy. | srt-proxy's requests name no client; a closed run records nothing more. | Run one srt-proxy and decider per agent run, under a supervisor that restarts it; after a restart, approve the new request. | [sandbox-runtime](integrations/sandbox-runtime.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |
| `tracekit srt-decider` decides each request on its method, URL and the body's SHA-256 only: content rules never see the body, so a rule on what a request sends (a secret, a customer record) can't match it. | Only the digest goes to the signer, so the run never holds the body; the digest binds an approval to it. | Decide such hosts by host and method; send calls whose content matters through the [tool executor](executor.md) or the [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md), whose policy sees their arguments. | [sandbox-runtime](integrations/sandbox-runtime.md#what-is-decided) |
| **(1.2)** A run is recorded as a child (`register_run` `parent`) only when the caller presents the parent's run token as the identity it was issued to, on the parent's signer, while the parent is not final. | The token proves the caller may act for the parent run; a final run takes nothing more. | Start sub-agent runs from the code holding the parent's handle (`client.run(..., parent=handle)`), or with a `delegate_run` token for the sub-agent's identity, before the parent finishes. The refusal (`run_token_invalid`, `unknown_run`, `run_closed`) says which. | [format v2](format-v2.md#3-events) |
| v1 OTLP receiver and ingest gateway are experimental. | They are being rebuilt; they run only with `--experimental`. | Use the v2 signer's OTLP import. | [OpenTelemetry](otel.md#receiving-traces) |
| v1 OTLP policy is evaluated after the fact: a call a rule would deny is recorded as `flag`, not stopped. | Spans arrive when the work is done. | Block with hooks, an SDK `tool()` or an adapter. | [OpenTelemetry](otel.md#what-it-means) |
| **(1.2)** An MCP call decided as `mcp:<server>/<tool>` reconciles with the model's tool use only when the model's name is the bare `<tool>`, `mcp__<server>__<tool>` (Claude Code) or `mcp_<server>__<tool>` (OpenAI Agents JS with `includeServerInToolNames`); its arguments are compared under that name. A framework that shows the server another way (mcp-agent's `<server>_<tool>`) gets `args_mismatch` for each call; with a bare name, two servers' same-named tools in one model turn are told apart only by call id. | Only names that can't be a different tool's are accepted: a suffix match would let an agent rename `delete_account` to `mcp:x/account`. | Expect `args_mismatch` for such frameworks until the policy can list their servers; keep the model's call ids (the shipped adapters do). | [MCP](integrations/mcp.md) |
| **(1.2)** With a bare model name (OpenAI Agents JS, the Vercel AI SDK, the mcp Python client), nothing ties the server in `mcp:<server>/<tool>` to the model's call: an agent that decides the model's `query` as `mcp:notes/query` instead of `mcp:postgres/query` is reconciled, and rules keyed on the server (`mcp:postgres/*`) don't apply. A server or tool with an edge `_` or a `__`, or a tool named like a prefixed form, gets no alias at all. | The model never saw the server; only the agent's adapter names it. | Key MCP rules on the tool name (it is also checked under its bare name) or use names that carry the server (`includeServerInToolNames`, Claude Code); put servers that need server-level rules behind the MCP gateway, which decides what it forwards. | [MCP](integrations/mcp.md), [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md) |
| **(1.2)** A long-running signer keeps a small stub of every run it has seen (about 1 KB in memory, 0.5 KB in each snapshot, plus the file store's per-record index), each final run's first late writers (up to 64), and every incident, resolved ones too (`incident_list` serves them); the request-id cache holds at most 100,000 answers (10,000 per identity). Snapshots are written whole on the writer, pausing writes for about 1 s per 300,000 runs. | Final runs answer tokens, late writes and `run_exists` from memory; the stubs are the signer's index of what it has signed. | Restart into a new store (a new `data_dir`, the old one kept for export and verification) once a store holds millions of runs; size memory at about 3 KB per run. | [deployment](deploy.md) |
| OpenCode (1.x) sends plugins no session or prompt events, and no `tool.execute.after` for a call that fails, is declined in its own permission prompt or is aborted: its run closes only at the signer's idle timeout, no prompts, live constraints or model text are recorded, and those calls stay `decided_not_completed` gaps. | Its plugin API has tool hooks only, and runs the after hook only for a call that completed. | Read such gaps as calls that didn't complete; use Claude Code, Codex or Gemini CLI where prompts and session ends must be in the record. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| **(experimental)** goose support hasn't been checked in a live goose session (1.48.0 or later). On Windows goose runs hooks with `sh -c`, so without `sh` on PATH (Git Bash) every call is blocked. Code Mode (`execute_javascript`, and `execute_typescript` or `execute_bash` before goose 1.56) is denied, so Code Mode can't be used with Tracekit. | The tests drive goose's documented payloads and its source's hook runner; Code Mode runs tool calls through `dispatch_tool`, which runs no hooks. | Use goose's own tools rather than Code Mode; on Windows install Git Bash. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| goose sends no tool result on PostToolUse and no error text on PostToolUseFailure: a completed call's tool.result carries no output, and a failed call's carries ok=False with no error text. Its installed hooks are tool events only (no session or prompt events), so no prompts, live constraints, transcript hashes or model text are recorded. | goose's hook payload table lists no result or error field for tool events, and Tracekit installs only PreToolUse, PostToolUse and PostToolUseFailure. | Read a goose run's calls from their decisions and arguments, not from outputs; use Claude Code, Codex or Gemini CLI where results and prompts must be in the record. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| GitHub Copilot CLI's installed hooks are tool events only (`preToolUse`, `postToolUse`, `postToolUseFailure`): no prompts, live constraints, transcript hashes or model text are recorded, and its run closes only at the signer's idle timeout. A release before 1.0.107 sends no `toolCallId`, and every call of it is blocked. Copilot's documentation says a hook that times out fails open: Tracekit's hook answers within 540 s, under the 600 s timeout init sets, but a hook entry edited to a shorter timeout lets a held call run when it is cut (`tracekit doctor`, `D-HOOKS-TIMEOUT`). Checked in live Copilot CLI 1.0.107 sessions on Linux only; Windows, the VS Code agent and the cloud agent are untested. | Tracekit reads Copilot's tool payloads only, and pairs a call with its result by the id Copilot gives it. | Update Copilot CLI to 1.0.107 or later; use Claude Code, Codex or Gemini CLI where prompts and session ends must be in the record. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| OpenCode's experimental `batch` tool (`experimental.batch_tool`, OpenCode 1.x before 1.4 only) is blocked whatever its calls: the plugin refuses it before it runs. | OpenCode runs the calls inside a batch without plugin hooks, so Tracekit could neither decide nor record them. | Leave `experimental.batch_tool` off; the model then makes each call itself, and each is decided. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| The OpenCode plugin kills a hook still running after 600 seconds and blocks its call, so a hook stuck on a slow or hung signer blocks the call rather than OpenCode. OpenCode 2.0 doesn't load 1.x plugins, so its hooks would stop with no error. | The plugin awaits each hook's process without blocking OpenCode's event loop, so OpenCode keeps streaming and decides parallel calls at once; the hook answers within 540 seconds, a held call's approval wait included, and 600 seconds is the other coding agents' pre-tool timeout. | Check a blocked call's message: `did not answer in 600 s` means the hook hung (`tracekit doctor` checks the signer); pin OpenCode 1.x until Tracekit supports 2.0. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| **(experimental)** Kilo Code support was run with the Kilo CLI 7.8.18 on Linux only: its VS Code extension, Windows and macOS haven't been tried. Kilo's `notebook_execute` (VS Code only, with `experimental.native_notebook_tools`, off by default) is blocked whatever its arguments: the hook refuses it before it runs. | It runs a notebook cell in the live kernel; the cell's code was written by `notebook_edit`, which is decided on its `path` alone, so no command rule ever reads it. | Leave `experimental.native_notebook_tools` off in `kilo.json`; the model then runs code through `bash`, and each command is decided. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| Kilo's own tools that Tracekit doesn't map pass through under their own names, as unknown tools: only the policy's `unknown_tools` (`TK-UNKNOWN-TOOL`) applies, and no rule reads their arguments. Among them: `browser_open` (VS Code; opens its `url` in a browser), `repo_clone` (clones its `repository`, a git URL or `owner/repo`, into Kilo's cache), `kilo_memory_save` (saves a note to Kilo's project memory, which later sessions read back) and `agent_manager` (VS Code; starts sessions). MCP tools reach the plugin under OpenCode's `<server>_<tool>` name, so they aren't decided as `mcp:<server>/<tool>` and MCP rules don't apply to them (OpenCode's too). A session `agent_manager` starts in worktree mode runs in a new git worktree under the project's `.kilo/worktrees/`; Kilo looks for project config only from there up to that worktree's root, so a `--project` install's `.kilo/plugin/` in the main checkout doesn't reach it (read from Kilo 7.8.18's source; not tried). | Tracekit maps the tools that run a command or read or write a file; these act through Kilo's own services, and Kilo's MCP names don't say where the server name ends. | Set `unknown_tools` to `ask` or `deny` where these tools matter; with Agent Manager worktrees, install without `--project`: the global plugin in `~/.config/kilo` is loaded in every session. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md), [policy v2](policy-v2.md) |
| Cursor's hook names a call by what it does on the computer, not by the model's tool: an edit by `StrReplace`, `ApplyPatch` or `EditNotebook` reaches its `preToolUse` hook as a `Read` and then a `Write` of the whole file, both under the model's call id, so the record shows a `Write` of the file, not the tool. The other names Cursor 3.29.15's hook sends pass through under their own names, as unknown tools (`TK-UNKNOWN-TOOL`: flagged on the dev and coding packs, held on server and strict), and no rule reads their arguments: `Fetch` (a `url`), `List`, `ReadLints`, `WriteShellStdin` (only `shell_id` and the length of the typed text, not the text), `ListMcpResources`, `FetchMcpResource` (with the `download_path` it writes), `ComputerUse`, `RecordScreen` and `XSearch`. | Cursor fires `preToolUse` where a call runs: an edit's file read and its write each fire it, under the names of those operations; the model's tool names are only in its transcripts. Its hooks docs list `Shell`, `Read`, `Write`, `Grep`, `Delete`, `Task` and `MCP:` tools; the other names are read from Cursor 3.29.15's code. | Put an `ask` or `deny` rule of your own on the names your agents shouldn't use; read what an edit changed in the file its `Write` names. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| Kilo's `send_file` uploads a file to Kilo's mobile app but is decided as a `Read` of it, so a secrets file sent that way is only flagged (`TK-F005`) where the same upload from a shell is held. | The upload goes to the user's own Kilo app, and the hook sees one tool name per call. | Hold `send_file` in a rule of your own if your agents shouldn't send files to the app. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| Kilo's `generate_image` given an `image` is decided as a `Read` of that file and of the `path` it writes, so the write rules don't see that `path` (a write under `~/.config/kilo/` or `.git/hooks/` that a `Write` there would hold or deny); Kilo gives the written file an image extension (`.png`, `.jpg` or `.jpeg`). | The hook decides each call as one tool, and the file it reads and sends off is the larger risk. | Hold `generate_image` in a rule of your own if image writes outside the project matter to you. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| Factory Droid's documented hook stdin gives a tool call no id. Two identical calls in flight are paired first in first out, so their results cannot be distinguished if they complete in another order. An allowed call with no PostToolUse (declined in Droid's permission prompt, blocked by another hook, or failed; Droid has no PostToolUseFailure) stays queued, so the next identical call's result can be paired to it and the call that ran closes as an error at session end. | Only the tool name and arguments connect Droid's PreToolUse and PostToolUse events. | Avoid concurrent identical calls when their result attribution matters. Blocked calls leave the queue so a later allowed call does not inherit their id. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| **(experimental)** Factory Droid support has not been checked in a live authenticated session or on native Windows. Its transcript format is not read, so model text and reasoning are not captured. Its documented events include no PostToolUseFailure; the minimum version is unknown, and whether Task-started sub-droids fire hooks is unknown. ApplyPatch's field and format are from Factory CLI 0.249.0's tool listing, not a captured hook payload. | Tests drive the documented stdin through Tracekit's real v1 pipeline and the v2 signer; the tool listing does not exercise hooks. | Verify a denied and a held call in Droid before relying on the integration; inspect the run with `tracekit last`. A failed call without PostToolUse remains incomplete until SessionEnd or the signer's gap handling. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| An agent's edit of Factory Droid's settings or hooks (`.factory/`, `~/.factory/`) is held (`TK-F009`, `TK-F015`), as for the other harnesses. Managed-only hooks or globally disabled hooks can also keep the user and project hooks from running. | The settings rules hold every write to the folder, a droid definition (`.factory/droids/`) an agent edits in ordinary work among them; and Droid applies its own settings before it launches the hooks, so a setting that turns them off takes effect before Tracekit sees a call. | Keep hook configuration outside the agent's write access, leave hooks enabled, check the effective hooks in Droid's `/hooks` UI, and verify calls appear in `tracekit last`. | [Droid hooks](https://docs.factory.com/harness/hooks) |
| Factory Droid's `UserPromptSubmit` prompt is recorded as the person's (trusted), and the hook reads live constraints from it, but it isn't confirmed that only a prompt the person typed fires it. | Devin's hook payload gives a tool call no id. | **(experimental)** Devin support has not been tested in a live Devin CLI or Desktop session: tests drive Devin's documented payloads through Tracekit's v1 pipeline and the v2 signer. Only `exec`'s input (`command`, `shell_id`) is documented, and Devin's decoder may match a key, or a tool's name, ignoring case: a tool is read under its name in any case (`EXEC`), `exec` is decided on its command under any spelling (`Command`), with `\r` read as a line end (should Devin type it into a terminal), and is denied in the hook with no command (or input that isn't an object) or a control character other than a tab or line end; a `!` in it is decided as written, though bash would expand it from history if Devin typed the command into an interactive shell; and a call that sends an argument's name twice (in any case, with any value: `{"command": …, "command": null}`) is denied in the hook, as a decoder may keep either value; `read`, `write` and `edit` are decided on every path under a key whose name, case and `_` ignored, ends in `path`, `paths` or `file`, `apply_patch` on every file a `*** … File:` header or a unified diff's `---`, `+++` or `diff --git` line names in any of its arguments, and `write_to_process` as a `Bash` command on its one argument besides `shell_id`, the text it writes, whatever its name. A path under a key with another name (`filename`, `target`) isn't decided, so beside one of those keys it goes undecided. A `read`, `write` or `edit` with none of those keys, an `apply_patch` with no file header, a `webfetch` with no `url`, or with one under another spelling too (`URL`), and a `write_to_process` with no text or another argument beside it, or whose text holds a control character (any C0 or C1 control but a line end, or DEL: the terminal's line editor acts on it, so the line that runs isn't the text; `\r` is read as a line end) or a history expansion interactive bash would make (a `!` before anything but a blank, a line end, `=` or `(`, or a `^` starting a line), or that uses a form of shell state Tracekit knows (below), are denied in the hook, before the signer, so they have no signed decision. A persistent shell's state, set by an earlier typed line, can change what a later line runs, while the policy decides one call at a time. Tracekit denies the forms it knows: history replay (`fc`; `history -s`, `-r` or `-n`), an `alias` definition, a function definition, `trap`, `bind`, `enable`, the `exec` builtin (it can make the shell read commands from a file, or replace it), `set` options (`set -o vi`, `set +H`), `shopt`, `hash`, `source` or `.`, setting `PROMPT_COMMAND`, `PS0` to `PS4`, `PATH`, `BASH_ENV`, `BASH_ALIASES` or `BASH_CMDS` (alias's and hash's tables) or a loader variable (`LD_PRELOAD`, `LD_LIBRARY_PATH`, `LD_AUDIT`, any `DYLD_*`), and a nameref (`declare`, `typeset` or `local -n`), which sets a variable under another name. Other variables a program reads (a pager or an editor: `GIT_PAGER`, `EDITOR`) are decided only as the line that sets them reads; the command a program later runs from one isn't seen. The rest of a shell's state (an ordinary variable a later line expands, the directory) isn't tracked from one call to the next, and `exec` calls aren't checked for these forms (a script there may source a venv or define a function), though Devin may run them in the same shell: where that matters, run Devin in a sandbox or in Tracekit Box. A relative path is decided as typed: the payload names no working directory. `run_subagent` passes through under its own name; whether a sub-agent's calls run hooks is not documented. Its transcript isn't read, so no model text is recorded, and its `UserPromptSubmit` prompt is recorded as the person's without a check that only a typed prompt fires it. A hook that outlives its timeout, exits otherwise than 0 or 2, or prints output Devin can't read lets the call run. | Devin is closed source; its hooks reference names the tools and their events, not their arguments, and treats any hook failure but exit 2 as a non-blocking error. | In a real Devin session, check that a denied call doesn't run, that a held call runs when Devin tries it again after `tracekit approvals approve`, and that its calls appear in `tracekit last`. If a call is denied for its arguments, report their names (the block message lists them) on [#566](https://github.com/Cygnux-Labs/Tracekit/issues/566). | [Devin hooks](https://docs.devin.ai/cli/extensibility/hooks/overview) |

Under Windows PowerShell a block's exit 2 can come back as 1, which Devin doesn't read as a block, and a hook that can't start (its Python moved or was uninstalled) isn't wrapped to fail closed. In each case the call runs undecided and unrecorded. Not tested on Windows. | Windows has no POSIX shell to wrap the hook in, and Devin's hooks reference names no Windows shell. | On Windows, run Devin under WSL. Otherwise install Python under a plain path (`C:\Python312`), run `tracekit init --dev --v2 --agent devin` again after moving or reinstalling Python, and check in Devin that a call the policy denies doesn't run and that its calls appear in `tracekit last`. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| Devin Desktop runs no hooks in Restricted Mode, so no call made there is decided or recorded. Devin's cloud sessions run hooks too (through plugins), on a VM where a laptop's signer isn't reachable: there each call goes by the hook's fail mode, and none is recorded. | Restricted Mode turns hooks off before they load; a cloud session runs on Devin's machines, not the one with the signer. | Don't use Restricted Mode where calls must be recorded; for cloud sessions, run a signer they can reach ([deployment](deploy.md)) or don't install Tracekit's hooks in what they read. | [Devin Desktop](https://docs.devin.ai/desktop/devin-local/overview) |

| OpenHands' hook payload gives a tool call no id (`metadata` is `{}`), so calls are paired by tool and arguments, first in first out. A call that runs no PostToolUse stays queued, and the next identical call's result is recorded against it: one the person rejects in the CLI's confirmation prompt (always-ask is its default; `UserRejectObservation`) and one the tool fails with an error before it runs (`AgentErrorEvent`). | OpenHands' `HookEvent` carries no action id, though OpenHands has one when it runs the hook, and neither observation runs a hook. Results come back in call order at any `tool_concurrency_limit`, so the limit itself doesn't mis-pair. | When attribution matters, treat the result of an identical call after a rejected or failed one as unattributed; a call Tracekit denies leaves the queue. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |

Nothing is decided or recorded there, and there is no gap. | OpenHands' `HookConfig.load` stops at the first file it finds. | Run `tracekit doctor` in the repo before running OpenHands on it (`D-OPENHANDS-PROJECT` warns), check an untrusted repo for `.openhands/hooks.json`, add Tracekit's hooks to it with `tracekit init --dev --v2 --agent openhands --project`, or use system mode, where the agent can't change what runs. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |

A remote or Docker sandbox can't reach a local signer, so those sessions aren't covered. `~/.openhands/hooks.json` is where the CLI reads (1.16.0 as released, on SDK 1.21.0: `Path.home()`; `$OPENHANDS_PERSISTENCE_DIR` moves its settings, not this file); `$OH_PERSISTENCE_DIR`, which SDK main reads in its place, isn't followed. An SDK app runs no hooks unless it passes `hook_config=`. **(experimental)** Tested through OpenHands' own `HookManager` with its real tools (the payloads in `tests/data/harness/openhands.json`), not yet in a live OpenHands CLI session. | The sandbox has no route to a dev signer's socket; the SDK leaves loading hooks to the app. | Use the CLI or Agent Canvas locally; in an SDK app pass `hook_config=HookConfig.load(working_dir=...)`. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |

An agent that answers a program's prompt or recalls history waits for an approval each time. | Such a call runs with what came before or comes after it (`git \` then `push --force origin main`, or `UP` then `ENTER`), which a decision on one call can't see. | Send each command whole, in one call; approve a typed answer once you've seen what it answers. | On Windows, OpenHands' hook command isn't wrapped, so a hook that can't start lets the call run (OpenHands blocks only at exit 2), and its terminal is PowerShell, which the policy decides as Bash. | `install._blocking` wraps a command in `/bin/sh`, and the v2 policy reads bash, not PowerShell. | Run OpenHands on macOS or Linux (or WSL) when Tracekit guards it. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |

Then no agy call is decided or recorded, there is no gap, and agy doesn't say so. | agy skips the hooks itself, and Tracekit sees nothing of a session whose hooks never run. | Sign agy in with a Google account, and don't set `"enabled": false` on Tracekit's hook in any `hooks.json`. | The global `~/.gemini/config/hooks.json` that `tracekit init --agent antigravity` writes is read by the Antigravity IDE app too ([#1329](https://github.com/google-antigravity/antigravity-cli/issues/1329)): the app's tool calls are decided and recorded as well, as agent `antigravity`, and a held call stalls the app's turn until someone answers it. #1329 reports the app running a call a hook answered `allow` that headless `agy -p` still declines ([#1053](https://github.com/google-antigravity/antigravity-cli/issues/1053)); whether that skips the app's own approval prompt is untested. | agy, the IDE and the app share one global hook config, and a hook payload doesn't say which of them sent it. | Install per workspace (`--project`: `.agents/hooks.json`) to keep the hooks out of other workspaces; answer held calls from another terminal (`tracekit approvals list`); keep the app's own approval settings as they are. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#antigravity-cli) |

A relative path in a `run_command` is decided as typed, not as a path in the folder the call names for itself (`Cwd`). | The field is undocumented (#1304 asks for it to be); `TK-A003` already holds the same request from Claude Code. The policy reads a relative path as typed, by its certain suffix: its path rules read no working directory. | Keep agy's own approval for commands outside its sandbox on; read a `TK-A003` hold on an `antigravity` run as agy's sandbox. Write path rules that also work on a relative path's suffix. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#antigravity-cli) |

An agent's edit of agy's hook files (`~/.gemini/config/hooks.json`, a project's `.agents/hooks.json`) is flagged (`TK-F009`, `TK-F015`), not held, and whether a project hook named `tracekit` replaces the global one is untested. A call whose arguments name a field Tracekit reads in another case, twice in different cases or as null, or carry a key outside ASCII, is denied in the hook before it reaches the signer, so it has no signed decision (agy's decoder may read such a key as the field; its own calls spell each field once, exactly). | agy acts on a hook's answer only once it returns, and logs no line when a hook runs ([#893](https://github.com/google-antigravity/antigravity-cli/issues/893)); the settings rules flag rather than hold, as for the other harnesses. | List and answer held calls from another terminal (`tracekit approvals list`); check that agy's `/hooks` lists `tracekit`; keep `.agents/` out of the agent's write access. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#antigravity-cli) |
| **(experimental)** Crush support has not been checked in a live Crush session or on native Windows. Tracekit installs only `SessionStart`, `SessionEnd`, `PreToolUse`, `PostToolUse` and `PostToolUseFailure`: `TurnStart` carries no prompt, and `TurnEnd` sends the turn's `last_assistant_message`, which Tracekit doesn't read yet, so prompts, live constraints and model text are not recorded. Crush runs no hooks inside a sub-agent, so the hook denies `agent` and `agentic_fetch`, and Docker MCP Gateway's `code-mode` tools, whose scripts call MCP tools no hook sees. It also denies a built-in tool call whose input repeats an argument name or has one that isn't lower-case snake_case. An MCP tool, `mcp_<server>_<tool>`, is decided as `mcp__<server>_<tool>`: the shipped packs' MCP rules read that joined name, but the server and tool aren't split apart, so a rule of your own written for `mcp__server__tool` doesn't match it, and a server whose name holds a rule's word after a `_` or `-` (`files_delete`) trips that rule on each of its tools, in every harness. `download` is decided as a write of the file it creates, so the fetch rules (`TK-F002`, `TK-F012`) don't see its URL. A relative path in a `bash` command is decided as typed, not as a path in the folder the call names for itself (`working_dir`). A hook that outlives its timeout lets the call run. | Crush's payload and event names are Claude Code's, but `TurnStart` has no prompt field; a sub-agent's tools aren't wrapped with hooks (`wrapToolsWithHooks`); Crush decodes a built-in tool's input into a Go struct, matching names ignoring case, so it may run other arguments than Tracekit reads; its MCP names join server and tool with `_`, which either name may contain; a call is decided as one tool; the policy reads a relative path as typed, by its certain suffix, and its path rules read no working directory; a timed-out hook is killed and treated as a non-blocking error. | Put what the agent must not do in the policy, not only in the prompt; put `permissions deny agent agentic_fetch` in `crushrc` (in `crush.json`: `"options": {"disabled_tools": ["agent", "agentic_fetch"]}`); write MCP rules on the joined name (`mcp__github_*`, or `mcp__.*[_-]<tool>` as the shipped rules do), name MCP servers without `_` (the server pack reads a `refund_*` tool only after one), or route MCP through the [gateway](mcp-gateway.md); write path rules that also work on a relative path's suffix; in a real Crush session check that a denied call doesn't run and that its calls appear in `tracekit last`. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| **(experimental)** Cline: a cancel stops Cline's whole task, not only the call, so a denied or held call ends the task, and the calls of its batch Tracekit had already allowed never run: they get no `PostToolUse`, and on the extension, whose calls are paired by tool and arguments, the result of an identical call made later can be paired with one of them. A held call runs once it is approved (`tracekit approvals approve`) and the task resumed. A run is one task: a cancelled task's run stays open, so the resumed task goes on in it, until the signer's idle timeout when the task isn't resumed (`tracekit last --open` reads it before then). Cline runs a call whose hook fails, prints nothing or doesn't answer in time (the CLI 120 s, the VS Code extension 30 s): each hook file Tracekit writes answers a cancel when the hook prints nothing, and a held call's hook waits at most 20 s for its answer (the v2 hook's `TRACEKIT_APPROVAL_WAIT`, the v1 hook's `approval_timeout_s`), then cancels it, but a hook file that can't start at all lets the call run: with no `sh`, or on Windows in the CLI under an execution policy that runs no local script (`Restricted`, the default on Windows' client editions, or `AllSigned`), since the CLI runs a `.ps1` hook with `powershell -File` and no `-ExecutionPolicy Bypass` (the extension passes it). The extension's payload carries no call id (its calls are paired by tool and arguments) and no result (4.1.26 sends `"[object Object]"` for each of its entries), so the output of its calls isn't recorded. The extension runs hooks only while Hooks is on (it is by default), and reads them in the Documents folder the system reports, which may not be the CLI's `~/Documents`. Tracekit's hooks in two of the folders Cline reads run twice. An agent's edit of Cline's hooks or settings (`~/.cline`, `.cline/hooks`, `.clinerules/hooks`, `Documents/Cline/Hooks`, or the whole `.clinerules` or `Cline` folder deleted) is held (`TK-F009`, `TK-F015`). Not checked live on Windows or macOS. | Cline turns a hook's cancel into a stop of the task and a failed hook into no answer; the extension's hook adapter sends no id and turns the result into a string. | Approve a held call, then resume the task. Keep Tracekit's hooks in one folder (`tracekit doctor` warns about a second copy, and on Windows about an execution policy the CLI can't run them under: `Set-ExecutionPolicy -Scope CurrentUser RemoteSigned`). Where `init` says the extension reads another folder, install with `--project`. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| **(experimental)** CodeBuddy Code support has not been checked in a live CodeBuddy session: its hook format, payloads and tool names come from CodeBuddy Code 2.183.0's bundled code (`@tencent-ai/codebuddy-code` on npm) and the hooks Tencent's LoopForge writes for it, and the payloads in `tests/data/harness/codebuddy.json` are derived from that code, not recorded. Its transcripts (`~/.codebuddy/projects/<folder>/<session>.jsonl`) aren't read: no model text or reasoning is recorded, and `tracekit audit` has no reader for them. | A session needs a Tencent CodeBuddy account. The transcripts hold items in the OpenAI Agents SDK's shape (`function_call` with its `arguments` as a JSON string), whose time fields no recorded session has shown yet. | Check with `tracekit view --dev` that CodeBuddy's calls are recorded; replay its calls with `tracekit audit --calls FILE --agent codebuddy`; a recorded session ([#757](https://github.com/Cygnux-Labs/Tracekit/issues/757)) replaces the derived payloads and lets audit read its transcripts. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| A CodeBuddy Code call of a deferred tool (`DeferExecuteTool` runs `NotebookEdit`, `Monitor`, MCP tools and others, by default) reaches Tracekit's hook twice under one call id, and each is decided as the deferred call, so the run holds two decision records for it. The one the call completes is its decision; the signer counts the other as superseded, so neither is a gap. | CodeBuddy's bundled code runs the `PreToolUse` hooks of the wrapper and of the tool it runs (or of that tool twice, when its permission check ran them first). The hook can't tell a repeated `PreToolUse` from a new one without trusting a file the agent can write, and the signer writes one record per decide: answering a repeated decide of an open, allowed decision with the same arguments from that decision would need the signer to do it. | Read the two decisions of one call id as one call: the one its `tool.result` names is the call's. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| A CodeBuddy Code call of a deferred tool that the policy holds can't run, even once a person approves it: its first `PreToolUse` (the wrapper's, or the deferred tool's in CodeBuddy's own permission check) consumes the approval, and the deferred tool's next `PreToolUse`, under the same call id, finds it used and is blocked (`NOT APPROVED`). That block completes the call as an error, with the block's first line, under the deferred tool's decision, which closes the first decision too: the run has one error result for the call and no gap. | One approval lets one call run once (`approval_consume`), and the hook can't tell a repeated `PreToolUse` of the same call from a second run of it without trusting a file the agent can write; the signer would have to answer the repeat from the first consume. | Turn CodeBuddy's deferred tool loading off (`"deferToolLoading": false` in its settings, or `CODEBUDDY_DEFER_TOOL_LOADING=0`), so the model calls those tools directly and each call is decided once; or have the person run the held action. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| A CodeBuddy Code call whose tool returned an error, rather than throwing one, is recorded `ok`, with the error's text as its result: a missing or unknown tool name, parameters the tool rejects, an MCP server that isn't connected, a deferred tool that failed inside `DeferExecuteTool`. A call Tracekit's hook blocked is recorded as an error by the block itself. | CodeBuddy sends `PostToolUseFailure` only for a tool that threw; a returned error reaches `PostToolUse` as the result's content alone (`ToolManager.extractRawResponse`), a string with nothing in it that says the call failed. | Read an `ok` CodeBuddy Code result next to CodeBuddy's transcript, which shows the error. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| On the v1 hooks, a CodeBuddy Code deferred call is recorded twice: a `tool.call` and a `tool.result` for the wrapper's hook and again for the deferred tool's, under one call id. | The v1 pipeline records each hook it is sent, and CodeBuddy sends both. | Read the two records of one call id as one call, or use the v2 hooks (`tracekit init --dev --v2 --agent codebuddy`). | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| A CodeBuddy Code PreToolUse hook that runs after Tracekit's and answers `permissionDecision: "allow"` lets a call run that Tracekit denied: CodeBuddy merges its hooks' answers and the last `permissionDecision` stands. The user's settings' hooks run first, then a project's `.codebuddy/settings.json` and `settings.local.json`, plugins', skills' and an SDK host's. `tracekit doctor` (`D-HOOKS-ORDER`) names a settings file with such a hook, in doctor's working directory too; it doesn't read plugins', skills' or an SDK host's hooks. | CodeBuddy's hook results are merged last-wins (`aggregateSettledResults`), whatever earlier hooks answered. | Have no hook that runs after Tracekit's answer `allow` (LoopForge's `approve-safe-commands.py` does, for commands it trusts), and run `tracekit doctor` from the project. | [doctor](doctor.md#v2-checks) |
| CodeBuddy Code runs no hook while `disableAllHooks` or `allowManagedHooksOnly` is set in any of its settings files or by policy, nor the hooks of a settings file a session leaves out of `--setting-sources` (LoopForge starts it with `none` and its own `--settings` file). With `CODEBUDDY_CONFIG_DIR` (or, for `--project`, `CODEBUDDY_PROJECT_CONFIG_DIR`) set when CodeBuddy starts but not when `tracekit init --agent codebuddy` ran (or the other way round), init writes another settings file than the one CodeBuddy reads, and so does a CodeBuddy build whose product configuration renames its folders (`dataLayout.configDir`), which init doesn't read. Then nothing gates or records the session's calls, with no gap. The shell rule (`TK-F015`) holds a write through either variable by its name, but a file tool's write to the folder one names is matched only when it is a `.codebuddy` folder. | CodeBuddy honours its own switches without telling the hook, and `tracekit doctor` doesn't read its settings layers. | Leave those settings unset, run init with the same variables CodeBuddy starts with, and check with `tracekit view --dev` that calls are recorded. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| CodeBuddy Code on native Windows is untested. It runs a hook under Git Bash where it finds one and under PowerShell otherwise, and nothing wraps Tracekit's command there: under PowerShell a quoted Python path (one with a space in it) can't start, and a hook that can't start isn't a block to CodeBuddy, so the call runs unchecked and unrecorded. | Windows has no POSIX shell to wrap the hook in, and CodeBuddy picks the shell itself. | Install Git for Windows, or use WSL, and check with `tracekit view --dev` that calls are recorded. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md) |
| The v1 hooks (`tracekit init --agent codebuddy` without `--v2`) record a CodeBuddy Code `LibraryUpload` under its own name but don't decide it as an upload: the v1 policy has no rule for that tool, so an upload of a secrets file is allowed there. | `TK-D022` and `TK-F024`, which decide it, are in the v2 packs; the v1 policy is kept as it was. | Use the v2 hooks (`tracekit init --dev --v2 --agent codebuddy`). | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#on-the-v2-signer) |
| A decision's `expires_at` (300 s after it) is signed, but the signer doesn't refuse a `complete` that comes later: a call can run long after it was decided. | Refusing it needs the executors and adapters that run a call to check the time first; a T1 agent decides when to run anyway. | The decision's and the result's record times show the gap (`tracekit view`). | [tool-call lifecycle](concepts/tool-call-lifecycle.md) |
| A call allowed or flagged after a deny of the same arguments in the same run is marked with a `decision_flip` gap, but the set of denied arguments lives in the signer's memory: after a signer restart, a flip against a deny from before it isn't marked. | The set isn't kept in the run's snapshot, so a restart doesn't replay every decision of every open run. | The run's records still hold both decisions; `tracekit view` lists every deny of a run. | [policy](concepts/policy.md) |

## Policy

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| The laptop default (`dev.yaml`) flags, and doesn't hold, a push of a named branch other than main and master (`git push -u origin feature`, `HEAD:feature`). Flagged means recorded and run: the commits leave the laptop, anyone who can read the remote can read them (everyone, for a public repository), CI runs on them, a preview deploy built from every branch goes out, and a merge without review is a person's or the remote's to stop (`TK-A033` holds `gh pr merge`). A repository whose default branch isn't main or master (`develop`, `trunk`) has its pushes to it flagged too, a tag pushed by its bare name (`git push origin v1.0`) reads as a branch, and a push through a Git alias (`git p`), `git subtree push`, a Sapling push without `--to`, or a Jujutsu `jj git push` of a named bookmark or of a revision (`-r @`), which may carry main's bookmark, isn't read; `TK-A056` holds a push to main or master (`git push`, `git send-pack`, `hub push`; `refs/heads/main` or `heads/main`), one whose branch the line can't tell (no refspec, `HEAD`, `@`, `origin/HEAD`, an expansion), a tag push and `--all` or `--mirror` (a force push is `TK-D003`'s deny, a delete `TK-A002`'s ask). | Holding every push would hold an agent's ordinary work: 216 branch pushes in one developer's measured week, 31 a day. | Protect the default branch on the remote and require review there; add an `ask` rule of your own for a deploy branch with another name; let CI that deploys run only on the protected branch. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A056` holds a publish or deploy only for the commands of `TK-F004`'s list (`npm`, `pnpm`, `yarn` or `bun publish`, `uv`, `poetry` or `cargo publish`, `gem push`, `twine upload` and any runner's `-m twine upload`, `docker` or `podman push`, `helm install`, `upgrade` or `push`, `terraform` or `tofu apply`, `pulumi up`, `kubectl apply` or `delete`), a `gh` or `hub api` call on main's or master's ref, any merge through `repos/<o>/<r>/merges` (into any branch: the base isn't read, as reading it in either order took the fallback engine seconds), a file put or deleted through the contents API (`-X PUT repos/<o>/<r>/contents/…`, unless a `branch` field names another branch), each read percent-decoded as GitHub reads it (a GET of main's ref is held too: the method isn't read there), a GraphQL `updateRef`, `updateRefs`, `mergeBranch` or `createCommitOnBranch` on any ref (as `TK-A002` reads `deleteRef`), `sl push --to main`, `git town ship` or `git-town ship`, and `jj git push` with no arguments, `--all`, `--tracked`, a main or master bookmark (`-bmain`, `exact:main`) or a `glob:`, a fork's `merge-upstream` or `gh repo sync <owner/repo>` into main or master (and a `gh repo sync` a runner may give a repository). The default branch is read as main or master: a push to a repository whose default branch is another (`develop`, `trunk`) is a named branch's, flagged, not held. A body read from a file or stdin is read only as far as the rows here say: a contents write with `--input` or `-F branch=@file` is held as one whose branch the line can't tell, and a GraphQL mutation or a REST body in a file (`--input mutation.json`, `curl -d @body.json`) isn't read. Another deploy CLI (`vercel`, `fly deploy`, `gh release create`) is recorded, not held, and so is a contents write or a merge through `curl` or another API client, a change of the default branch or a rename of main (`gh repo edit --default-branch`, `default_branch=`, `branches/main/rename`), `git http-push`, and `sl push --to=refs/heads/main`; an API write to another branch's ref (`refs/heads/mainline`), `sl push --to feature` and `git town sync` aren't flagged. It is in `dev.yaml`, not the coding pack: the server pack and `strict.yaml` don't have it (`strict.yaml` holds every push, publish and apply as `TK-F004`). | The rule names the verbs `TK-F004` names; a server agent's deploy is often its job. | Add an `ask` rule of your own for the deploy commands your agents use. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| The laptop default is composed of the server pack's parts (#604), with four differences. The server pack's shadow detection isn't in it. A tool no pack maps is flagged, not held, unless it is given a command line or a SQL statement to run (`TK-A062`, held, as the server pack holds every unmapped tool), so an app's own tool given data (an `s3_put` with a bucket, a key and a body) runs with only the rules of no class (`TK-S001`, `TK-S002`, `TK-S004`) reading its whole call: 2 harmful calls of E21's held-out and blind pools that the server pack holds as unknown tools run here. And a coding agent's messages to its own agents (Claude Code's `Agent`, `Task`, `SendMessage`, `SubagentHandback` and `TodoWrite`, and the other harnesses' tools the hooks map to `Task` or `TodoWrite`) are class `harness`, whose `target` is only the URLs and network addresses they name: a credentials file's name in a prompt (`TK-S004`) or a secret-looking path (`TK-F005`) isn't held or flagged there, while a secret's value (`TK-S002`, also right after an escaped newline or tab) or the metadata service's address (`TK-S001`, as a URL or a bare IPv4 address in any form, punctuation around it too) is denied; a bare IPv6 address or host name with no scheme (`[fd00:ec2::254]/latest/`, `metadata.google.internal/…`) isn't read as one. The rules of no class read a harness call's whole text, so `TK-M003` (a mass email), `TK-C001` (an access change, when an argument is the command itself: a `Task` whose `command` is `aws iam create-user …` is denied) and `TK-F012` (a URL, flagged as egress) act on what a prompt says, and a harness call over 64 KiB is held unread (`TK-OVERSIZE`). A file a subagent then reads is decided as its own call; every `SendMessage` is flagged (`TK-F018`) as a message that can leave the machine, and a remote `Agent` or `Task` is held (`TK-F019`): none of its calls are decided here. And a Write, Edit or MCP call over 64 KiB, which `TK-S002` can't read, waits for a person (`oversize: ask`: `TK-OVERSIZE` as an ask) where a server denies it: 4 Writes in one developer's measured week. | A laptop runs tools and MCP servers no list names, and other coding agents' built-in tools. A prompt that names a credentials file doesn't open it: 24 of the server rules' stops in one developer's week were such names in messages between Claude Code's agents. `TK-S002` can't read past 64 KiB, and a cut would let a key hide past it. | Map your app's tools to a class (`tools:` in your policy) so the class rules read them, or run the server pack, which holds every unmapped tool; write a large file in parts. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped), [evaluation](evaluation.md#e21-policy-robustness) |
| `TK-A062` reads what an unmapped tool is given to run (`engine._runs`). A command argument (`command`, `commands`, `cmd`, `cmds`, `script` or `shell_command`, in any case, at any depth) is a command line unless it is one word of plain characters (letters, digits, `_ . : / -`) other than `reboot`, `shutdown`, `halt`, `poweroff` or `killall5`; a list, an object, a blank, a quote, a shell metacharacter or an expansion makes one. Each string under a `sql`, `query` or `statement` argument (in any case, at any depth) is read as every SQL dialect lexes it (`classes.sql_read`: comments and literals blanked, any blank a space; before a literal or comment that doesn't close, the statements a `;` ends) and split into statements; one is held when it writes or changes the schema or access (`INSERT`, `REPLACE` or `MERGE INTO`, `UPDATE x [alias] SET`, `DELETE FROM`, `DELETE x FROM`, `DELETE x`, `DROP` of an object, `TRUNCATE x`, `ALTER` of an object, `CREATE USER` or `ROLE`, `GRANT` or `REVOKE` on or to something, `COPY` to or from a program or a file, a CTE, `DO`), or is a `SELECT`, `CREATE`, `EXEC` or `CALL` ended by a `;`. A search in English shaped like one of these (`delete file from repo`, `truncate text`, `create user in linux`) is held too, and so is a statement with a no-break space for a blank, which some databases reject. Flagged, not held: a statement under another argument name (`input`, `text`, `q`, `stmt`, `sql_query`) or behind a leading `(` or `EXPLAIN`, an unterminated `SELECT` (`SELECT * FROM users`, `SELECT pg_read_file(…)`), an unterminated `EXEC` or `CALL`, and Claude Code's `PowerShell` tool, unmapped on purpose (the signer reads its command as bash too). A Claude Code or Gemini CLI MCP tool (`mcp__…`) is the MCP rules' (`TK-S010`, `TK-S012`, `TK-A041`): a Gemini CLI or Crush tool whose name doesn't say it runs SQL (`mcp__mydb_run_query`) is flagged when given `DROP TABLE`, as on main; an MCP name in another case (`MCP:x/y`), which no pack maps, is held for any command argument, as `TK-S010` holds one. | The argument names and the text are all a rule reads of a tool no pack maps; holding every `query` or `command` would hold every search tool, skill and editor, and holding SQL for every MCP name would hold a code search for `delete from users`. | Map an app's own tools in your policy (`tools:`): `sql` for one that runs SQL, `shell` for one that runs a command, `app` for one that runs neither. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-the-laptop-default-holds) |
| Some spellings of short-list actions aren't read on any pack: a `kubectl` alias (`k delete namespace production`), a credentials file reached through a `cd` (`cd ~/.aws && cat credentials`) or a pattern (`cat ~/.aws/cred*`), and PowerShell's `type`/`Get-Content` of `$env:USERPROFILE\.aws\credentials` on the laptop default (held on a server as an unknown tool). `TK-A056` doesn't hold a `kubectl` dry run as kubectl reads one (its last `--dry-run` bare, `=client` or `=server`, before any bare `--`, with no `--raw` and no unresolved expansion among its words, and no `--dry-run` word right after an option that may take it as its value: one written without `=` that isn't among kubectl's boolean flags, or a short option that ends its word, as `-f` or `-Rf`, not `-nprod`); `TK-C010` holds a delete of the kinds it reads whatever its dry-run options say. | An alias, a later directory change and a pattern are resolved only when the command runs; the rules read the line. | Hold the program by its alias in a policy of your own, and keep credentials files out of the agent's reach (the sandbox, system mode). | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-the-laptop-default-holds) |
| On the dev signer, an app's own tool named `Agent`, `Task`, `SendMessage`, `SubagentHandback` or `TodoWrite` (an SDK agent's, not Claude Code's) is read as a coding agent's message to its own agents (class `harness`): the file names in its arguments aren't read by `TK-S004` or `TK-F005`, a `SendMessage` is flagged as one, and an `Agent` or `Task` whose arguments hold `"isolation":"remote"` is held. | The policy maps tools by name; the dev signer serves coding agents' hooks and SDK apps alike. | Name your app's tools otherwise, or map them in a policy of your own (`tools:`), which wins over the pack's. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A056` reads a remote's push or mirror setting only when the push line gives it (`git -c remote.origin.push=HEAD:main push origin feature` is held). One set earlier with `git config remote.origin.push …` (`TK-A013` doesn't hold that), in the repository's config or in a global one, sends a later `git push origin feature` where it says, to main too, and the push stays flagged. | The push line is all a rule reads; the config file isn't read when a call is decided. | Protect the default branch on the remote; review a change to `remote.*.push` in a repository's config. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| A single message, email or post sent outside isn't held: `gh issue create`, `gh pr comment`, one email or a chat MCP tool's message is recorded (an MCP call flagged, `TK-F006`); a secret in it is denied (`TK-S002`), and so are mass email and a whole-channel notice (`TK-M003`, `TK-M004`, held). | No rule reads what a single message says. | Add an `ask` rule of your own for the messaging tools your agents have. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| An agent's edit of its own harness's settings, hooks, MCP servers, skills or slash commands (`.claude/settings.json`, `~/.claude.json`, `.claude/skills/` and `.claude/commands/`, whose `!` lines run commands, `~/.gemini/settings.json`, `.codex/`, `~/.cursor/hooks.json` and `mcp.json`, OpenCode's `.opencode/`, `opencode.json` and, from the shell, `~/.config/opencode` or `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/opencode` (the shell rule matches the variable's name; a file tool gets the expanded path, so its write is denied only at `~/.config/opencode`, `TK-D009`), Qwen Code's `.qwen/` and `~/.qwen/settings.json`, CodeBuddy Code's `.codebuddy/` and `~/.codebuddy/` (and, from the shell, `$CODEBUDDY_CONFIG_DIR` by name), Factory Droid's `.factory/`, gptme's `~/.config/gptme/`, `gptme.toml` and `gptme.local.toml`, OpenHands' `.openhands/` and `~/.openhands`, Copilot CLI's `.copilot/`, `/etc/github-copilot/` (its `policy.d/*.json` hooks) and a repository's `.github/hooks/` and `.github/copilot/settings*.json` (where `disableAllHooks` turns every hook off; a `COPILOT_HOME` moved elsewhere isn't matched), Devin's `.devin/`, `~/.config/devin` and, on Windows, `AppData\Roaming\devin` (the folder `%APPDATA%` names: a path written through the variable isn't matched), Antigravity CLI's `~/.gemini/config/hooks.json` and `.agents/hooks.json`, goose's `.agents/plugins/` and `~/.config/goose`, Crush's `crush.json`, `.crush.json`, `crushrc`, `.crushrc` and `~/.config/crush`, PowerShell's profiles (a `*profile.ps1` in `Documents/WindowsPowerShell/` or `Documents/PowerShell/`, OneDrive's Documents too under its localized name (`OneDrive - Contoso/Dokumente/`), pwsh's `~/.config/powershell/` on Linux and macOS, or the `$PSHOME` of Windows PowerShell (`System32/WindowsPowerShell/v1.0/`) or PowerShell 7 (`PowerShell/7/`, `PowerShell/7-preview/`), and `$PROFILE` or `$global:PROFILE` by name from the shell), which run before a hook PowerShell starts without `-NoProfile`, by a file-editing tool (Write, Edit, MultiEdit or NotebookEdit) or a recognized shell write) is held (`TK-F009`, `TK-F015`, since #604);
