one the rules don't read (below) runs, and once it turns the sandbox off, `TK-A003`, `TK-A004` and `TK-A014` have nothing to hold: later commands run outside it with no escape-hatch argument. The file rule reads the normalized path, and the shell rule reads `WRITE_TARGET:` and `DELETE_TARGET:` effects (redirects, `tee`, `cp`, `mv`, `sed -i`, `rm`, `find -delete`, `git rm`, awk's `print > "file"`, …). Both also match casefolded paths. Reads and content that merely names these paths do not match. Not flagged: Cursor's rules (`.cursor/rules/`), Windsurf's and VS Code's settings, a Claude Code config folder moved elsewhere (`CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, `~/.config/claude-code`) and instruction files agents edit in ordinary work (`AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md`); `TK-F016`/`TK-F017` read an `ANTHROPIC_…BASE_URL` name only when it is written in one piece (not split by a line continuation), and not a proxy variable (`HTTPS_PROXY`). A file inside one of Claude Code's or CodeBuddy Code's worktrees (`.claude/worktrees/<name>/…`, `.codebuddy/worktrees/<name>/…`) is read as it is in the worktree: an ordinary file there isn't flagged, the worktree's own `.claude/`, `.codebuddy/`, `.codex/`, `.mcp.json` and the rest are, and so is a worktree named as a dot folder, the worktree itself, and a shell target with a `..`, or an expansion or glob that can make one (`$D`, `{..,a}`, `.*`), among its first 32 parts after the worktree, or more parts than that. A link made in a worktree that points out of it (`ln -s ../.. .claude/worktrees/x/up`, then a write to `up/settings.json`) isn't followed, as a link made anywhere else isn't. `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` (where the agent's model calls go) is flagged wherever a call names it, in any case (Windows reads a variable's name so): in a Write, Edit, MultiEdit or NotebookEdit call's arguments (`TK-F016`) and on a shell line from any shell tool, an assignment prefix (`ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL=x claude`) and the text a heredoc or `echo` writes included (`TK-F017`). A mention is flagged as well as a setting, so a grep, a note or a comment that names the variable is flagged too: noisy by design, and only a flag. Under `QWEN_HOME`, the shell rule matches only the variable's name (`$QWEN_HOME/settings.json`); a file tool's write to the directory it names is not matched. A write through any other variable, a script or another program (`curl -o`, `wget -O`, `touch`, `rsync`, `git restore`, an editor, an interpreter one-liner) is not matched, nor is a PowerShell cmdlet that writes or deletes a profile (`Add-Content`, `Set-Content`, `Out-File`, `Remove-Item`) or a profile in a Documents folder redirected elsewhere (a path without `Documents` or `OneDrive`). | The rules cover every file under those directories (skills, commands) but Claude Code's memory notes (`.claude/projects/<project>/memory/*.md`, the agent's own notes, not settings) and the ordinary files of its and CodeBuddy Code's worktrees (a checkout of the project, not settings); agents edit those files in normal work. The rest are held: on one developer's week of Tracekit work, 19 calls in 7 days, 13 of them test fixtures written in scratch folders or a worktree's removal. | Approve a held settings change only when you meant it; keep the sandbox's settings where the agent can't write (managed settings, a read-only mount); add an `ask` rule of your own for the settings files your agents never need to touch. | [Claude Code's sandbox](integrations/claude-code-sandbox.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt), [Gemini CLI's sandbox](integrations/gemini-sandbox.md) |
| `TK-A003` holds a call only when it carries `dangerouslyDisableSandbox: true`. Commands matching `excludedCommands`, hooks and MCP servers run outside the sandbox without it. | Claude Code marks only the unsandboxed retry; the rest are outside the sandbox by configuration. | Keep `excludedCommands` short; the policy still decides each excluded command and MCP tool call. | [Claude Code's sandbox](integrations/claude-code-sandbox.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |
| `TK-A014` holds a call whose `allowed_domains` names a host; it doesn't see hosts opened by the sandbox's own settings (`network.allowedDomains`), and it can't tell which of the listed hosts a command then reached. **(1.5.1)** It reads only Bash, PowerShell and Monitor calls, the tools Claude Code documents as opening hosts this way (WebSearch's `allowed_domains` only narrows results): a new tool that opens hosts with `allowed_domains` isn't held until the pack names it. | The hook sees the call's arguments, not the sandbox's settings or its proxy's traffic. | Keep the hosts a project always needs in `network.allowedDomains` (a person edits the settings) and approve the rest one command at a time. | [Claude Code's sandbox](integrations/claude-code-sandbox.md#per-command-allowed-domains-tk-a014) |
| A tool name a framework rewrote (CrewAI's `bash` for `Bash`, `multi_edit` for `MultiEdit`) is also decided as the tool `tools:` lists with that spelling (case, accents, spaces and punctuation ignored), as each one when two listed names share it (the server pack's `bash` and `Bash`), and its results are untrusted when that tool's are; an MCP tool's bare name is read the same way. Nothing else reads it so: a rule whose `tool:` names a tool `tools:` doesn't list (`tool: RefundTool`, which CrewAI sends as `refund_tool`) doesn't take it, and constraints, the egress observer's host, detection's sum limits and a fail mode in an outage go by the class of the name as sent (`unknown`); an agent approver can't answer it, as the policy doesn't name it exactly. A name with no ASCII letter or digit, or a name over 64 characters (CrewAI cuts it and adds a hash) is decided only as sent. Each name a call is decided as gets the fallback regex engine's time budget of its own, so on a crafted 64 KiB argument a renamed tool's decision can take a second per name (re2 is unaffected). | A `tool:` pattern can't be spelled backwards; deciding both names and keeping the stricter decision never decides a call less strictly than before. | List each tool under its declared name in `tools:` (`RefundTool: app`), or write a rule's `tool:` as the framework sends it; keep the `default` fail mode closed, as it ships. | [policy](policy-v2.md#classes-and-fields), [CrewAI](integrations/crewai.md) |
| The `decide` RPC answers `allow` for a flagged call; only the signed `policy.decision` says `flag`. | `decision` tells the client what to do, and clients since RPC 15 know only `allow`, `deny` and `ask`; the signer can't tell a client's version. | Read a flag from the record, or from the flag rule's id in the response's `rule_ids`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#decisions) |
| Policy rules are tripwires that make attempts visible, not the protection itself. | They match strings; a rewording a rule doesn't anticipate isn't matched. The protection is the signer's isolation, its storage and the witnesses. | Rely on isolation and witnesses; read E3's misses. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped), [evaluation](evaluation.md#e3-policy-gate) |
| Shell variables are not expanded and encodings are not decoded: a variable's value, or a value that splits into several words, is unknown. A command is also read as bash runs it when its expansions (`$X`, `$(cmd)`) are empty: a word made only of them is gone, one inside a word joins the rest (`rm $X-rf ~/$X` is `rm -rf ~/`), and a default is its word, with that word's own expansions empty (`${Z:--rf}` is `-rf`, `${X:-$Y}` nothing); an alternate's word and a substitution's replacement are read as one more value (`${X:+-rf}` and `${X/*/-rf}` may be `-rf`), and without the colon a default may also be empty (`${E-d}`). A word is read in each way its expansions combine, each one empty, as written or its word, the word's own expansions alike (`${HOME:+approve}${X:+d}` may be `approve`, `d`, `approved` or nothing), an expansion written twice taking one value; a command, in each way the words that read more than that combine, the others as written and empty. Past 256 ways (a word's, or a command's words' together) the word or command is unread and asks (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`, `readings`), never read fewer ways. A `${F=w}` or `${F:=w}` of a literal `w` gives F that word too, for the rest of the line (`: ${F=--force}; git push $F` is `git push --force`). So every way a word's unset and empty variables join or drop its characters is read; across words that read no more than all as written or all empty, only those uniform ways; and an unknown value is read as written, never split into words (`git push $OPTS`), while a value the parser reads is split at its blanks where unquoted, as bash splits it: a literal the line gives (`F="-x -q"; pytest $F` is `pytest -x -q`; one with a blank also where the line may not have given it yet, after `&&` or in a loop), a default's, alternate's or replacement's word (`env =${X:- }cmd` runs cmd). A word of nine or more unknown values (`echo "$(a) $(b) … $(i)"`) asks for that reason. `$PWD`, a variable the line gives a literal first (`D=dist; rm -rf "$D"/*`, `export D=dist`) and a `for` loop's over literal words (in its body) are never read as empty, unless something before may have emptied them (an assignment after `&&` or `\|\|`, in a pipeline, subshell, `if` or loop, or in a `{ }` group after it ends; `X+=y` of an X the line doesn't know; `read`, `set -k`, `declare`, `printf -v` (also `-vNAME`), `compgen`, `eval`, `trap`; an unreadable command name); after `cd` or `pushd` to a literal `$PWD` is that literal (`cd /`), after any other target unknown; `X=`, `unset X`, a loop over `""`, `${X:+}`, `${X:0:0}` and `${X%%*}` are empty for certain, so a deny they make stays one; a loop's body and what follows the loop know only the loop's own variable, as the body's later commands may change any other for the next pass. Those literals are also read as the variables' values (`F=~/.x; cat "$F"` is `cat ~/.x`), and so is a default, assignment or check of a variable whose literals aren't empty (`D=/; rm -rf "${D:?}"/` is `rm -rf //`), but not a value any other operator makes of them (`D=xy; echo ${D#x}` isn't read as `y`: it is unknown, as an unknown variable's); one whose literals include an empty one (a loop over `/` and `''`) is read as its default alone. After a command that may set any variable, a default or check is the variable, unknown: `${D:?}` is held as `rm -rf "$D"/` is, while `${D:-x}` is also read as its word, so `D=/; read X; rm -rf ${D:-x}/` is only flagged. A deny that only an empty reading makes (`rm -rf "$VENV"/`, `find "$DIR/" … -delete`) takes `on_parse_error`'s section, so it asks by default, with the rule's id; `$OLDPWD` is unset in a new shell and can be read as empty. | Static parsing can't know runtime values; whether `$VENV` is set is a question for a person, not a certain deny. | A command name built from an expansion asks (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`); keep those held. Approve a held call whose variable you know is set, or name the path in full or give the variable its value on the line. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| A pathname pattern is read as the names its bracket expressions can make, up to 16 (`>> ~/.ssh/authorized_key[s]` may write `authorized_keys`), as a second reading (a deny only it makes asks); a `*` or `?`, a negated set (`[!x]`), a class (`[[:alpha:]]`) or more names than that is read as written, and a `for` loop over a pattern (`for f in ~/.ssh/id_rs[a]`) doesn't give its variable a value. Of a command's readings by a variable's literals or a pattern's names, a rule on the line or code (`TK-D011`) reads the first 4, a rule on its words all; a line whose readings pass the parse budget (a loop over some 2,000 words) is held (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`). | A pattern names the files that exist when the command runs, which a policy decided before it runs can't list; a bracket names few enough to read each. | Name the file in full; keep reads of secret files and writes to login scripts held by their own rules, which read the names as written. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| `$'\x{…}'` whose braces hold a character that isn't a hex digit is read as the hex digits before it, then the rest as written (`$'\x{2fq}'` is `/q}`), as bash 5's `lib/sh/strtrans.c` was recalled; it wasn't checked against bash's source. | bash's source could not be fetched when this was written. | Write the escape plainly (`/`); a rule that must see the character reads a held line's text too. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| In a `$(( ))` whose text has `$(` or a backtick, the parser reads each of those command substitutions, which get the rules as any command does (`echo $(( $(nproc) * 2 ))` is read as `nproc`). It asks (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`) instead when that text also has a quote (`'`, `"` or `$'`), a newline, a `[` (an array index), parentheses that don't pair up, or a backtick or backslash together with a parenthesis. | bash reads quotes, brackets and parentheses inside `$(( ))` its own way, so the parser can't be sure where such text starts and ends. | Approve the held call, or compute the value in a command of its own first (`m=$(sysctl -n hw.memsize); echo $(( m / 1073741824 ))`). | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| A parameterized arithmetic subscript on a line that also has a command substitution or backtick is held (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`); a direct substitution in an arithmetic subscript is read when its syntax is supported. | Bash may evaluate an expanded array index again, and the resulting command text may be unavailable to the parser. | Compute the value in a command of its own first, or approve the held call. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| A command line the parser can't read asks (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`) and is still decided on the line before the word it stopped in, parsed, and on its text split at `;`, `&`, `\|` and newlines into blank-separated words, so a deny there stands (`bash -c 'sudo id' ${X:-'$'}`). A quoted mention of a denied command in such a line is denied too (`echo 'a; sudo id' ${X:-'$'}`), and a deny that needs the parser after the word it stopped in (code in a later `bash -c '…'`) asks instead. A brace bash 4 expands its own way (`{1..9..2}`), one beside a substitution, or one whose words would add more than 16 KiB to the line or take it past 64 KiB (`echo {1..5000}`), is kept as written and holds only its command. A backslash-newline between `$` and `(` or `{`, and bash 5.3's `${ cmd; }`, make the whole line unreadable (one before a quote is read: `$\⏎'x'` is `x`). In the text split, a `#` that starts a word comments out the rest of its line, quotes in it too. A line whose reading spends the decision's work budget (four times a 64 KiB line's characters, each command it reads counting 15 more: about 13,000 one-word commands) asks the same way (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`, budget); the budget is the whole decision's, so the line's other readings, what is read of a line the parser can't read and each argument read as a command (128 more each) spend it too, and once it is spent nothing more is read. | The parser can't tell quoted text from a command in a line it can't read; the words of a larger expansion would make a subject past 64 KiB, which isn't read; the signer reads a line holding Python's interpreter lock, so a bound keeps one call from stalling every agent. | Approve the held call, or write the line in the shell the parser reads (`seq 5000` for a long sequence), or split a long line into several calls. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| A shell call's `commands` list with several entries is read twice, joined (as LangChain's ShellTool runs it, in one shell) and each entry on its own (as OpenAI's shell tool and Cline's `run_commands` run them), from one parse budget: a very long list of tiny entries (around 13,000) spends it and asks (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`) where one reading would have read it whole. | A deny either reading finds has to stand, and the budget keeps a decision's time bounded. | Split a long list into several calls. | [policy v2](policy-v2.md) |
| Held (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`) rather than read: bash's old `$[ ]` arithmetic; `env -S` with a backslash, `$` or `#`; `hash -p`; setting `BASH_CMDS` or `BASH_ALIASES`; a function given through the environment (`env BASH_FUNC_x%%='() { …; }'`, any `env` name holding `%%` or `()`); `fc`, and `history -s`, `-r` or `-n`, which put commands in the history it runs; `function` after an assignment or a redirection (`>f function x`), where bash runs a program of that name; an arithmetic assignment to a name expansions build (`$(($Y$W=1))` may set IFS); code sh, dash or ksh reads, or a line that may turn on POSIX mode, that uses an operator only bash has (`&>`, `\|&`, `>&file`, `[[`, `$'…'`, `<<<`), as their grammars read it otherwise; a `shopt` that changes how bash parses (`interactive_comments`, `extglob`, `expand_aliases`, `posix`) on a line with code bash reads again; a prompt (`PS0` to `PS2`, `PS4`), `PROMPT_COMMAND` or `BASH_ENV` set to a value the line can't read, a prompt with a `\` escape or a character past ASCII, or a file from a stream; `ENV` so set, which only an interactive shell reads, on a line that runs one with `-i` (`sh -ic`, `bash -i`; not one a later call runs in a shell that persists, or one a program such as `tmux` starts); `${X@P}` of a variable the line doesn't give; a subscript with an expansion given to `read`, `unset`, `declare`, `test -v` and the like; an alias whose name the line builds, or whose text has a heredoc, a comment or a trailing `\`; a script the line writes from a program's output (`sed … > s.sh; sh s.sh`, as `sed … \| sh` is) and then sources or runs; a trap's action the parser can't read, or a lone word with an expansion given to `trap`; a command name with arithmetic in it (`python$((3))`); a subscript with a blank where a command starts (`a[ x ]=1 cmd`); an empty process substitution (`<()`); a heredoc delimiter with an expansion; a command git runs given in text the parser can't read (`git -c core.pager="$X"`, `--config-env=core.pager=X`, `GIT_EDITOR="$E"`); from an expansion that assigns in any other spelling (`${!G=}`, `${F[0]=}`, `${F::=}`, a `${F=…}` whose word isn't literal, or one whose command reads F, or a variable named indirectly, in two copies of one expansion), an arithmetic assignment (`$((F=1))`, in an offset, a subscript, `let`, `[[ … -eq … ]]`), a name list (`${!X@}`) or a nameref or integer variable (`declare -n`, `-i`) on, that command and every later one on the line, as what they expand to can't be followed (cause `assigns`); on a line that gives IFS a value, an unquoted expansion that may hold a character of it, a blank or a digit, and one of a variable the line gives a literal with a blank only after it (a loop may run that first), as bash splits them where the parser doesn't (cause `split`); a `$IFS`, quoted or not, after the line may have changed IFS (an `IFS=` given to `eval`, `.` or a function too; unquoted, also `unset IFS`, `read` or `let`), or spelled otherwise (`${IFS:-}`, `${IFS:0:1}`, a quoted `${!x}`); an alias the line defines and doesn't use (a shell that persists runs it on a later call), one ending in a blank before another alias, or named as a reserved word; a code variable set with `+=`, by a loop, or through a nameref with no target; a variable `read`, `declare`, `local`, `typeset` or `env` gives under a name the line builds from text it makes (`read "$N"`, `declare $(printf …)`, `env "$(printf …)=…" cmd`); a name `export` or `readonly` gives from text the parser can't read (`export $(cat .env \| xargs)`) only on a line that also runs a shell or a script by its path, turns on xtrace or uses arithmetic (neither builtin evaluates a subscript in the name, as `declare`, `local` and `typeset` do, but the name may be `BASH_ENV`, `PS4` or one arithmetic reads); in code bash parses again (`eval`, `sh -c`'s string, a trap's action, a heredoc or herestring into a shell, `compgen -C` or `-W`) or in a script the line writes and runs, any expansion but a number (`$#`, `$((…))`, the digits a line of one pipeline prints) and a variable or parameter the parser knows one literal of there, expanded plainly (`$X`, `${X}`): one the line gives on its straight line, not after `&&` or `\|\|`, in a branch or loop that may skip or repeat it, through `declare`, `local`, an array, a nameref, a copy (`Y=$X`) or a trap, nor a loop's several words, nor after `${X:=…}`, `${X=…}`, `{X}<`, arithmetic or another command gives it a value, anywhere on the line (a heredoc's body, a prompt's `@P` or `PS4`, `eval`'s text); never one bash sets itself (`$_`, `REPLY`, `OPTARG`, `MAPFILE`, the `BASH_` and `COMP_` names); quoted, or a value bash neither splits nor globs, on a line that doesn't name IFS (`X='*'; eval echo $X` is held); so a substitution's output, a variable the environment gives (`$HOME` too), what `read`, `mapfile` or a loop over unknown words sets, an operator (`${X/a/b}`, `${X,,}`, `${@:2}`), and a positional parameter but the literal a call or script run gives it are held there (`bash -c "cd $HOME && make"` too: write the path, or run the commands without `-c`); such a script written by `printf` with a conversion other than `%s` or `%b`, or run under a name the line builds. A quoted `$IFS` is read as bash's space, tab and newline otherwise, so code made of it is read line by line. The aliases a line defines are read wherever a command has the name, whether or not bash expands them there. A script the line writes with text it gives (`echo`, `printf`, or a heredoc or herestring `cat` or `tee` copies, appends joined in order) and then sources or runs (`source`, `.`, a shell given the file or its `--rcfile` or `--init-file`, or its path with a shell's `#!`) is read as its code, and one `history -w` or `-a` writes holds text the line can't read; an interpreter's (`python3 s.py`, a `#!` naming one) as its inline code, for what it runs and writes, but code there the parser can't read isn't held; one written from another program's output (`sed … a.py > b.py; python3 b.py`), or by an earlier call, isn't read. | Each makes bash run code, or a program by another name, that the line holds only at run time. | Write the command plainly, or approve the held call. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| A command a tool runs from its arguments is held (ask) with the id of the deny rule it matches: an argument word with a blank, or its value after an option's or name's `=` (`--eval=…`, `--checkpoint-action=exec=…`, `ProxyCommand=…`), or an assignment's value (`MANPAGER=…`), without a leading `!` or `\|`, read as a command line and also without its first word (`hyperfine 'cmd'`, `make --eval='x: ; cmd'`, `vim -c '!cmd'`, `gdb -ex 'shell cmd'`, `osascript -e 'do shell script "cmd"'`). Data isn't read so: a commit or tag message, a log's `--grep`, `echo` and `printf` text, a search's words (`grep`, `rg`, `ag`, `git grep`), a GitHub title or body and a `jq` filter, nor a program's words the parser reads as code itself (a shell's, an interpreter's, `eval`'s, `git`'s). Runners that take the command as their own words are read exactly (`entr`, `watchexec`, `nix develop -c`, `bundle exec`, `pixi`, `rye`, `micromamba` and `mamba run`, `coverage run`), and so are the commands `ssh`, `scp` and `sftp`'s `-o` (`ProxyCommand`, `LocalCommand`) and `rsync -e` run. A command in one word with no blank (`man -P reboot`), or one a tool reads from a file, isn't read, nor is a text with no letter but in `\n`, `\t` or `\r` and no `$'…'` (`x '1 2'`, `psql -c '\n \n'`), which names no program. A process call in inline code reads every literal of its arguments as the argv (a list, a tuple or separate arguments; `exec` and `spawn`'s file before it; `sys.executable` as python), each decoded as its language does (escapes, side-by-side and triple-quoted literals, Perl's `q()`), and a value the code makes at run time (`chr(45) + "f"`, an f-string's `{…}`, a `%` or `.format()` placeholder) as an unknown variable, so a program name made so is held; a call's name inside a string, whose argument no expression makes, isn't read. A file the line writes, then a program reads commands from (a Makefile, a package.json's scripts, git's config or the file `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL` names, `setup.py`), is read as those commands, and one it can't read (an `.npmrc` that names a program, `pyproject.toml`) is held; one under a name the line builds isn't read. Keys `screen -X stuff` and `tmux send-keys` type are read as the line they make, and a git `ext::` URL as the command it runs. `env` with an assignment that expands a variable the line gave from expansions it doesn't know there (`X=${X::0}" cmd"; env =$X a`) is held, as bash may split it into the command (a quoted one, which doesn't split, is held too); one the environment or a substitution gives (`env HOME=$HOME/x`, `H=$(mktemp -d); env HOME=$H`, `S=$PWD; env HOME=$S/h`) isn't. | Holding every argument that isn't a literal held 29 calls of a real week (`git show f"{rev}:f"`); reading one as an unknown variable holds 4 (a program name from a variable, `[PY, "-m", …]`), and every `env` assignment with an expansion 102; a word with no blank is most often a file or a name. | Run the command directly rather than through a tool's argument, or approve the held call. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| Arithmetic text built at run time can name a command bash runs while evaluating an array subscript; policy can't read that command. A literal the line gives, on any of its lines, is read where the line may evaluate it (arithmetic, a subscript, `${!X}`, `read`, `declare` …). Text the line makes (by `printf`, `echo -e`, `awk`, an interpreter, a herestring, a file it writes such text into) and that bash then evaluates (`$((x))`, `let` or `declare -i` with it as a name or in a value, `${s:x}`, `${!x}`, `test -v`, `printf -v`), also through a loop over it or a positional parameter given it (`set -- "$(printf …)"`, a function's, a script's or `sh -c`'s arguments), is held (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`), as is a value that joins parameters the parser doesn't replace by a literal (`Z=$X$Y`, `declare Z=$X$Y`, `$(($1$2))`, a positional parameter given a `[` or `$`), or one an operator makes (`l=${s##*:}`). What only reads files or the system (`cat`, `grep`, `cut`, `head`, `wc`, `date +%s`, `mktemp` …) or prints only digits is trusted as running a file already on disk is, so a file an earlier call wrote can still hold such a command (`n=$(cat f); echo $((n))`), and so is a variable the environment gives; so is the stdin the call gives (`n=$(grep -c x $f)` with `$f` empty), but not one the line may have moved (`exec < f`, a pipe or redirection into a `{ }` group, a loop or code bash reads again), stdin however it is spelled (`//dev/stdin`, `/proc/thread-self/fd/0`), a descriptor a variable names (`<&$fd`), or, on a line that writes a file, a file named through a variable (`cat "$F"`). | The text is unavailable to the parser when the policy decision is made. | Keep destructive rights and secrets off the agent; use T2 or a sandbox for enforcement. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| `TK-D002` and `TK-A046` read parsed shell code: a bare download pipe into a shell or interpreter is denied, while inline code or a script path is held for review. A download piped, at any later stage of its pipeline, into any program not known to read only data (`jq`, `grep`, `tee`, `cat`, `less`, `head`, `tail`, `wc`, a hash, `sort`, `sed`, `awk`, `base64`, a decompressor, `tar -t` and the like) or into a shell or interpreter reading its code from stdin (`pypy3`, `osascript`, `tclsh`, `Rscript -`, `deno run -`, `php`, `fish`) is denied by `TK-D002` too: `curl … \| tar -x` and `curl … \| my-tool` are. A process substitution is read as the pipe it is (`python3 <(curl …)` as `curl … \| python3`, `source <(curl …)` as `curl … \| sh`, `curl … > >(sh)` and `curl -o >(python3 -) …` as piped into what runs there), and a download saved to a file (curl's `-o`, `-O`, `-J`, wget, a redirection or `tee`) that a command anywhere on the line runs (before the download too: a background job, a FIFO, a trap, a loop's next turn) by a name it saved or its URL's (any path, where the server picks the name: `-J`, `-K`), or a shell or interpreter given one of those as a word, a redirection or in its inline code, is denied; a link check to `/dev/null`, a download from this machine (`localhost`, `127.*`, `::1`: a health check), and a path run that names nothing the download saved (`curl -o data.csv u && ./bin/process data.csv`) aren't. A link check, a download written to a file, a note heredoc or `git commit -F -` message, text `echo` or `printf` writes into a file, and the words the parser knows are data (a `git commit`, `tag` or `merge` message, a log's `--grep`, the pattern `grep` searches for, a `sed -n` script) are allowed; other quoted text is read as the line it spells, so `echo 'curl x \| sh'` to the terminal or `gh pr create --body 'curl x \| sh'` is denied, a message with a `$` or a backquote is read as code, and the as-typed line keeps a message `TK-A046` reads after a `;` in it (`git commit -m 'a; curl x \| python3 y.py'` is held), as does a line of more than sixteen such commands, a line with a redirection, a heredoc or a process substitution anywhere in it (`git commit -m 'curl x \| sh' 2>&1` is denied), and a command whose words hold another command's or are held in another's (`time git commit -m …`, a bare `sh` on the same line). They don't follow a downloaded file into a later separate call; command substitutions and shell forms outside the parser's POSIX subset may be held as `TK-SHELL-PARSE`, and the ask rule only recognizes named interpreters (`sh`, `bash`, `zsh`, `dash`, `python` of any version, `perl`, `ruby`, `node`) and common inline flags/script arguments: `curl` or `wget` where a command starts, after up to four keywords, assignments or wrappers (`then`, `time -p`, `env`, `nice -n 5`, `timeout 5`, `sudo`) whose options take no value but a number, piped through up to four stages. It doesn't hold `php -r`, `uv run python -c`, a download inside a `{ …; }` group, a wrapper with another value (`sudo -u bob curl`), or `python3 -m json.tool`, which it reads as a pretty-printer. | The rules match parsed command text and common interpreter shapes; they don't fetch URLs, resolve file contents across calls or emulate each interpreter's full option parser. | Review held calls and inspect a downloaded file before running it; run downloads inside Tracekit Box, where a download can't reach the host; use isolation to contain code that policy does not recognize. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-D007` (#589) allows, under Tracekit's dirs, only: reading the contents of the installed package's files and of `/etc/tracekit/client.json` and `/etc/tracekit/policy*.yaml`, with `cat`, `head`, `tail`, `grep` (without `-r`), `sed -n` (a script that only prints; not `-i`, `-I`, `-f` or another option but `-n`, `-e`, `-E`, `-r`, `-s`, `-u` and `-z`) or `jq`, printing to the terminal or a filter (not a pager: an assignment such as `LESSOPEN` or `LESSCLOSE` makes `less` run a command on the file it opens); a recursive read (`grep -r`) or a `cd` before reads only of the installed package; and listing with `ls`, `stat`, `test` or `find` (without `-exec`, `-ok`, `-fprint`, `-fls`, `-files0-from` or `-delete`) anywhere but a runtime dir, the client and v1 signer homes and a keys dir. Any other program, a read whose output goes to a file, a substitution, `xargs` or a loop, a pattern, and an assignment to `CDPATH`, `HOME`, `OLDPWD` or another variable the shell reads on its own denies there, as do writes, deletes, runs, kills and starting the signer. `tracekit signer vkey` and `trust` (also as `python -m tracekit`) only print or write the public keys and are exempt, so their `--data-dir` and `--config` may read a data dir's public keys or a signer.yaml there; trust's output (`-o`, `--out`, or a prefix of it argparse takes, `--o`, `--ou=`) is a write target of its own, denied there. Signer code may reach only `rpc_schema`'s tables, `reconcile.compared` and `model_names`, and the service's `load_policy` and `DEFAULT_POLICY`, by those names. It matches locations by name, not the live signer's: a scratch `HOME` or `TRACEKIT_RUNTIME_DIR` under test still counts, and so does SDK code that reaches the dev signer (`autospawn` beyond `status` and its errors, `dev_data_dir`), read outside string literals (a string `exec`, `eval`, `importlib` or `runpy` runs too; not one `timeit` or `pdb.run` runs). A kill counts when its pids come at run time from what finds the signer by its usual command lines or names (`pgrep`, `pidof`, `ps` through `grep` or `awk`, or `pkill`'s or `killall`'s own pattern, matched as an extended regex on the engine's own backend: one it can't compile counts), so `kill <pid>` of the signer, or of every pid a `ps` with no filter lists, isn't one (matching the live signer's pid needs the signer to hand it to the engine); its usual command lines are autospawn's (a client's interpreter by its full path, often a venv's `python`, with `-P`), so a pattern on an interpreter's path counts. Code that runs the CLI (`tracekit.cli`'s `main`, `tracekit.__main__`, `runpy.run_module('tracekit')`) counts where its argv is built at run time or names `down`, `uninstall`, `up` or `signer`. A loop's words, a relative path after a literal `cd`, `pushd`, `env -C`, `tar -C`, `git -C` or `make -C`, a path through a `CDPATH` or `HOME` the line sets or a link it makes to a folder, a pattern standing for a component of Tracekit's paths (`trace?it`, `Application Support/*`, `find -path`), and a shell script the call writes and then runs count as what its commands name; a `cd` from the working directory the line doesn't name (`cd ..`), a `CDPATH` or `HOME` the environment already holds, and a bare pattern elsewhere (`~/*/x`) can't be seen. | The policy reads shell argv, file names and the parser's data-flow hints, not filesystem or process state. | Rely on isolation (system mode, a sidecar or a central signer); `TK-D013` still denies a file tool's write to Tracekit's paths. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| A variable the line sets to a plain path (`PY=.venv/bin/python; $PY -m pytest`) is read as that program (#589), as bash runs it with its default IFS. A line that names IFS in any spelling reads no variable through, and a value with characters other than a path's is held; but in a shell that persists between calls, an IFS an earlier call set is still in force, and a path value may then split into another program and its arguments. | The policy reads one call at a time; it can't see the shell's state from earlier calls. | Run agents with a fresh shell per call (Claude Code's Bash tool, Codex's exec) or reset IFS (`unset IFS`) before such a line; a person approves a held call only when what it runs is clear. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| A shell function the line defines and calls is read where it is called and not held (#589), but on a line that may download, where it is held (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`) as on 1.5.3: `TK-D002` reads that line's text, in which a body comes before the download it may run. A line may download when its text names `curl` or `wget`, a command on it is one, or a command's name isn't written plainly (quotes, an escape or a variable made it: `c''url`, `$'\x63url'`, `$X''rl`). A download through another program (Python's `urlretrieve`, `git clone`) isn't one, as `TK-D002` knows only `curl` and `wget`; in a shell that persists between calls, a later call names only the function, and the policy reads that later call as a program of that name, not the body this line gave it. A function the line doesn't call after defining it (a body given after its name's last call too: `f(){ ls; }; f; f(){ sudo id; }`), or exports, is read as if it ran and held. | The policy reads one call at a time; it doesn't know whether the harness keeps its shell. | Run agents with a fresh shell per call (Claude Code's Bash tool, Codex's exec); a person approves a held definition only when its body is fine to run later. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| `TK-A054` holds removing or stopping Tracekit only in the forms it names: `tracekit uninstall` or `down`, `pip`, `python -m pip`, `uv`, `pipx` or `poetry` removing `tracekit-ai` by name, `claude plugin uninstall` or `disable`, and `systemctl` stopping a unit named tracekit. It doesn't hold `python -I -m pip uninstall` (an option before `-m`), a name pip reads the same with doubled separators (`tracekit--ai`) or from a file (`pip uninstall -r reqs.txt`), `conda` or `brew`, the TypeScript SDK's `npm uninstall`, `claude plugin marketplace remove`, or removal done in code (`python -c`); editing a harness's settings to drop the hooks is held (`TK-F009`, `TK-F015`), and so is the agent's own `tracekit init`, `--no-hooks` among it (`TK-A057`); a command that deletes the signer's files or kills its process is `TK-D007`'s deny. | The ways to remove a package or a hook are many; the rule names the ones Tracekit's own docs give. | Run system mode, where the agent's user can't stop or remove the signer, and read `harness_config` flags. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A039`–`TK-A041` hold a destructive statement through a database client (`psql -c`, `mysql -e`, `mongosh`/`mongo --eval`, `sqlite3`'s bare SQL argument, `clickhouse-client --query`, `redis-cli`/`valkey-cli`/`keydb-cli`, a wrapper's `docker`, `podman`, `docker compose`, `kubectl` or `oc exec` (up to eight words before `exec` and eight after it, and a shell's `-c` inside it), an SQL-like MCP tool's `sql`/`query`/`statement` argument) but don't read one run from a file (`psql -f`, `mysql < file.sql`, a `mongosh` script file, a file piped into `redis-cli`), one piped or heredoc'd into `psql`, one built in a variable (`SQL="DROP TABLE x"; psql -c "$SQL"`), a Redis command sent through `EVAL` or a script, or a flush piped through a stage that isn't echo, printf or tee. `TK-A040` reads a flush echoed or printed into the client (also through `command`, `builtin` or `env`, by path, or in a `( )` or `{ }` group), fed to it as a herestring, or on a line of its own in a heredoc's first nine (also through any wrapper or `bash -c`); it misses one printed with other text (`printf '%s' FLUSHALL`, `echo -e 'SELECT 2\nFLUSHDB'`), `env` with options or assignments, a client another wrapper runs from a pipe (`echo FLUSHALL \| docker exec -i c redis-cli`), options after the herestring, more than eight options before it, and a heredoc's later lines. It reads the whole line, a heredoc's body included, so a flush shape in a heredoc that is only written into a file (a test script's cases) is held too. They also miss a statement after a delimiter of the client's own (`mysql --delimiter=//`), a `#` comment before the keyword, a block comment ending in `**/`, a backslash inside a `--` comment, in `TK-A041` a `--` comment containing `"`, an abbreviated option (`psql --comm`), more than thirty-two words before the client's flag, a statement run by dynamic SQL (`DO $$ … EXECUTE 'DROP …' $$`), a `WHERE` that is always true but not literally so (`WHERE id > 0`) and more than eight statements before the destructive one. A comment or a vertical tab between keywords (`DROP/**/TABLE`, `DELETE/**/FROM`) separates them, and the text of MySQL's and MariaDB's executable comments (`/*! … */`, `/*!50000 … */`, `/*M! … */`) is read as the statement it runs, as the SQL tools' `TK-DB001`..`TK-DB005` read it; after `FROM` a `DELETE`'s table and `WHERE` are read with whitespace and comments only between their words. A `psql`, `mysql` or `mariadb` short-option cluster of more than nine flags that take no value, ending in `c` or `e` (which no one needs), is held without being read; a value attached to an option that takes one (`psql -dmydatabase`, `mysql -pdevelopment`) is not such a cluster. A rolled-back destructive statement is held although the transaction would undo it: there is no ROLLBACK exemption, a comment or a mysql \G/\g terminator kept breaking it, so one approval for a test transaction is the price. | The rules read the command's words and the MCP tool's arguments as typed; a file's content and a variable's value aren't on them. They read `;` (and mysql's `\G`/`\g`) as a statement's end and `--` and `/* */` as comments, and skip at most eight statements, a bound that keeps the fallback engine fast. | Approve the held call, or run migrations through the deployment's reviewed migration path; keep production credentials out of the agent's reach. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A041` holds a destructive statement in an SQL-like tool's `sql`/`query`/`statement` argument, whatever the tool's class (an MCP tool, one a pack maps to class `sql`, one no pack maps), and reads the tool's name: only a tool whose name looks like a database tool is held, so an ordinary search whose `query` text is itself a destructive statement (a search for `DROP TABLE x`) is not, while a database tool that only checks a statement is (LangChain's `sql_db_query_checker` given a `DROP` asks on `coding`). A call over 64 KiB to a tool whose name looks like a database tool (`execute_sql`, `run_sql`, a bulk `insert_rows` on a `db` server), of any class, asks on `coding` whatever it holds (`TK-OVERSIZE`), where any other call that size is flagged: the rule reads the whole arguments, and a subject over 64 KiB is never cut. `server` and `strict` deny every call that size, and `dev` holds it (`oversize: ask`). | SQL tool names are unbounded, so the rule matches names like `sql`, `postgres`, `mysql`, `sqlite`, `clickhouse`, `mongo` or `database`; a destructive statement through any other tool name is not held. | Approve the held call; the hold is ask, not deny. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| **(1.5.3)** `TK-DB003` reads a DELETE's condition as constant only in plain forms (`1`, `TRUE`, `NOT FALSE`, two numbers or two literals compared, one pair of parentheses), and only first before an `OR` or last after one outside parentheses: `WHERE id IS NOT NULL`, `WHERE x = x`, `WHERE 1=1 OR …` inside parentheses and an expression that is always true some other way aren't denied, and a constant false one (`WHERE 1=0`, which deletes nothing) is. `TK-DB005` reads a `COPY` of a server file after a table name of up to 256 characters and a column list of up to 1000, or a query's `) TO` a literal. | The rules read the statement's text, not its meaning; the bounds keep the fallback engine linear. | Give the agent's database role no DELETE on tables it mustn't empty, and no superuser or `pg_read_server_files`/`pg_write_server_files`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-S012` (the server packs) reads a code runner by its name and argument keys: a runner named otherwise (`mcp__x__sandbox_submit`) or taking its code under another key (`snippet`) isn't held, and a tool named for running that isn't a runner (`run_report` with a `source`) is held. `TK-S010` holds a `command`, `cmd`, `script` or `shell_command` argument in any MCP tool, but not one named otherwise (`query` holding a shell line). | The rules read the tool's name and its arguments, not what the server does with them. | Map the deployment's own runners to the `shell` class, or name them in an ask rule of its own. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| A shell whose language the parser doesn't read (`fish`, `csh`, `tcsh`, PowerShell, `nu`, `xonsh`, `elvish`) asks (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`) whenever it runs commands from its line or stdin, even `pwsh -c 'Get-Date'`; a script file it runs (`fish build.fish`) isn't read, as a POSIX shell's isn't. | Their syntax isn't POSIX, so the rules anchored on a command's name can't read what they run. | Approve the held call, or run the command through a POSIX shell. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| With `on_parse_error: deny`, an ordinary command whose program the parser can't read (`$EDITOR notes.md`, a script piped into `bash`, an unclosed quote) is denied, not held. | The signer can't tell such a command from one built to hide what it runs, and with deny no person sees it. | Keep the default (`ask`) wherever a person answers holds; with deny, have the agent name programs literally. | [policy](policy-v2.md#file-format) |
| `strict.yaml` holds ordinary development work: every push or publish, package install, `docker run`, apply, network command and `PATH=`-style setting waits for a person (50 of E17's 944 benign calls, 5.3%, and 20 of E21's 130 developer-session calls, 15%: [the rates](evaluation.md#e21-policy-robustness)), and a command whose program comes from a variable (`$EDITOR notes.md`) is denied. "Every" is the programs `TK-F004`, `TK-F011` and `TK-F013` name ([policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped)): another package manager, an install with options before its verb (`npm --prefix x install`), a network call made inside an interpreter (`python -c 'urllib…'`) and a startup file of another shell (fish, PowerShell) aren't held; and `TK-F004` and `TK-F011` read the whole line, so a commit message or `echo` naming `npm install` or `PATH=` is held too. | It trades interruptions for seeing every change that leaves the workspace before it runs; the flags it raises can't tell a routine push from a harmful one. | Use it where a person answers holds promptly; elsewhere use `server.yaml` and read the flags. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| On calls written blind to the rules (E21's held-out pool), the server pack stopped or held half the harmful ones on its one blind run (211 of 420), and more after rules were added that target the pool's shapes, a number that overstates blind recall; it holds a few percent of the benign ones. It misses many exfiltration, privilege-escalation, prompt-injection, destructive, defence-evasion and supply-chain calls (the counts by category: [evaluation](evaluation.md#e21-policy-robustness)). | The packs match known command shapes; calls phrased in ways no rule anticipates aren't matched. | Treat the packs as tripwires: run agents in a sandbox, keep credentials out of their reach, rely on the signer and witnesses for the record; `strict.yaml` holds more at more interruptions. | [evaluation](evaluation.md#e21-policy-robustness) |
| A secret's value read in a way `TK-A035` to `TK-A038` don't name isn't held. They read the commands of AWS Secrets Manager and SSM Parameter Store, GCP Secret Manager and Azure Key Vault, and MCP tools whose names say they return a secret (or read an SSM parameter with decryption turned on). Not held: another store's shell commands (1Password's `op read` or `op item get`; Kubernetes and Vault reads are `TK-A052`'s and `TK-A053`'s), a command run through a wrapper the shell parser doesn't read (`aws-vault exec … --`), an AWS call whose decryption comes from `--cli-input-json`, an ALL-CAPS tool name, an MCP tool whose server chose another name (`fetch_credential`) or a spelling the patterns lack, a CLI command given to an MCP tool whose name doesn't end in a word for running one (`TK-A036` reads `call_aws`, `run_command`, `execute`, `bash` and the like) or whose words are quoted (`TK-A036` reads the command as text, not as a shell would: `aws 'secretsmanager' …`), an SDK call in an interpreter one-liner (`python -c`), and a credential that isn't in a secret store (`aws ecr get-login-password`, `aws sts assume-role`). A tool that both lists secrets and gets one (Azure's Key Vault tool) is held for both when its tool name says it gets a secret; one named only `keyvault` that takes the operation as an argument isn't. A call over 64 KiB to a tool `TK-A036` or `TK-A038` reads (`call_aws`, `run_command`, `get_parameter`) asks on `coding` and `dev` whatever it holds (`TK-OVERSIZE`), where any other MCP call that size is flagged: the rules read the whole arguments, and a subject over 64 KiB is never cut. `server` and `strict` already deny every call that size. | The rules read command words, tool names and the arguments the tool is given; they can't tell what a tool with another name returns. | Hold the tools your servers use for secrets by name in a rule of your own, and keep agents' IAM roles from reading secrets they don't need. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A052` and `TK-A053` hold only the `kubectl`, `oc`, `vault` and `bao` commands they name. Not held: the Kubernetes API read another way (`kubectl get --raw /api/v1/namespaces/p/secrets/db`, `curl` to the API server, a client library), `kubectl edit secret` with an editor that prints (`EDITOR=cat`), kubectl run through a program the shell parser doesn't read (`microk8s kubectl`, `minikube kubectl --`) or an alias (`k`), an output option given before `get`, or before the type with a space in its value, or more than sixteen words after the type, or a type ninth or later in a list (`kubectl get secret a b … q -o yaml`), and kubectl reached through an MCP tool (a `run_command`, or a Kubernetes MCP server's `kubectl_get` with `secrets`); a Vault login or another path that returns a credential (`vault login`, `vault write auth/approle/login`, `vault write pki/issue/web`), `vault token lookup` (it prints the token's id), Vault's API through `curl` or a client library (`hvac`), and `VAULT_TOKEN` read from the environment. A long option other than `--namespace` is read with or without a value, so a type named as its value (`kubectl get --context secrets pods -o yaml`) is held. | They read each command's words as typed; what an editor or a library does, and which long options take a value, isn't in the command. | Approve the held call; give the agent a Kubernetes role without `get` on secrets, and a Vault policy that can't read the paths or create the tokens its task doesn't need. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A058` to `TK-A060` don't read: weights saved with `torch.save` or similar inside a Python script; folders copied or unpacked (`cp -r`, `rsync`, `tar -x`, `unzip`); other formats (`.bin`, `.h5`, `.onnx`, `.pkl`, `.npz`, `.npy`, `.msgpack`, `.joblib`); download output options (`curl -o`, `wget -O`); deleting weights (`rm`); Python SDK uploads (`huggingface_hub.upload_folder(…)`); or Git/Git LFS pushes to `huggingface.co`. They also leave other hub subcommands alone. | The file rule reads the path the file tool is given; the shell rule reads shell write targets (`WRITE_TARGET:`) from the command's effects, not script contents; the hub rule reads command argv. | Keep production weights on a read-only mount and publish from CI with a token the agent does not hold; add a rule for other formats or SDKs. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A071` to `TK-A074` hold the raw socket and cgroup moves they name, not every way to the same end. Not held: a socket named through a variable the line sets elsewhere (`D=/var/run/docker.sock; curl --unix-socket $D …`) is read only where the parser resolves it; `DOCKER_HOST=unix://…` set after `env -u NAME`, `timeout 5 env` or an assignment whose value has `;`, `&` or `\|`, or a `(`, `)`, `{` or `}` outside one `$(…)` or `${…}` (quoted or not: `A='(x)'`, `X={a,b}`, `A=$((1+2))`), or a socket path with one (`unix:///x{a}`), or by `export DOCKER_HOST=…; cmd` for a later program (`TK-A072` reads the assignment where a command starts, after at most eight words that are assignments, options, `env` (also by its path), `command`, `time` or `!`); a program given the socket by a configuration file or a Docker context; a cgroup's `release_agent` written by a script's own code. Held on purpose: a test runner that reaches Podman or Docker through `DOCKER_HOST`, as testcontainers does (`DOCKER_HOST=unix:///run/user/1000/podman/podman.sock mvn test`, `… ./gradlew test`), since a container started through the raw socket can mount the host; approve it once, or run it where the socket isn't reachable. Held although harmless: `TK-A072` reads the line with its quotes resolved, so a quoted text such as `git commit -m 'fix; DOCKER_HOST=unix:///x pytest'` is held, and any path longer than eight directories before `docker` is read as another program. | `TK-A071` reads curl, wget, socat, nc, ncat and netcat by name in each command's argv; `TK-A073` reads shell write targets (`WRITE_TARGET:`), not script contents; `TK-A074` reads the path a file tool is given. | Keep the container socket out of the agent's environment and run the agent's containers through a rootless engine; add a rule for another client or form. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A075` to `TK-A078` hold the login, account-file, setuid and startup-file changes they name, not every way to the same end. Not held: macOS's own account tools other than `dscl`'s user records (`TK-A075`); a here-document's body written to a startup file, a network shell more than 128 characters from the write, and `nc` with more than four words before its exec option (`TK-A078`); each such startup-file write is still flagged (`TK-F011`). Held though harmless: a project folder's own `etc/passwd` or `etc/group` (`TK-A076` reads the path's end, as it can't tell a project's copy from the system's), and setgid on a shared folder (`chmod g+s`, `TK-A077`). | The rules read a command's words and the files it writes as the parser sees them, each within a bounded stretch so the fallback engine stays linear; a here-document's body is the command's input, not its words. | Keep the machine's account, sudo and cron files and the agent user's startup files out of the agent's reach (system mode); approve a held project file or shared folder once you have looked at it. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| A rule's `remedy` is advice the policy's author wrote: lint checks its form (one short present-tense sentence), not that it is right, and an agent may ignore it. | It is text shown with the decision, not a rule. | Write a remedy that names a safe alternative the agent can carry out; the deny or hold stands either way. | [policy](policy-v2.md#file-format) |
| A relative path, or one with `..`, is unverifiable; rules match its certain suffix. | A symlink could redirect it. | Write path rules that also work on suffixes. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| Path rules, and shell rules on `target` and `effects`, are matched casefolded too (`cp x .ENV` is denied as `.env` is), and a command's program names are read casefolded (`ſh` is `sh`). A rule on the shell `tracekit` field (`TK-D007`) matches in any case, as its pattern names folders in mixed case (`Library/Application Support`). | The agent's file system may ignore case whatever the signer's does: macOS opens `~/.ssh` for `~/.SSH` or `~/.ſsh`, and runs `/bin/sh` for `ſh`. | Write path rules in lower case, or with both cases of a letter. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| Patterns must stay inside an RE2 subset. | A decision must not depend on which engine is installed. | `tracekit policy lint` rejects the rest. | [policy](policy-v2.md#patterns-the-re2-subset) |
| Without `google-re2`, a large subject, or a short one under a repeat of overlapping alternatives (`secret(.\|\s)*key`), can exhaust the 1 s budget: the decision denies, is marked `nondeterministic: true` and says so in its reason, so the two engines can decide differently. Every shipped pattern searches 64 KiB built to make it back off or rescan within 150 ms on CI's runners (about 20 ms on a laptop; `TK-A040` within 0.25 s, `TK-A001` and `TK-A011` slower on repeated options, below), except eight on one command or SQL statement that repeats their first word thousands of times, measured at 64 KiB on an M-series Mac: `TK-D002`, `TK-D004`, `TK-D006`, `TK-D011` and `TK-DB003` over 5 s, `TK-C010` 3.4 s, `TK-D007` 0.3 s and `TK-A032` 0.05 s (`curl` with no pipe, `rm -r` with no root or home path, `curl` with no `.env` upload, `cat` with no upload, `DELETE FROM` with no `;`, `aws s3 rm` with no `--recursive`, `kill` with no `tracekit` after it, `aws s3 sync` whose `s3://` words are each followed by an option and none ends the command): each occurrence rescans the rest, so such a call can deny on the fallback engine where RE2 decides it. `TK-A039` reads up to sixteen words after `sqlite3` as options before its SQL, so 64 KiB of short words after `sqlite3` takes about 0.2 s, and `TK-A040` up to eight options after each `redis-cli` on a line, so 64 KiB of `redis-cli -a ` takes about 0.07 s on a laptop and up to 0.2 s on CI's runners (both linear); `TK-A029` takes about 0.1 s on 64 KiB of newlines after `journalctl`, as 1.4.2 did. `TK-A001` and `TK-A011` read any number of options, each with or without a value, before `pulumi`'s (and `railway run`'s or `heroku run`'s) command and `systemctl`'s verb, so 64 KiB of `pulumi -a ` or `systemctl -a ` takes about 0.25 s on an M-series Mac: backing off that repeat grows with the square of its length on the fallback engine. | The fallback `regex` engine backtracks; RE2 is linear. A rule that looks for one thing after another with any text between can't be written linearly for a backtracking engine without atomic groups, which RE2 lacks. | Install `google-re2` on the signer (a dependency from Python 3.10). | [policy](policy-v2.md#patterns-the-re2-subset) |
| `TK-F007` flags a detached tmux session only when its `-d` comes within 200 characters after `tmux new`, on either engine; one further along isn't flagged. | The bound keeps the pattern linear on the fallback engine, and both engines run the same pattern so they decide alike. | Read long `tmux new` commands in the record yourself, or add a rule of your own for the tmux options your agents use. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `tracekit policy lint` refuses an unbounded repeat whose body starts with an unbounded repeat (`(a+)+`), not every super-linear pattern: a bounded repeat of one (`^(.*?,){11}P`: 5 s on 260 characters) or overlapping alternatives under a repeat (`(a\|aa)+$`: 3 s on 35) pass it. | Refusing every repeat over an unbounded body would refuse linear patterns too (`TK-M003`'s `(([^"@\\]\|\\.)*@){10}`); telling them apart needs to know whether the repeated class can match what follows it. | Install `google-re2`; time a new pattern on the fallback engine before shipping it (the speed test in `tests/test_policy2.py` does this for the shipped packs). | [policy](policy-v2.md#patterns-the-re2-subset) |
| A rule's subject over 64 KiB is never cut: the rule's own section decides. An http call's `urls` list of more than 64 URLs is denied (`TK-OVERSIZE`), as a shell command over 64 KiB is. On the laptop default (`oversize: ask`) each of these is held instead: a `Write` of a 108 KB file, a large edit or MCP write, or a long agent prompt waits for a person, where the server pack's `TK-S002` (which reads every call's whole arguments for a secret) denies it. The 64 KiB is counted on the subject as its rule reads it, not on the file or text the call carries: a rule that reads a whole call reads its arguments' canonical JSON, where a newline or a quote takes two bytes, so a `Write` of 61,440 bytes of short lines (71,724 bytes as JSON) is over it. | Cutting or windowing would let content hide past the cut; each URL is read on its own. | Write a large file in parts under 64 KiB each, or approve the held call; expect large arguments to be flagged, held or denied by the matching section on a server; fetch more URLs in several calls. | [policy](policy-v2.md#decisions) |
| A `decide` whose parsed arguments nest deeper than a request may (128 levels, counting the request's own, or too deep to check) is refused as a whole (`invalid_request`), not decided: no `policy.decision` names the call, only the signer's refusal count does. Raw arguments (`args_source: "raw"`) nested past 128 levels are denied and recorded (`TK-ARGS-INVALID`). | Checking, hashing or copying such a request would exceed the parser's limit before anything could be recorded. | Nothing to do for safety: the hooks and integrations don't run a call they couldn't get decided. Flatten arguments that deep if a tool really needs them. | [policy](policy-v2.md#decisions) |
| URL rules read the URL text; a public name that resolves to an internal address isn't caught. | The signer doesn't resolve names. | Add network egress controls. | [Browser Use](integrations/browser-use.md) |
| **(1.5.3)** In an MCP tool's arguments, `target` (read by `TK-S001`, `TK-S004`, `TK-F005` and `TK-F012`) leaves out the text the tool only carries: the string value of a `title`, `body`, `text`, `description`, `comment`, `message`, `subject` or `content` argument at any depth when the tool's name says it posts, comments, replies, creates an issue, comment, pull request, discussion, review, ticket or note, or sends a message (`create_issue`, `add_comment`, `sendMessage`; not `create_file` or `create_or_update_file`) and doesn't say it types, sends keys, runs, executes, evaluates or holds code or a cell, and the `query` or `q` of a tool whose name says it searches and doesn't say it fetches, reads or opens. | `TK-A047` (removing a repository's secrets, branch protection, rulesets or security features through GitHub) doesn't read another forge's CLI or API (GitLab's protected branches, Bitbucket, Gitea), a GraphQL mutation (`deleteBranchProtectionRule`), a `security_and_analysis` change whose body comes from a file or a pipe (`gh api -X PATCH repos/o/r --input off.json`) or whose status comes from a variable, a JSON body padded so more than 200 characters sit between `security_and_analysis` and `status` or more than 16 non-word characters between `status` and `disabled`, a token used from a script file or an SDK, other HTTP clients (`wget`, HTTPie, `xh`), a `gh` alias or a shell function, a path handed over by `xargs`, a `curl` config file (`-K`), a URL `curl` rewrites before sending it (globbing such as `{branches}` or `branche[s-s]`, `--expand-url` variables, `./` and `../` segments), a repository named by its numeric id (`repositories/<id>/…`), a branch name longer than 250 characters, a percent-encoded path part (`branche%73`), a doubled slash, a path part in capitals (`REPOS/`) or `status=Disabled` (whether GitHub accepts these is untested), more than four options before a subcommand, more than sixteen space-separated pieces before the method, between the method and the path or between `gh repo edit` and its flag (each space inside a quoted argument starts a new piece, so one long `-H` header or `--description` can use them up), or a change made in the repository's settings page. Card spending (Issuing cards and their limits), Shared Payment Tokens and subscriptions aren't held. A Stripe test-mode call is held too, and so is a request whose body value names a money URL; `curl -G` (a GET) is read as one only as curl's first argument, and help only as `stripe <resource> [<operation> ...] --help` (or `-h`) with nothing else (an option takes the next word as its value, so `--description -h` is no help). | The rules read each command's words and each MCP call's name and arguments as typed: what a script, file or variable holds is known only when it runs, the key's mode isn't on the command line, and Stripe doesn't document `stripe_api_write`'s argument names (`TK-A045` holds a refund named anywhere in the call). | Also turn on Stripe's [approval rules](https://docs.stripe.com/account/approvals) with an agent-tagged key: Stripe enforces them on its side, whatever the agent runs. | A hold on moving money (`TK-A043` to `TK-A045`, `TK-M001`, `TK-M002`, `TK-M005`) gives the rule's fixed reason and what to check, not the amount, currency and payee parsed out of the call; a call with no amount (a full refund, a confirm, capture or invoice pay) moves an amount the call doesn't show. | The hold's reason is the rule's own text, chosen by its id; a per-call summary would need a parser for each provider's arguments in the signer. | Read the command and any file it sends (`tracekit approvals show <id>` prints the call) before approving, and look up the charge, payment intent or invoice a call with no amount names; set amounts on the call that creates the payment where you can. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#calls-that-move-money) |

A money tool mapped to `app` (or `sql`), or one named otherwise and called with no amount, isn't held. | The pack reads what the deployment's `tools` map says a tool is; a name says what it does only by convention. | Map every tool that moves money to `payment`, or add an `ask` rule of your own for its name. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-M003` holds an email to ten or more recipients in one `to`, `cc`, `bcc` or `recipients` argument (a list, or a string of ten addresses, display names included) or to a whole-company address (`all@`, `everyone@`, `everybody@`, `staff@`, `all-staff@`, `all-hands@` and `all-employees@` with `-`, `.`, `_` or nothing between the words, `customers@`, `users@`, in any case, after a display name, a separator or an escaped character such as a quote or a newline). It misses a list address with a tag or a quoted local part (`all+news@`, `"all"@`), ten or more recipients written as objects (`[{"email": …}, …]`) or a single object (`{"to": {"address": "all@corp.com"}}`), ten split across arguments (five in `to`, five in `bcc`), an argument named otherwise or in another case (`emails`, `To`), and a list address by another name (`company@`). | The rule reads the call's arguments as JSON text, not a parsed recipient list; counting across arguments or inside objects would need a parser for each email tool's arguments. | Add an `ask` rule of your own for your email tool's recipient shape and your organisation's list addresses. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-D010` (writing a credentials file from the shell) also denies a read redirected from one (`while read l; do …; done < .env`), and `TK-A011` and `TK-A013` hold one from their places. | Each rule reads every command and every redirection target on its own, anchored, so the fallback engine stays linear on long commands; a redirection's target doesn't say which way it goes. | Read the file another way (`cat .env`, or the agent's file tool). | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |

| `TK-D004` (recursive delete of root or home) denies `rm -r` of root or a home (and of a pattern that may match each of its files or folders: `~/*`, `~/.*`, `~/?*`, `/*/`, `///*`), among any number of other plain words or up to sixteen of any other shape (`rm -rf a.txt ~/`), with `-r` before or after it, and `find … -delete` on root, or on a home with no filter but depth and type (or `-true`, `-print`, and a name, path or regex that matches every file, `*` or `.*`, or `-size +0`), or a negated name, path or regex, which picks all but a few), as one of up to nine starting points after up to eight of find's own options, and `-exec rm -r … {}` read as `-delete`; `rsync --delete` into root or a home; a home is `~`, `~user`, `$HOME`, `/home/<user>`, `/Users/<user>` or `/root` (also under `/var`, `/private` or macOS's `/System/Volumes/Data`: `/var/root`, `/var/home/<user>`), and the folder an interpreter's tree delete (`shutil.rmtree`, `fs.rmSync`, `FileUtils.rm_rf`) or a container's `rm -r` of its mount of one names, also as a relative operand after a `cd` there (`cd && rm -rf *`), through a link the line made to one (`ln -s ~ h; rm -rf h/*`) and as the words `echo` pipes into `xargs rm`. A `find` there that picks files by name or age (`find ~ -name '*.log' -delete`, `-mtime -100000`, `-newer f`), a root or home reached through another directory (`/tmp/..`) or a variable other than `$HOME`, and a link or `cd` from an earlier call, aren't denied; a `cd` in a subshell or branch counts for the rest of the line. | A find that picks files under the home is often routine cleanup, and a deny can't be approved. | Run agents as a user whose home holds nothing they can't lose, or in a sandbox that keeps them out of it. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-D014` (recursive delete or permission change of a system folder) denies `rm -r`, `find … -delete` (with no filter but depth and type, or one that matches every file), `git clean` and `chmod -R` or `chown -R` of `/etc`, `/usr`, `/var`, `/bin`, `/sbin`, `/lib`, `/lib64`, `/boot`, `/opt`, `/srv`, macOS's `/System`, `/Library`, `/Applications`, `/private/etc` and `/private/var`, or `C:\Windows` (`C:/`, `/c/`), in any case, with extra slashes, `/./` or one `<folder>/../` step, and of a pattern holding a `*` in one (`/etc/*`); `rm -r` among any number of plain words or up to sixteen of any other shape. Not denied: a `find` whose filters are in parentheses (`find /etc \( -type f -o -type d \) -delete`, allowed) or that changes what it finds through `-exec rm` without `-r` or `-exec chmod -R`, `xargs rm -r` fed by a herestring (`xargs rm -rf <<< /etc`), a folder named by a pattern (`/et?`, `/us*`), more than sixteen words before it that aren't plain (`a1`, `x.txt`), two `<folder>/..` steps (`/a/b/../../etc`), a subfolder followed by `..` (`/etc/ssh/..`, flagged), Windows through WSL's or Cygwin's mount (`/mnt/c/Windows`, `/cygdrive/c/Windows`), and the commands the rule doesn't name: `chgrp -R` and `chattr -R +i` (allowed), `rsync --delete` into one and `mv /etc …` (flagged; `strict.yaml` holds the `rsync`); the others are flagged (`TK-F003`). | The rule reads each command's words as typed, as `TK-D004` reads a home: a pattern or a herestring is expanded only when the command runs, and the other commands reach a system folder in ways it doesn't name. | Run agents as a user who can't write system folders (not root, no `sudo`), or in a container or VM; add an `ask` rule of your own for `chgrp`, `chattr`, `rsync --delete` or `mv` of a system folder if your agents shouldn't run them. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-S006` reads tar's `--to-command`, `--checkpoint-action=exec` and `--use-compress-program` (each also as a unique prefix) and `-I`; tar's `--info-script` (`-F`), `--new-volume-script` and `--rsh-command` also run a program and aren't read. | These run only with a multi-volume archive (`-M`) or one on another host (`host:path`). | Add an `ask` rule of your own for these options if your agents use tar that way. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| **(1.5.3)** `TK-S006` denies rsync's `-e`/`--rsh` and man's `-P` only when they name a shell or an interpreter (`sh`, `bash`, `python`, `env`, …, by a path of up to 128 characters too), man's pager also when it holds a `;`, `&`, `\|`, `<`, `>`, a backquote or `$(`; another program they name (`rsync -e ./x`) isn't denied, nor git's `difftool -x`. | `rsync -e ssh` and `man -P cat` are everyday commands; only the program's name tells them apart from one that runs a shell, and a bounded read keeps the fallback engine linear. | Keep the programs an agent may run on its path to the ones it needs, or add an `ask` rule of your own on these options. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| **(1.5.3)** `TK-C001` (changing cloud identity or access) denies sharing an RDS or EC2 snapshot with `all` only in the sharing option's own value, read up to the next option: `--values-to-add` naming `all` (alone, in a list, or in JSON), `--create-volume-permission` adding `Group` `all` (in shorthand or JSON, after a `Remove` list too), or `--operation-type add` with `--group-names all`, each also by aws's unique prefixes and in any case. The value is read a word at a time, up to 1,000 words and never into the next option, so `all` or a `Group` after 1,000 words isn't read (about 250 pretty-printed EC2 entries; a `Remove` list before the `Add` is read whole), and through `call_aws` an option in quotes is read as part of the value (`… 111122223333 "--values-to-remove=all"` is denied); an `Add` list read on into a `Remove` list that names `all` is denied too, and a share sent as a whole request (`--cli-input-json`, a file) isn't read. | A snapshot shared with every AWS account is public; the rule reads the options the CLI documents for it, and stops at the next option so it decides in time. | Pass `Add` and `Remove` in separate calls; deny `--cli-input-json` on these commands in a rule of your own. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| **(1.5.3)** `TK-C002` (changing cloud identity or access through an MCP tool) reads a tool's name: attaching or putting a policy, an IAM policy binding, creating an access key, a service account key, a role assignment or binding, and a grant of a role, access, a privilege, IAM, admin, owner or permissions, in snake, kebab, camel or Pascal case. In an MCP tool's `cli_command`, `command` or `cmd`, `TK-C001` and `TK-C010` read words split by spaces and escaped tabs, newlines and returns, as `TK-A036` does, but kubectl's options and type after `delete` only by spaces (`kubectl delete\tns\tprod` isn't held). | Which kubectl options take a value isn't in the command, and the resources a file names are read only when kubectl runs. | Approve the held call; give the agent a Kubernetes role that can't delete namespaces or volumes, or add an `ask` rule of your own for `kubectl delete -f`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |

It holds every `update-trail --s3-bucket-name`, the trail's own bucket too. | What a file or input holds is read only when the CLI runs; a trusted IP list is also routine setup, and its command doesn't say whether its addresses are safe; the bound keeps the fallback engine linear. | Approve the held call; give the agent an IAM role that can't update trails or detectors (or a service control policy that denies it), and add an `ask` rule of your own for `create-ip-set` if your agents shouldn't add one. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-S004` denies the credentials files it names wherever a call acts on them (cloud, cluster, registry, database and Git credentials, `.netrc`, a Vault token, `/etc/shadow`, a process's `environ`, mounted secrets, and a private key in `.ssh`: `id_` and a name with no `.` after it, `id_rsa_work` and `id_ed25519_sk` too, not `id_rsa.pub` or `id_rsa-cert.pub`). It doesn't read a key elsewhere or named otherwise (`~/keys/deploy.pem`, `.ssh/id_rsa.old`, `.ssh/github`), other tools' token files (`~/.config/gh/hosts.yml`, `~/.terraform.d/credentials.tfrc.json`, an `.npmrc` or `.pypirc` with a token), a path built at run time, or a file's content once it is copied under another name. | It reads a path's name; a file's content and where a program keeps its token aren't on the call. | Keep keys and tokens out of the agent's reach (a sandbox, a separate user), and redefine `TK-S004` with the paths your hosts use. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |

It reads the command `trap` runs, what `at` or `batch` get in a herestring or heredoc (a name there built from a variable, `at now <<< "$JOB"`, isn't held for `TK-SHELL-PARSE`, as before they were read; one in a trap's action is), and the sources `zip -m` deletes. A script the call writes, then runs, is read as that interpreter's inline code, and each word the run gives it counts as written. In a call with a writer that gets its files at run time or runs code (`xargs rm`, also fed a herestring or heredoc; `rm "$f"`, `parallel 'rm {}'`, `docker exec c sh -c 'rm …'`, `perl -e` with a write, `at` or `batch` fed from a pipe, `osascript` with `do shell script` or a Finder `delete`), or a line whose commands the parser can't read and holds (`TK-SHELL-PARSE`: a pipe into `sh`, `fish -c`, a name built from a variable) where a writer's name reaches that command (in its words, in an `echo` or `printf` piped into it, or in a value the line gives the variable its name expands: `C=rm; $C -rf …`), any word, herestring or heredoc naming those folders counts, so a read in that call is denied too, on any of its lines (`rm "$f"; ls ~/.codex/sessions`, also with the `rm` in a `trap`). Deleting a project's own `.claude` is flagged (`TK-F015`), not denied, and so is a shell write into OpenCode's config (`~/.config/opencode` or `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/opencode`); a file tool's write to `~/.config/opencode` is denied (`TK-D009`). Claude Code's memory notes beside the transcripts (a `.md` file directly in `.claude/projects/<project>/memory/`) aren't held or flagged, from the shell or a file tool. A link made in those folders is denied (`ln`, `cp -s`, `cp -l`, also as a memory note), but not one made elsewhere that points at a transcript (`ln -s ~/.claude/projects/p/a.jsonl ./x`), nor one moved into memory/ with `mv`: a later write through it writes the transcript. | It reads each command's words as typed; what the working directory, a variable or a glob holds, and what interpreter code does, is known only when it runs. A project's `.claude` can't be told from a home's without knowing the home. | Run the agent in a sandbox that can't write outside its workspace ([Claude Code's](integrations/claude-code-sandbox.md), [Gemini CLI's](integrations/gemini-sandbox.md)), and on Linux import a kernel sensor's records ([Tetragon](integrations/tetragon.md)): each file write is signed into the run with its path, and `tracekit analyze --v2` flags one no call explains (`TK-X206`). | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |

A shell command writing one isn't held: an append to a startup file is flagged (`TK-F011`), `crontab` is flagged (`TK-F007`), and a copy into `/etc/cron.d` or `/etc/profile.d` passes, unless what it writes runs a download (`TK-D002`). Other ways to run something at login or boot aren't held: `systemctl link`, `loginctl enable-linger`, other supervisors (`pm2 startup`, supervisord, runit, OpenRC) and Windows' Run keys, Startup folder and `schtasks`. | Each service manager keeps its own places and commands; the pack names those of the hosts coding agents run on most. | Add `ask` rules of your own for the managers your hosts have, run the agent where it can't reach them (a sandbox, or a container without them), and on Linux import a kernel sensor's records ([Tetragon](integrations/tetragon.md)): each file write is signed into the run with its path, and `tracekit analyze --v2` flags one no call explains (`TK-X206`). | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A013` holds setting or unsetting `git config core.hooksPath` typed as a command (and `git config --get core.hooksPath x`, a read with a value pattern); it doesn't hold the hooks path set another way: `git -c core.hooksPath=… <command>` for one command, `GIT_CONFIG_KEY_*` environment variables, a `.git/config` a file tool writes, or a config file `include` pulls in; and `TK-A012`/`TK-A013` don't hold a write to a folder the hooks path already points at (`.husky/pre-commit` where Husky set it). | Git reads its configuration from several places when it runs; the rule reads one command's words. | Use the server pack (its `TK-S006` denies `git -c` with a hooks path), add an `ask` rule of your own for file-tool writes to `.git/config`, and review a repository's hooks path (`git config --show-origin core.hooksPath`) before anyone commits in it. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
