A global option the CLIs document as taking a value (`-H`, `--context`, `--config`, `--log-level`, podman's `--url` and `--connection`, the TLS and storage paths) takes the next word even when it is named like a verb (`docker --context run run …`); another global option takes the next word unless an option or `run`, `create`, `exec` or `container` follows, so an unknown option with a value named so hides the verb after it. Single capabilities (`--cap-add SYS_ADMIN`), other host namespaces (`--net=host`, `--userns=host`), a file under `/etc` (`/etc/passwd`), `docker compose run`, other runtimes (`nerdctl`), compose contents and API calls aren't read. | File policy sees the path, not file contents; shell policy sees only command arguments. | Review compose changes and remote/container API access separately, or use a runtime policy that sees the resolved container configuration. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-D021` blocks direct HTTP(S) package URLs in named install commands (**(1.5.3)** in any case, and as a PEP 508 direct reference, `name @ https://…`); `TK-A028` asks on a version-control source anywhere in an install's words (`git+https://` and the other `git+` schemes, `git://`, npm's `github:`/`gitlab:`/`bitbucket:`/`gist:` shorthands, a PEP 508 `name @ git+…`, `cargo install --git`), but not npm's bare `user/repo` shorthand or options before the install verb. `npm i <url>`, `pip install -q <url>`, `pip install -U <url>`, other option orders, preconfigured unknown sources, transitive dependency URLs and other package clients may not match. `TK-A024` and `TK-A030` hold a file tool's or a shell command's write to known source-config paths (`.npmrc`, `pip.conf`, `.pypirc`, apt sources, yum repos), and `TK-A025` installs with explicit non-default index, registry or `--find-links` options (pip, uv, uvx, npm, npx, pnpm, yarn, bun, poetry, cargo, gem), `config set` (`npm set`) of the index or registry, and the source variables pip, uv and npm read set before a command or through `env` or `export` (`PIP_INDEX_URL`, `UV_INDEX_URL`, `npm_config_registry`, …), npm's scoped registry option (`--@acme:registry=`), gem's `-s` on `install`, `update` or `fetch`, and `gem sources --add`. An index named inside a requirements or lock file (`pip install -r` of a file with `--index-url`), a config file chosen by a variable (`PIP_CONFIG_FILE`, `NPM_CONFIG_USERCONFIG`), a config file written by an interpreter or under another name, and other package managers (conda, `poetry source add`) aren't held. | The signer doesn't resolve package-manager configuration or dependency metadata before deciding, and reads files only by the names a command gives them. | Pin and verify package sources in a controlled build environment. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A026` and `TK-A027` hold edits and recognized shell writes/deletes (`effects`: a redirection, `rm`, `mv`, `cp`, `ln`, `tee`, `truncate`, `shred`, `dd`, `sed -i`, `perl -i`, `find -delete`) to named history and log paths (also `$HISTFILE`, and `//var/log`, which Linux and macOS read as `/var/log`), and `TK-A029` `history -c`, turning history off and vacuuming the journal; macOS's user logs (`~/Library/Logs`) aren't named. Other history-file writes such as `history -w`, application-managed history, remote logs and paths reached through an unrecognized program (`python -c`) may not match. | The rules match visible paths and command arguments; they don't observe filesystem effects. | Preserve logs outside the agent's control and use a sandbox or kernel sensor to detect writes. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A027` holds `find … -delete` and `find … -exec rm` (or `unlink`, `shred`, `truncate`) on history and log paths, not a delete made by a shell `find` runs (`find /var/log -exec sh -c 'rm "$1"' _ {} \;`): the paths reach that `rm` only as `$1`. | Variables aren't expanded, so the rule can't tell which files the inner shell is given. | Add an `ask` rule of your own for `find` with `-exec sh` on the paths that matter, or keep logs outside the agent's reach. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-D008` (the harness's transcripts), `TK-A027` (history and logs), `TK-A030` (package sources) and `TK-F015` (harness settings) read a relative write or delete target as it is typed, not from the folder an earlier `cd` or `pushd` moved into, nor from the one a shell tool's own working-directory argument names (Codex's `workdir`, Gemini CLI's `dir_path`, Cursor's `working_directory`): `cd ~/.codex/sessions && rm *.jsonl`, `cd ~/.claude/projects/x && : > s.jsonl` and `cd ~/.codex && rm -rf sessions` aren't denied, `cd /var/log && rm syslog` and `cd /etc/apt/sources.list.d && echo deb x > evil.list` aren't held, and `cd ~/.claude && echo '{}' > settings.json` isn't flagged. A relative target that still names the folder is matched (`cd ~ && rm -rf .claude/projects` is denied). | The shell parser doesn't follow `cd`: which folder a relative path lands in is known only when the command runs. | Run the agent in a sandbox that can't write outside its workspace ([Claude Code's](integrations/claude-code-sandbox.md), [Gemini CLI's](integrations/gemini-sandbox.md)), and on Linux import a kernel sensor's records ([Tetragon](integrations/tetragon.md)): each file write is signed into the run with its path, and `tracekit analyze --v2` flags one no call explains (`TK-X206`). | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| `TK-A024` and `TK-A025` read npm's and pip's ways of setting a package source; Yarn Berry's (`yarn config set npmRegistryServer <url>`, or `.yarnrc.yml` written by a file tool) isn't held. `TK-A025` reads a pip option's unique prefix (`--index-u`), not another client's abbreviated option (npm's `--reg`, gem's `--sou`), which those clients may take, and reads `config set` only as the first word after the options, so a global option's value before it (`pip --proxy http://p config set global.index-url …`) hides it. | Each package manager names its registry setting differently, and the rules name npm's, pip's, apt's and yum's. | If your agents use Yarn 2 or later, add `ask` rules of your own for `yarn config set npmRegistryServer` and writes to `.yarnrc.yml`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#packs-shipped) |
| The `payment` class doesn't extract `new_payee` yet. | Not built. | Match payees with your own rules. | [policy](policy-v2.md#classes-and-fields) |
| `unless` on a whole command line could exempt a different target. | The subject holds several targets. | Use `unless` only where the subject is one target. | [policy](policy-v2.md#what-a-rule-matches-the-subject) |
| v1 rules (`tracekit/policy/default.yaml`) match raw strings, so quoted text that mentions a risky command can trip them. The v1 `TK-D008` and `TK-D009` leave Claude Code's memory notes (a `.md` file directly in `.claude/projects/<project>/memory/`) writable. They match case as written, so on a file system that ignores case (macOS, Windows) `.ENV`, `~/.SSH/` or `RM -Rf ~` isn't matched as `.env`, `~/.ssh/` or `rm`. | The v1 engine doesn't parse commands, and the v1 policy is kept as it was. | Use the v2 signer's policy; add exceptions for known false positives. | [evaluation](evaluation.md#e3-policy-gate) |

## Provenance (1.1)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| **(1.1)** Provenance matches values verbatim: a value the model paraphrases, splits or re-encodes reads as a new value. | It is a tripwire on the commonest way injected instructions act, not information-flow control. | To prevent the flow, use information-flow control (CaMeL, FIDES) in the agent. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.1)** Only untrusted tools' results and observed inputs are indexed: a page fetched with a tool the policy doesn't list as untrusted (`curl` in a shell) isn't seen. | Results of other tools can echo the agent's own arguments, so they aren't counted either way. | List such tools under `untrusted`, or observe the content with `run.observe`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.1)** Only an input observed as trusted makes a value trusted: a value from your own data that also appears in untrusted content (a customer's address from the CRM that is also in their ticket) is held. | Counting other tool output as trusted could launder an injected value. | Observe the record you looked up as `trusted`, or answer the hold. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.1)** Whenever a run's index is not `complete`, `from: untrusted` rules fail closed: they match whenever their pattern does (the packs' `TK-P` rules ask). It is `unavailable` after a signer restart, `truncated` once a result carries more than 4096 values (or 1 MiB of text) or the run reaches 20,000, `partial` once an untrusted tool completes with a digest or a client's events skip a `client_seq` (an observe or a result lost in a signer outage), and `timeout` for a decision whose values took past its 1 s deadline. | The index lives in memory only and is bounded; a value it can't vouch for may be injected, and a lost event may have carried one. | Watch `provenance_state` and `provenance_reason` on decisions; split very long runs; expect more holds after a restart or an outage, and start the task again in a new run to end them. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.1)** Provenance needs the result itself: for a tool the policy lists as untrusted, `decide` answers `send_result` and the shipped adapters (Python, TypeScript, the Claude Code hook) send the result instead of its digest. A result over 512 KiB, or an adapter of your own that sends a digest, makes the run `partial`. | The signer can only index content it receives; it redacts and commits to it and keeps nothing in clear. | In your own adapter, send `result` itself when the decision says `send_result`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted), [privacy](privacy.md) |
| **(1.1)** `trust: "trusted"` on `observe` is the calling process's claim. Provenance protects against injected content, not against a malicious agent program, which can observe anything as trusted (or send digests) and get past `from` rules. | The signer can't tell who wrote content the caller supplies. | Observe the task from your application, not from the agent; use the other rules for a hostile agent. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.1)** Bare IPv6 addresses and integer IPs in text are not read as hosts (inside a URL they are). | Bare forms are ambiguous in prose. | Rely on the URL forms, or add rules of your own. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| A Browser Use navigation or an http GET with a query string (or, for http, a `params` argument) sends data to its host, so `TK-P003` holds one to a host that only a page named unless the task named it. An http GET that sends nothing but its URL's query isn't held when untrusted content gave that URL exactly as it stands (a search result's link with `?id=5`, followed as given; the index answers that only when it is complete), but is held with anything added or changed (`&d=…`, another value, a character after it) and a browser navigation is held as before. Choosing which of a page's links to follow can still signal a few bits to its author. Data in a URL's path or fragment isn't counted as sent, and neither is data in an http call's `headers` or `cookies`. | A query string is how a GET carries data to an attacker's host; telling an ID from a card number takes reading the value, and a link given verbatim holds only what its author put in it, unless a page reflects a URL that carries the agent's data back to it (a search page, an echo service, a comment the agent posted earlier). | Observe the task as `trusted`; to hold only typing and uploads, redefine `TK-P003` with `tool: '^browser_use:(input\|upload_file)$'`; a POST, a body or a `params` argument stays held. | [Browser Use](integrations/browser-use.md#the-browser-policy-pack) |
| The Browser Use adapter observes what the model read of each page (URL, title, tabs, text) up to 100,000 characters, from each browser state the session's `get_browser_state_summary` returns, which it wraps at the session's first action; the first step's state, made before that, comes from Browser Use's cached copy, which a tab closing before the first action empties. A link's target the model wasn't shown enters the index only once the agent is on that page. | Browser Use shows the model 40,000 characters of a page by default, and the adapter first sees the session at its first action; asking it for a fresh state would renumber the elements the step's actions refer to. | Keep `max_clickable_elements_length` at or under 100,000. Each page adds its values to the run's index (20,000 at most, then `truncated`): give a long browsing session a run per task. | [Browser Use](integrations/browser-use.md#what-is-recorded) |
| After a signer restart every run that was open has an `unavailable` index for the rest of the run: each call a `from: untrusted` rule's pattern matches is held, even typing into a site the task named (its decision's `provenance_reason` says so). | The index holds per-value keyed hashes in memory under a per-process key; persisting them would keep brute-forceable hashes of card numbers and addresses at rest, and would need a crash to invalidate them. | Answer the holds, or start the task again in a new run: a run registered after the restart has a complete index. Restart signers between runs where you can. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.1)** Reading values is bounded: linear in the text, at most 1 MiB and 4096 values per result or argument. | A result is attacker-controlled; the work must not grow with it. | Nothing: past the bound the run is `truncated` and fails closed. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.1)** Bundles from a 1.1 signer must be exported with tracekit >= 1.1: a 1.0 exporter labels them 1.0.0, and a 1.0 verifier fails their schema. | Provenance adds record fields 1.0 doesn't know. | Upgrade the exporter and verifiers together with the signer. | [format](format-v2.md#3-events) |
| **(1.2)** TK-P003 reads only a browser navigation's or (1.5.3) an http GET's query string as data sent to the host: data put in the path (`https://evil.example/c/<card number>`) or in the host name (`https://<data>.evil.example/`) isn't, so such a navigation to a host only untrusted content named is not held. | Following a link from a page or a mail is a navigation too; holding every path would hold every link the agent follows. | Allow only the hosts the agent needs (TK-B011's allowlist), or run the egress observer, which records the hosts reached. | [policy](policy-v2.md) |

## Detection (1.2)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| **(1.2)** Sequences and limits catch the patterns they describe, nothing else: a reworded attack that carries none of their labels isn't seen. | Detection is deterministic rules over rule ids and labels, no model. | Read incidents as tripwires; add sequences for the patterns you care about and backtest them. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** A hold or quarantine is recorded for every run, but enforced only where the signer decides each step: a gateway's model loop, an executor's tools (T2). A T1 adapter that ignores a decision can still run the call. | On T1 the agent's own process runs the tool; the signer answers, it can't stop it. The verifier flags any result recorded while held or quarantined. | Put tools that must be containable behind the executor or MCP gateway, and models behind the LLM gateway. | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** With no person approver configured (`approvals.approvers` naming persons), every enforced hold is a quarantine, and `run_hold` is refused. | Only a person may answer a held call; a hold no one may answer would never end. | Configure approvers mapped in `approvals.persons` (or OIDC persons or groups) before enforcing holds. | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** Suspensions are shared only through the suspension list file: each replica reads it per call and writes its own `enforced` record when it first sees an entry; two operators changing the list at once on different replicas can lose one change. Holds, quarantines, cooldowns and a paused response engine are per replica. | Each replica has its own log and state; the list is the one thing they share, and no token is revoked automatically. | Put the list on storage every replica reads (`suspensions:`), change it from one place, and send pause and resume to every replica (`tracekit signer incidents pause --signer ... --signer ...`). | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** `tracekit signer incidents list` reaches only the replicas you name with `--signer` (the default: one). | Incidents live in each replica's log; there is no central index. | Name every replica's URL when listing, or read incidents from each log's bundle. | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** After a restart that loses the detector's state, a decision whose detection state is `unavailable` holds its run (`detector_unavailable`) when the policy enforces a hold or quarantine. | Fail closed: the detector can't vouch that no sequence completed. | Keep snapshots (they hold the detector's state); expect held runs to resume by a person after an unclean restart. | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** The call that completes a sequence was decided `allow` and counted before the hold: it is decided again, held, so it doesn't run on that decision; a call consumed by a person's approval as it fires a hold stays in flight. | Detection reads the signed decision, so the decision exists before the incident. | Read `in_flight` on `incident.opened`: those calls may still complete. | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** The verifier recomputes only incidents of scope `run` whose cites are in their run, under the one policy that run was decided by; session, owner and principal incidents, operator incidents and runs decided under several policies are counted signer-asserted. | A bundle holds one run's records; other runs' records and earlier policies aren't in it. | Export with the signer's config so its policies are bundled; export the run-set to see the other runs of a scope. | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** Incident webhooks are delivered at least once (a receiver may see one twice) and a 4xx-refusing endpoint is skipped. A new webhook starts at the log's end, not its beginning; one whose saved cursor is unreadable sends every incident record of the log again. | The outbox is a cursor over the log that moves only once the endpoint took a batch; a refusal won't change by resending. | Deduplicate by incident id and record uid; fix the endpoint and read missed incidents from the log or `tracekit signer incidents list`. | [webhooks](webhooks.md) |
| **(1.2)** No trigger suspends an identity: `respond` holds or quarantines runs, and only an operator suspends (lint refuses `suspend:` in `respond`). | A run's owner is often an identity every conversation of a server shares, and content the agent reads can steer every trigger, so an automatic suspension could stop every user's agent. | Alert on incidents (`action: notify`) and suspend with `tracekit signer incidents suspend` after a look. | [incidents](incidents.md) |
| **(1.2)** The signer refuses to start when one `authorize` entry grants both `register_run` and a containment operator call. | An agent's identity must not resolve, resume or pause its own containment. | Give operators an identity of their own (a person's OIDC login or a separate uid or token). | [incidents](incidents.md#operators) |
| **(1.2)** `session` scope joins runs only through `parent_run`: a sequence split across runs registered without `parent` is two sessions. | The signer joins runs only on a link it can check (the parent's run token). | Register subagent runs with `parent`; use `owner` scope to join all runs of one identity. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** Windows use the signer's clock, and are fixed: an NTP step moves them, and a burst straddling two windows can reach up to twice `max` before a limit fires. | Records' `ts` is the only clock a verifier can recompute; fixed windows keep the work O(1) per call. | Keep the signer on NTP; set `max` at half the burst you must catch. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** A sum limit holds a call of a known class it counts without a non-negative integer amount and a three-letter currency code (`ask`, `TK-LIMIT-SUM`), unless its `respond` mode is `off`; approved, or of an unknown class, such a call isn't added to the total. | A total across currencies, of negative amounts or of an unreadable amount would mean nothing; an unmapped tool's arguments needn't be a payment's. | Send amounts as non-negative integers in minor units with their currency code; map payment tools to a class; approve held calls knowing the limit doesn't see them. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** Decisions say `detect.state: unavailable`, and steps and counts from before are missed, after a restart from a snapshot written without detection or under another policy, after a replay of decisions made under another policy, and for a run the detector never saw registered. | What came before such a snapshot is no longer read, and another policy's decisions carry its rule ids, not this one's. | Change a detection policy when runs are quiet; expect missed steps and counts until the longest window has passed after the restart or the last such decision, and backtest the store for them. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** A scope with no record for an hour (or the policy's longest window, if longer) starts over, open incidents included: a later match opens a new one. | State must not grow with every run and owner the signer has seen, and the reset follows the records' `ts`, so a backtest or verifier gets the same incidents. | Group incidents by trigger and scope when you read them. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** Only opened incidents count toward the caps: past 100 of one trigger in a run, 20 from one run in a minute, or 60 in a tenant in a minute, one `incident.capped` is written and the rest are not. An open incident is updated at most once per window of its trigger (a minute for one without). | A loop must not flood the log with incidents, and one run or trigger must not use up another's. The share is per run, so an identity with several runs can use up more of the tenant's 60, and another agent's incident that minute is only `incident.capped`. | Read the run's decisions (their `detect` field) or backtest the policy to see the rest; keep `open_runs` quotas tight for agent identities. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** The server pack's limits were checked on small shipped runs only, and `TK-Q001`'s window was not backtested. | Those are the runs that ship, and none of them reads a secret or sends anything. | Run the pack in shadow, backtest your own store (`tracekit policy backtest`) and set `max` above your busiest minute. | [policy](policy-v2.md#choosing-thresholds) |
| **(1.2)** One call whose own names match consecutive steps takes them all, each name once (a command that reads a secret and sends it completes `[secrets_access, egress]`); a step matched only through `*` or an unknown class takes a call one step. | An unmapped tool matches every step; letting it chain would complete any sequence in one call. | Give each step its own label; a sequence that repeats a label needs that many calls. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** `egress` (`TK-F012`, `TK-F013`, `TK-F024`) is a URL a call acts on, a network command or an upload tool's file; `secrets_access` (`TK-F005`) is a call that acts on a secrets file's name, writes included (a lone env template path, such as `.env.example`, excepted). Mail, payments, SQL, a program that opens its own connection, and content that holds a secret without such a name are neither. | The labels read what the signer can see in the arguments, deterministically. | Label your own tools that send data or read secrets (`label: egress` / `secrets_access` on a flag rule) to bring them into `TK-Q001`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** A step counts when its call is decided (`allow`, `flag`) or its approval consumed, whether or not the call then runs; a call that runs after a deny or an unapproved ask never counts as a step. | Detection reads decisions, which the signer has before a call runs; a call run against its decision is `executed_against_policy`. | Keep the server pack's `executed_against_policy` trigger on (shadow, notify), and read those incidents alongside sequences. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** A decision retried under a new `request_id` is a new decision and counts again. | Each decide writes its own signed decision; remembering every counted call would grow the detector's state with calls. It can only over-count. | Retry with the same `request_id` (the signer answers it from its cache); allow for client retries when you set `max`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** An incident of a `session`, `owner` or `principal` scope is opened in the run whose record fired it; its `incident.updated` records are written in the runs whose records matched later. | Every incident record is written in the run of the record that fired it, so it lands in that run's hash chain. | Follow an incident by its `incident_id` across the runs of its scope. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** `backtest` reads the rule ids decisions recorded: for decisions made under another policy, rules and labels the new one adds or changes are not applied. | Arguments are not stored, so a decision can't be made again. | Heed its warning (the count of such decisions); backtest changes to sequences, limits and `respond`, and measure new rules by running the policy in shadow. | [policy](policy-v2.md#detection-sequences-limits-and-respond) |
| **(1.2)** A bundle with `detect` fields or incident records needs a 1.2 verifier. | They are format 1.2 additions. | Upgrade verifiers with the signer. | [format](format-v2.md#3-events) |

## Content vault (1.1)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| **(1.1)** With `content.retain` on, whoever holds `keys/content.key` and the store can read retained content. | The signer must decrypt it to answer `content_get`. | Keep the keys dir to the signer's user (`D-CONTENT-VAULT`), keep retention short, erase runs you no longer need. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.1)** The content master key is a file key (`keys/content.key`), not wrapped by KMS, even when the log key is in AWS KMS. | The KMS log key is an Ed25519 signing key, which can't wrap keys. | Protect the data volume as you do the other file keys; back the key up with them. | [deploy](deploy.md#keeping-content-for-investigations) |
| **(1.1)** `keys/content.key` has no rotation path: replacing it makes every run already in the vault unreadable (`content_get` answers `unavailable` for them). | Each run's key is wrapped by the master key, and nothing re-wraps them. | Keep the key for as long as you keep content; replace it only to give up everything the vault holds. | [deploy](deploy.md#keeping-content-for-investigations) |
| **(1.1)** Erasure (and retention) reaches the live store only: a backup, WAL archive or replica taken earlier still holds the run's wrapped key and blobs, and opens with `keys/content.key`. | The signer can't delete copies it doesn't hold. | Keep backups and archives shorter than `retention_days`, or rotate `content.key` to cut off older copies, which today also makes every run still in the vault unreadable (no re-wrapping yet). | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.1)** A decision's arguments are kept redacted: if redaction changed them, they no longer open `args_commitment` (`content_get` marks them `redacted`, without a salt). The same holds for a model exchange redaction changed whose tool uses carry `args_commitment`: no salt, so its `content_digest` can't be checked either. | Nothing unredacted is written; the policy decided on the unredacted arguments. | Use the tool result and the model exchange, which open their commitments, for what the call did. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.1)** Content over `max_record_bytes` isn't kept (`too_large`), and one `content_get` answer must fit the 1 MiB frame, so an item near 1 MiB can't be read. | The RPC answers in one frame. | Ask for fewer seqs at a time (the refusal says so); keep `max_record_bytes` well under 1 MiB if you need every item back. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.1)** The gateway asks the signer once whether the vault is on, needs the `status` grant, and sends no body over half a frame (`not_retained`). | One status call per gateway process; the frame bounds a request. | Grant the gateway `status`; restart it after turning the vault on or off. | [gateway](gateway.md#configure) |
| **(1.1)** Erasing a run erases the replay runs (`tracekit why`) the vault holds for it when it runs; a replay run that is still being written as the run is erased can keep what it records after that. Finding them reads the first record of every run with content in the vault. | Replay runs copy the run's content into their own requests; the store does not index runs by what they replay. | Erase a run once no investigation of it is running. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.2.2)** The dev signer keeps each call's redacted arguments and results for 7 days in its data dir, readable by your user, so the dev viewer can show them. | The viewer needs the content, and the dev signer runs as you: you hold every dev key already. | `TRACEKIT_DEV_CONTENT=0` before the dev signer starts keeps nothing (the viewer then shows rule reasons only). | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.1)** Retention counts from the first item kept for a run, not per record. | A run's items share one key, erased at once. | Pick `retention_days` for your longest runs. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.1)** `content_get` and `content_erase` reach runs of the caller's own tenant only. | The tenant comes from the caller's identity. | Give each tenant's investigator its own identity. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| **(1.1)** On an existing Postgres database, `tracekit signer migrate` adds the vault's tables but grants the signer's role nothing on them: until you do, items read back `not_retained`. | `migrate` runs the schema only; grants are applied by the init script or by hand. | Apply `tracekit.storage.postgres.GRANTS` to the signer's role after migrating (the signer's log line says so). | [deploy](deploy.md#keeping-content-for-investigations) |
| **(1.1)** An item is stored just after its record: a crash or storage error in between leaves it `not_retained`, and a model body that isn't strict JSON (an integer past 2^53, a non-finite number, a lone surrogate) is recorded without it (the gateway sends none: `not_retained`). | The record is never held back for its content. | Treat `not_retained` as missing evidence, not as an empty value. | [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |

## Approvals

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| On the dev signer (no `signer.yaml`) the requester can answer its own approval. A signer started from `signer.yaml` refuses that unless `approvals: {self_approval: allow}`, and without an `approvals` section has no approvers at all. The shipped packs deny the agent's own `tracekit approve` or `reject` and `tracekit approvals approve` or `reject` (`TK-D012`), also through the wrappers and runners the shell parser reads (`env`, `sh -c`, `eval`, `uvx`, `uv tool run`, `uv run`, `pipx run`, `hatch run`, `conda run`, …) and as `python -m tracekit` (through a module runner too, `-m runpy`, `-m trace --module`, `-m cProfile -m`, or a path to the entry script, `python3 ~/.local/bin/tracekit`), as a copy or link of the entry point the same call makes and runs under another name (`cp "$(command -v tracekit)" /tmp/tk; /tmp/tk …`), and as a command git runs (`git -c core.pager=…`, `GIT_EDITOR=…`, `rebase -x`, `bisect run`, `submodule foreach`). An action the parser can't read is denied too (a positional parameter it doesn't follow into `eval` or `sh -c` or past `set --`, an indirect expansion such as `${!#}`, a substitution's output, a pattern, an expansion with a word, a variable alone as the action that the line gives no value there, `$X`, `$_`, `$OPTARG`): it may be `approve`. After an approvals option such a word anywhere denies, as the joined words can't tell a `--signer` value with a space in it (`--signer "/tmp/a list" "$1"`) from the action, so an approvals command with a variable socket path and such a word is denied too (`--signer "$1" list`). They don't deny code that calls the signer's `approval_decide` itself (`python -c`, a script, a copy or link of the entry script an earlier call made, run under another name), an action read from stdin (`xargs tracekit`) or held after approvals' options in a named variable the parser doesn't know (`--signer /tmp/s "$a"`) or made from one it knows by an operator other than a default, alternate or replacement (`D=xapprove; tracekit ${D#x} ID`), an unquoted `--signer` value bash splits into a value and an action, a runner's own options before its subcommand (`uv -q run`), or a runner the parser doesn't name (`hatch env run`). | The dev signer has no separate approver; a policy rule reads commands, not what a program does. | Configure `approvals: {approvers: [...]}` (system mode does, and refuses the agent's own answer); self-approved answers cap assurance at `dev` and show `approvals: self`. Give `--signer` a literal path, or have a person run an approvals command that needs a variable one. | [approvals](approvals.md#who-may-answer) |
| A T1 approval binds the arguments the agent reported, and the agent's process runs the call. | The adapter runs in the agent. | Use `approval: {executor: t2}` and route the call through the [tool executor](executor.md) or the [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md), which run the approved arguments outside the agent. Adapters run the approved arguments too, but in the agent's process. | [approvals](approvals.md#tiers-t1-and-t2-executors) |
| Approvers answering through the web viewer or Slack are vouched for by the bridge (`attested: false`). | The signer authenticates the bridge, not the person. | Grant `approval_decide_on_behalf` only to bridges you run; read `via` on each approval. | [approvals](approvals.md#web-approvals-and-passkeys), [approvals](approvals.md#slack) |
| The signer takes the bridge's word for who registers a passkey, and keeps a person's first passkey. | It has no proof of its own of the person behind a session. | Have each approver register right after onboarding; check `passkeys.json` against the approver list. | [approvals](approvals.md#web-approvals-and-passkeys) |
| Break-glass answers aren't available through Slack. | Break-glass needs a reason and a direct identity. | Answer break-glass approvals from the CLI. | [approvals](approvals.md#slack) |
| After a restart the Slack bridge posts the approvals still pending again. | It keeps which messages it posted in memory. | Expect duplicate messages after a restart. | [approvals](approvals.md#slack) |
| An approval expires one hour after it was requested. | Nothing should consume a stale approval. | Answer within the hour, or request again. | [approvals](approvals.md#expiry-and-abandon) |
| **(1.5.1)** A call a coding-agent hook holds (Claude Code, Codex, Cursor, Gemini CLI, Qwen Code, OpenCode, goose, Droid, Antigravity CLI) is blocked at once: it runs only when the agent sends the same call again after a person approved it, within the approval's hour. | No harness shows a hook's output while the hook runs, so a hook that waited for an answer waited for a person who couldn't know (in 1.5.0 every hold waited 8 minutes, then was blocked). | Approve with the command the block names, then ask the agent to try the same call again, with exactly the same arguments (in Claude Code a Bash call's `description` may differ: a retry that differs only there from the session's last 32 held calls of the same command runs under the newest approval of them, recorded with the approved arguments, unless one of them was rejected or couldn't be read; it is held again when the signer redacted a secret-looking string in what it shows the approver, or a remote signer's `authorize` doesn't give the hook's identity `approval_get`, with which the hook reads the approved arguments). Where Slack, the viewer or an agent approver answers during a wait, set `TRACEKIT_APPROVAL_WAIT` (seconds) in the agent's environment: the hook waits up to about 480 s, as it answers within 540 s and keeps 60 s for the answer and the approval's use. | [held calls](coding-agents.md#held-calls-approve-then-retry) |
| **(1.5.1)** Cursor, Gemini CLI, Antigravity CLI, Codex and Droid get a block message in 500 characters: a longer one (most holds, as the message's lines say why, what to do instead, the look, approve and reject commands and what the agent does) ends with `…`. The first line (the approval id and rule ids) comes first, then the `Person:` line with the commands, then the reasons and the agent's line, cut from their end. A long `--signer` path in those commands (past about 40 characters with one rule id) pushes the reject command past the cut. With `TRACEKIT_APPROVAL_WAIT`, the wait's outcome comes first (`NOT APPROVED, nothing ran: … rejected by …`, or the hold), then the waiting line. | The hook cuts what it returns to the harness to that length. | Approve or reject with the commands it keeps (`tracekit approvals list` names every held call); the rule id names the rule, whose whole reason and remedy are in the policy (`tracekit policy compile <file>` prints them). | [held calls](coding-agents.md#held-calls-approve-then-retry) |
| **(1.5.1)** The hook reads `TRACEKIT_APPROVAL_WAIT` from the agent's environment, which the agent can change: it can make its own held calls wait (up to about 480 s) or block at once. The hook can't tell whether a Slack bridge, the viewer or an agent approver is there to answer. | The wait changes only when a held call is blocked, never whether it runs: the signer decides that. Which bridges run is the signer's configuration, which the hook doesn't read. | Set it in the harness's own settings (Claude Code: `settings.json` `env`) where someone answers during the wait. | [held calls](coding-agents.md#held-calls-approve-then-retry) |
| **(1.5.1)** An approved approval is used by the next call of the same run, tool and arguments that asks for one, whichever call that is: a second identical call asked while the first still waits to consume it (an agent approval's veto delay, a slow adapter) runs in its place, and the first is refused. A call sent again before anyone answered gets a new approval each time, which stays `requested` until it expires. | A retry carries a new tool call id: matching the approval on run, tool, arguments, policy and rules is what lets it run, and one approval runs one call. | Expect one call per approval. Approve any one of a call's approvals (the next retry runs) and let the others expire, or reject them. | [approve, then retry](approvals.md#approve-then-retry) |
| **(1.5.1)** A retry uses an earlier call's T2 approval (`executor: t2`) only when its consume names it (`approval_id_hint`): without the hint it is refused `TK-APPROVAL-REQUIRED`. | The signer reads its copy of the approved arguments, which a T2 consume returns, before the writer runs, for the approval it is told. | Pass the `approval_id` that `approval_request` returned, as the shipped hooks do. | [approve, then retry](approvals.md#approve-then-retry) |
| An MCP call is named by the server's own name (`mcp:<server>/<tool>`); an mcp 1.x session whose `initialize()` ran before `tracekit.instrument()`, or one wrapped by hand without `server=`, is recorded as `mcp:unknown/<tool>`, with a warning. | The name comes from the server's initialize result, which an mcp 1.x session doesn't keep. | Call `tracekit.instrument()` before any session initializes, or pass `TracekitSession(..., server="<name>")`; write rules for server-named tools knowing the server picks its name. | [MCP](integrations/mcp.md#not-covered) |
| An adapter without saved state (MCP, Browser Use, the Claude Agent SDK hook) holds a call while waiting; the wait doesn't survive the process, and a long wait ends in a denial. | There is no resumable state to carry the approval. | Answer within the adapter's wait; use a framework with saved state for long waits. | [MCP](integrations/mcp.md#not-covered), [Claude Agent SDK](integrations/claude-agent-sdk.md#not-covered) |
| MCP: an input-required call (MCP 2.3) whose rounds are sent from different asyncio tasks (a web app that keeps the `request_state` and sends the next round in its next HTTP request) is decided again in each round, and each round of an allowed call before its last stays decided and never completed: a signed `decided_not_completed` gap at run close, one per round, counted toward the run's 100 such gaps (past them, one gap counts the rest). | A round continues only the call its own task left waiting, so two calls never share a decision, whatever `request_state` a server returns to both; a round sent from another task can't be told from a new call that reuses the state. | Drive all of a call's rounds in one task, as `mcp.Client.call_tool` does; read such a gap next to the later round with the same tool and arguments. | [MCP](integrations/mcp.md#not-covered) |
| MCP: the input responses an input-required call (MCP 2.3) sends the server are neither decided nor recorded: what a person answered an elicitation, a sampling callback's model output and the client's roots (or the `input_responses` a caller passes) go with the next round, under the first round's decision when the call is allowed. A round is decided on its tool and arguments alone, `complete` commits to those and the result, and only the server's input requests are recorded (untrusted observed input). | `TracekitSession` decides `call_tool` and `get_prompt` on their tool and arguments, and passes their other arguments (`input_responses`, `request_state`) to the session as given. | Check what leaves in your own sampling, elicitation and roots callbacks, which run in your code before the answer is sent; give a client of a server you don't trust none of them, so mcp answers that it doesn't support them. | [MCP](integrations/mcp.md#not-covered) |
| LangGraph: code that catches exceptions around a gated `ToolNode` (a `try`/`except Exception` in a node that invokes it, a middleware outside `TracekitMiddleware` that turns exceptions into messages) ends an `ask`'s pause: the model gets an error, the call doesn't run, and its approval expires. The node's own `handle_tool_errors` and `.with_fallbacks()` let the pause through. | LangGraph pauses by raising `GraphInterrupt` out of the node; code that catches it ends the pause before LangGraph sees it. | Re-raise `langgraph.errors.GraphBubbleUp` before any broad `except`, or put the error handling in the ToolNode (`handle_tool_errors`, `.with_fallbacks()`). | [LangGraph](quickstarts/langchain.md#approvals-wire-it-by-hand) |
| LangGraph: when one model message has more than one call that asks and one `ToolNode` runs them all, as when `tools_condition` routes the message to it (a `tracekit_tool_node`, or a `ToolNode` that `tracekit.instrument()` wired), a resume can reach a call other than the one its pause showed, under `invoke` and `ainvoke` alike. Any of those calls may then be denied (`TK-APPROVAL-UNBOUND`) and never run, even after its own pause was approved. A pause may repeat one already approved, or show a call that already ran under a new approval, which runs it again once approved. A call that ran is gated again on each later resume, so the model may get a denial for a call that ran. An allowed call in the same message may run again on each resume. No asking call runs without an approval a person gave for it. | LangGraph runs the node again from its start on each resume and pairs resume values with the node's `interrupt()` calls by the order they are reached, which the node's parallel calls (threads under `invoke`, tasks under `ainvoke`) reach in no fixed order. | Give each call a task of its own: build the agent with `create_agent`, or route each call to the node with its own `Send("tools", [call])`, and resume every pending pause by its interrupt id (`Command(resume={i.id: {"approval_id": i.value["tracekit"]["approval_id"]} for i in out["__interrupt__"]})`). Or have the model make one call per message (`bind_tools(..., parallel_tool_calls=False)` on a chat model that takes it). Read the run's `tool.result` records, not the messages the model got, for what ran. | [LangChain](integrations/langchain.md#decisions) |
| A Browser Use action waits for an approval at most five sixths of Browser Use's action timeout (150 s of 180 s); then the approval is withdrawn and the action refused. | Browser Use cancels an action that outlives its timeout, approval wait included, with a misleading "dead CDP WebSocket" error. | Answer within the wait, or raise `BROWSER_USE_ACTION_TIMEOUT_S` (and the Agent's `step_timeout`) to give people longer. | [Browser Use](integrations/browser-use.md#decisions) |
| OpenAI Agents' `LocalShellTool` and `ApplyPatchTool` can't wait for an approval: an `ask` for them is refused. | The SDK gives no approval hook for them. | Allow or deny them by policy. | [OpenAI Agents](integrations/openai-agents.md#not-covered) |
| v1 approvals name the held call, not its exact arguments. | v1 approvals predate argument binding. | Use the v2 signer, which binds approvals to the exact arguments. | [laptop threat model](threat-model-laptop.md#open-problems) |
| **(1.2)** The agent-approvals kill switch (`approval_agents`) is signer-wide: switching it off stops and revokes agent approvals for every tenant. | It protects the signer from a misbehaving reviewer, which may serve several tenants. | Grant `approval_agents` only to the signer's operators, not to one tenant's. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** An agent approver's bounds bind the approval, not the execution, unless the rule has `executor: t2`. | Under T1 the agent's own process runs the call with the arguments it holds. | Put `executor: t2` on agent rules, above all on money-moving ones (`tracekit policy lint` warns), and run held calls in a worker that executes what `approval_consume` returns. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** A reviewer agent's judgment is model output: within the bounds, the requesting agent can steer it. | It reads arguments the requesting agent chose. | Set bounds you would accept every call inside of; start in `mode: shadow` and compare its suggestions with your people's answers. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** The untrusted-value floor relies on an honest adapter's trust labels: content the adapter reports as trusted (or never reports) does not send a call to people. | `observe`'s `trust` is the caller's claim, and only untrusted tools' results are indexed. | List every tool whose results come from outside in the policy's `untrusted`, and observe outside content as untrusted. | [policy](policy-v2.md#provenance-untrusted-and-from-untrusted) |
| **(1.2)** Budgets and review caps are counted per signer: replicas that each write their own log multiply them. | Each signer counts the agent approvals in its own records. | Divide budgets by the number of replicas, or route a tenant's runs to one replica (`route`). | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** An agent approval under a `sum` budget records the value it took (`spent`) in the clear. | The budget is rebuilt from the signed records, which otherwise hold commitments only. | Use `sum` on amounts you are fine to see in a bundle; use `count` alone otherwise. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** Anti-shopping matches the same arguments or the same tool call id: a call with one argument changed is a new call an agent may answer. | Any other match would need a notion of "near" arguments. | Rely on the bounds and budgets; a run gets at most 10 agent reviews and a tenant 1000 an hour, then people answer. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** After a signer restart, calls of runs registered before it go to people. | The provenance index lives in memory, so their provenance is `unavailable`. | Expect more person answers right after a restart; new runs are not affected. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** The verifiers can't check an agent approval's argument bounds. | The records hold the arguments only as salted commitments. | Read the `approvals by agents` line as "bounds the signer checked"; to audit one, `tracekit signer reveal --record <seq>` and recompute its commitment from the arguments. | [verdicts](verdicts.md#approvals-in-the-report), [privacy](privacy.md#the-v2-signer) |
| **(1.2)** Without the policy snapshot of the decision's `policy_hash` in the bundle, the verifiers don't check that the rules permitted agents (a warning: `agent approval bounds unchecked`). | `tracekit export --v2` bundles the policy an agent approval's decision cites from the signer's `<data_dir>/policies`, where the signer writes each policy it starts with; an export with another data dir (another replica, a copied store) or after that file was removed has none. | Export with the signer's config; keep `<data_dir>/policies` with the store, or check the old policy file's hash by hand. | [verdicts](verdicts.md#approvals-in-the-report) |
| **(1.2)** An approval an agent's answer can decide (enforce mode) reaches people (Slack, the desk's "for people") only once it is escalated: with no agent answering, after `agent_timeout_s` (default 600 s). | The signer gives the agent approver that long to answer first. | Keep the reviewer running on every replica; lower `approvals.agent_timeout_s` for faster handover; `approval_agents {enabled: false}` hands every pending one to people at once. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** The reference reviewer sends the redacted arguments of the tenants it serves to a model provider. | Judging a call means reading it. | List in its `tenants` only tenants that accept this; see [privacy](privacy.md#what-leaves-the-machine). | [approvals](approvals.md#the-reference-reviewer) |
| **(1.2)** The reference reviewer keeps which approvals it answered in memory: after a restart it answers a shadow-mode approval still pending again (a second `approval.suggested`). | Shadow answers leave the approval listed for agents. | Count suggestions per approval id when comparing; enforce-mode answers are not affected (the signer refuses a second one). | [approvals](approvals.md#the-reference-reviewer) |
| **(1.2)** `tracekit reviewer eval` judges views without arguments. | Past arguments are in no record (salted commitments) and the signer deletes its copy once an approval ends. | Treat eval as a first check of the prompt and the rules; measure on live traffic with `mode: shadow`, where the view is complete. | [approvals](approvals.md#the-reference-reviewer) |
| **(1.2)** Each review registers a run of its own on the signer (the reviewer's run identity), one per approval. | The model call is evidence too, linked to the approval it judged. | Size the signer's run quotas for one extra run per agent-answered approval. | [approvals](approvals.md#the-reference-reviewer) |
| **(1.2)** An agent's approval runs only within `agent_consume_s` (and after `veto_s`); an adapter that waits longer gets `TK-APPROVAL-EXPIRED`. | An unconsumed agent approval should not wait for a better moment. | Consume right after approval; raise `approvals.agent_consume_s` for slow adapters. | [approvals](approvals.md#agent-approvers) |
| **(1.2)** The verifiers check who answered an approval and that an agent's answer stays within its bounds, not the order of the approval's records: an agent answer after `approval.expired` or after another agent's rejection, a consume after `approval.revoked`, or a consume outside `not_before`/`consume_by` still verifies. | The signer enforces each of these when it happens; the offline check of their order is not built yet. | Read an approval's records in order (`tracekit approvals show`, the viewer) when a veto or an expiry matters. | [approvals](approvals.md) |
| `tracekit approvals show` doesn't say when the call was requested, or its record's seq, and gives a held call's why and what to do instead only from a policy on this machine whose hash the approval records: a shipped pack, or one the dev signer kept. With a signer whose policy file is elsewhere (system mode, a remote signer) it names the policy's hash instead. | `approval_get` returns neither time nor seq, and returning them is a new RPC version; the hold's reason is in the decision the hook got, not in the approval. | `tracekit view --approval ID` (with the viewer's `--config` or `--data-dir`) opens the held call's record, with its time; the block message the agent got has the why and the Instead step; `tracekit policy compile <file>` prints a policy's reasons. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#held-calls-approve-then-retry) |

## Custody (1.2)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| **(1.2)** An approval the executor or MCP gateway stopped waiting for stays `requested` until it expires (an hour): a person may still approve it, and it runs only if the agent sends the same call again through the same gateway (the same `tool_call_id`, or **(1.5.1)** a new one with the same tool and arguments: [approve, then retry](approvals.md#approve-then-retry)). | The gateway holds the call, and `approval_abandon` is the agent's call, which may not touch a held one. | Reject approvals whose caller has given up (the viewer shows the request's age); keep `approvals.ttl_s` short where agents retry. | [executor](executor.md) |
| Custody covers only the actions routed through the tool executor, the MCP gateway, the LLM gateway and the A2A gateway. | An agent that holds a credential, or can reach an API itself, can act around them. | Keep the credentials only in those services and fence the agents' egress (`agentEgress` in the central chart). | [executor](executor.md#limits), [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md#limits), [A2A gateway](a2a.md#limits), [Kubernetes](deploy-kubernetes.md#custody) |
| **(1.5)** The A2A gateway does not see push notifications an A2A server sends outside it. | The receiver opens that connection to the URL in a message's configuration; the gateway sees only requests sent through it. | Read state through `message/stream` or `tasks/get` (the gateway refuses a message carrying a push notification config) and fence receiver egress. | [A2A gateway](a2a.md#limits) |
| **(1.5)** The A2A gateway relays only `message/send`, `message/stream` and `tasks/get`. | Each method needs its own decision and record. | Cancel or resubscribe directly only if unrecorded access is acceptable, or request support for the method. | [A2A gateway](a2a.md#calling-it) |
| **(1.5)** A cut A2A stream has no `tool.result`; its `held_call_unresolved` gap is written only at run end. | The gateway cannot know how the task continued, and the signer finalises open gateway calls at run finalisation. | Read a final run and use `tasks/get` through the gateway for state. | [A2A gateway](a2a.md#what-is-recorded) |
| **(1.5)** The A2A gateway reads each Agent Card once, at start. | Re-reading on every message adds a round trip and would let a card change mid-run. | Restart the gateway when a card changes. | [A2A gateway](a2a.md#limits) |
| **(1.5)** A2A messages name no skill; rules see the skills the card offers. | The protocol has no requested-skill field. | Allow-list `skills` and `route`. | [A2A gateway](a2a.md#policy) |
| **(1.5)** The A2A gateway retains at most 1,024 task and context ids per caller and 1,024 callers. | An evicted id cannot be read or continued through the gateway. | Use the task before the bound is exceeded, or retain its state elsewhere. | [A2A gateway](a2a.md#limits) |
| **(1.5)** The A2A gateway checks task ownership only for `tasks/get`'s `id` and messages' `taskId`, `contextId` and `referenceTaskIds`; it forwards free-form `params.metadata` unchanged. | Metadata is outside the gateway's task-reference check. | Do not put a receiver that reads task references from metadata behind the gateway, or deny it with an A2A rule such as `field: target`, `pattern: '"metadata":.*"(taskId\|contextId\|referenceTaskIds)":'`. | [A2A gateway](a2a.md#limits) |
| **(1.5)** A transfer is recorded only when code calls `run.transfer` or `tracekit.integrations.c2c.fuse`. | A direct in-process hand-off crosses no capture layer. | Route every transfer through one of those calls. | [latent transfers](c2c.md#limits) |
| **(1.5)** Transfer fields are the caller's claims or measurements as marked. | The payload remains in the calling process; the signer cannot check the claimed or measured values. | Pin route and measured weights in policy; retain a copy where appropriate. | [latent transfers](c2c.md#runtransfer) |
| **(1.5)** Transfers are Python-only while experimental; the TypeScript SDK has no `transfer`. | The first integration follows Python C2C research code. | Record transfers with Python `RunHandle.transfer` or `tracekit.integrations.c2c`. | [latent transfers](c2c.md#limits) |
| The agents' egress NetworkPolicy is off by default, and enforced only by a CNI that enforces egress policies. | Turning it on cuts every other connection the agents make; some CNIs ignore egress rules. | Set `agentEgress.enabled` once the agents need nothing else, on a CNI that enforces egress (Calico, Cilium, ...); test it from an agent pod. | [Kubernetes](deploy-kubernetes.md#custody) |

| The events a gateway (the executor, the MCP gateway, the LLM gateway, the OpenShell middleware, the `ext_mcp` processor) writes for an agent count against the gateway's rate for that agent (`limits.events_per_s`, `burst`), its approval waits against its `concurrent_waits` for that agent, and the runs it registers for one (the OpenShell middleware's, one per sandbox) against its `open_runs` for that one; the result of a call it decided counts against nothing. A gateway's total is the per-agent limit times the agents it serves, but for open runs at most `limits.gateway_open_runs` (10,000) of its own and those it registered for all its agents together **(1.4)**; a suspended agent gets no run through a gateway either. | So an agent can't spend the budget its service's records need, nor one agent what the service's other agents need, and a call's result is never refused for rate once its decision was taken. The agent a gateway names is its word, so a cap of its own keeps a gateway naming made-up agents from opening runs without end. | Size `limits` for one agent's traffic through a gateway, and `gateway_open_runs` for the runs one gateway keeps open at once; list only gateways you run under `gateways`. | [executor](executor.md#what-is-recorded) |
| A call the executor or the MCP gateway decided is theirs, for the identity they decided it for: the agent's own `decide`, `approval_request`, `approval_consume`, `approval_abandon` and `complete` on its `tool_call_id`, or the service's for another identity on the run, are refused (`forbidden`), and the executor refuses a `tool_call_id` the agent decided itself. An approval of such a call can't be abandoned; it expires. | Otherwise the agent, holding its run token, could record a forged result or ask for the approval in its own words for a call made with credentials it doesn't hold. | Leave an unanswered approval to expire. The adapters leave the tools the executor helpers build to the executor (the Claude Agent SDK hooks: in Python, the actions of the server `executor_tools.claude_agent_sdk` built, by exact name; in TypeScript, the `executorActions` you name, of the in-process server you put under `tracekit-executor`; in both, when you hand the hooks the same `mcp_servers`); keep a tool you build yourself that calls the executor out of the adapters (in TypeScript, out of `executorActions`). | [executor](executor.md#what-is-recorded), [approvals](approvals.md#tiers-t1-and-t2-executors) |

A WebSocket upgrade is decided, but its call never completes, so the run ends with a `held_call_unresolved` gap for it. | OpenShell selects middleware by destination host and passes it only the HTTP it inspects; the middleware doesn't take WebSocket messages, and OpenShell runs no response middleware on an upgrade. | List every host the sandbox's network policy allows under the entry's `endpoints.include`, keep `on_error: fail_closed`, and turn on the OCSF import (`ocsf`) to record OpenShell's own blocks. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |

| At most 64 responses stream through the OpenShell middleware at once, shared by every sandbox: one sandbox's slow downloads can hold all 64 (OpenShell's deadline is per exchange, not per stream). One more is refused (`RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`), which OpenShell handles by the entry's `on_error` (`fail_closed`: the sandbox doesn't get that response), and its call ends as a `held_call_unresolved` gap. | Each open response stream holds a thread; the threads kept for request evaluations must stay free, or every sandbox's requests would wait. | Keep hosts the sandboxes download large or slow responses from out of the middleware's `endpoints` (another policy entry); the middleware prints each refused response on stderr. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |

Replicas behind one endpoint each register their own run per sandbox, and with `ocsf` replicas under one signer identity stop each other's OCSF import (the signer refuses the process another took over from, `conflict`). | The run tokens never leave the process. | Restart it rarely and run one replica per endpoint; read a sandbox's runs together (their ids start `openshell-<sandbox id>-`). | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#what-tracekit-records) |
| The OCSF import's calls count against the middleware's own event rate (`limits.events_per_s`, `burst`: 200 a second by default), one budget for every sandbox's events, not one per sandbox as its calls have: around 100 busy sandboxes spend it, and then OpenShell's batches are refused (`UNAVAILABLE`) and sent again, and those it gives up on become its gap records. | The import is one observer, the middleware's identity; its effects are attributed to the sandboxes' runs only by their run tokens. | Raise the middleware identity's `limits` in `signer.yaml` with the number of sandboxes; read a run's `observer_lost` gaps for what was dropped. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#openshells-ocsf-events) |

OpenShell reports its sandbox's entrypoint, not each process the agent starts. | Tracekit's records have no form for those classes yet; OpenShell's process events cover only what it starts. | Keep OpenShell's own OCSF JSONL output (`[openshell.gateway.ocsf_log]`, `ocsf_json_enabled`) for the rest; run a [kernel sensor](integrations/tetragon.md) to see every process. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |
| OpenShell's OCSF export is best-effort: it drops events when its queue is full or a batch fails four times (its next batch reports how many, which becomes a signed gap), and events lost while it shuts down get no report. A sandbox whose export stops is noticed only once the middleware has decided one of its requests, 60 s later. | OpenShell counts its own drops but numbers no events, so a loss it doesn't report can only be inferred from silence; every request the middleware decides is one event OpenShell should send. | Keep the middleware and the signer reachable from every supervisor; read a run's `observer_lost` gaps before trusting the absence of a block. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#openshells-ocsf-events) |
| The OCSF import covers only the runs the middleware registered (the sandboxes'): when the middleware stops or restarts, each of those still open gets an `observer_lost` gap; other runs of the tenant get none. An event for a sandbox whose run the signer already closed (`idle_s`) is an `effect_outside_run` gap in the tenant's run `tracekit/signer`, until the sandbox's next request opens a new run. | The import names the middleware's identity as its one caller (`callers`), so it covers that identity's runs alone; the middleware learns that a run closed only from its next call for that sandbox. | Set `idle_s` above the sandboxes' quiet periods. | [OpenShell](integrations/openshell.md#openshells-ocsf-events) |
| Behind the MCP gateway, Docker's MCP Gateway with its dynamic tools on (`mcp-find`, `mcp-add`, `mcp-exec`, `code-mode` and others) adds servers and runs other tools from inside one call, which Tracekit decides as that one call. So does a coding agent's hook when Docker's gateway is the agent's own MCP server: `mcp-exec` is decided by its own name, so a rule for the tool it runs (`TK-A044`'s `send_money`) doesn't apply. | Docker's gateway makes those calls itself; the MCP gateway and the hooks decide only the call they see. | Start each Docker upstream with `--servers=<server>`, which turns them off (or `docker mcp feature disable dynamic-tools`). | [Docker MCP Gateway](integrations/docker-mcp-gateway.md#setup) |
| Every server behind one `docker mcp gateway run` upstream is decided under that upstream's name (`mcp:<upstream>/<tool>`), so rules keyed on the server (`mcp:fetch/*`) don't tell them apart. | The MCP gateway names a call by its upstream, and Docker's gateway doesn't prefix its servers' tools by default. | Give each Docker server its own upstream, named after the server. | [Docker MCP Gateway](integrations/docker-mcp-gateway.md#setup) |
| The MCP gateway doesn't cut the secrets Docker's MCP Gateway gives its servers from what it answers and records, and doesn't see the image, mounts or network a call ran with. | It knows only the `env` and `headers` values in its own config; Docker keeps its secrets and containers to itself. | Keep Docker's `--block-secrets` on (the default): it refuses a call whose arguments or text result look like they carry a secret. | [Docker MCP Gateway](integrations/docker-mcp-gateway.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |
| An agent that can use Docker (the `docker` group, a mounted Docker socket, Docker Desktop as its own user) can start Docker's MCP Gateway itself and call its servers around Tracekit. | Custody covers only the calls routed through the MCP gateway. | Run the agent as a user without Docker access, and the MCP gateway as one with it. | [Docker MCP Gateway](integrations/docker-mcp-gateway.md#setup) |
| Docker's MCP Gateway's OpenTelemetry spans (`mcp.tool.call`) aren't imported as tool calls: the OTLP import counts and skips them. | They carry Docker's own attributes (`mcp.tool.name`, `mcp.server.name`), not the conventions the import reads, and no arguments or results. | Record Docker's tool calls through the MCP gateway. | [Docker MCP Gateway](integrations/docker-mcp-gateway.md#what-tracekit-sees-and-doesnt) |

## Egress observation (1.2)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| The egress observer sees hosts, ports, byte counts and times, never paths, headers or content: a call to an explained host can still carry anything. | It doesn't intercept TLS, so it never holds the agents' traffic in clear or a CA they would have to trust. | Route actions whose content matters through the executor or the MCP gateway; read `effect.observed` as where the run went, not what it sent. | [egress](egress.md#what-is-recorded) |
| Attribution by identity (no run token in the proxy URL) gives an observation to the identity's latest open run, which is ambiguous when one identity runs several at once. The record says `attribution: identity`. | The proxy knows who connected, not for which run, unless the client says so. | Set `HTTPS_PROXY=https://<run_id>:<run token>@...` per run (`attribution: token`); in system mode one agent user runs one session at a time. | [egress](egress.md#attribution) |
| `posture` (`network: enforced`, `tool_calls: enforced`) and `gateway_mandatory` are what the operator asserts in signer.yaml: `run.registered` records them with `attested: false`, and the matrix's `enforced` for model calls, tool calls and network rests on them (`asserted`). Without `tool_calls: enforced`, tool calls are at most `reported`, even when every decision came through the executor or the MCP gateway. | The signer can't see the cluster's network policy, where provider keys live, or whether the agent has another way to act than the calls it saw. | Back it with the central chart's `agentEgress` NetworkPolicy on a CNI that enforces egress, keys only in the LLM gateway, agents with no tools of their own, and test each from an agent pod. | [egress](egress.md#posture) |
| A host only `explains` covers (CDNs, redirects, package indexes) is `reconcile.unexplained_effect` until it is listed, so a fresh deployment sees many. The server pack only notifies on them (shadow). | The signer knows the hosts of http calls, not what a shell command or a library contacts beneath them. | Tune the policy's `explains` from the warnings (the detail names the host) before you set `unexplained_effect` to enforce. | [egress](egress.md#explains) |
| An `explains` host glob over a public suffix of two labels (`*.co.uk`, `*.github.io`) passes lint and explains every host under it, whoever holds it. | Lint only requires a dot and two or more labels after the last wildcard; it has no public suffix list, and `explains` is the operator's own policy. | Write the narrowest host you mean: the hosts themselves, or a suffix one owner holds (`*.cdn.example.co.uk`). | [egress](egress.md#explains) |
| A laptop without Linux system mode's `--egress` has no observer: its network is `reported` (http calls) or `not covered`. `--egress` needs Linux with iptables' owner match; macOS has none. | Only a rule outside the agent's user can force its traffic through the observer. | Use [system mode](deploy.md#laptop-system-mode)'s init with `--v2 --egress` on Linux, or run agents on a server deployment behind the egress observer. | [egress](egress.md#linux-system-mode) |
| An observer covers the runs of its tenant registered while it is alive, and which runs it covers lives in the signer's memory: a run registered before its first heartbeat, or open across a signer restart, is not `observed`. After a restart with no snapshot since, a run's earlier http calls no longer explain a host. | The heartbeat watch is the transcript tailer's, which keeps no state in the log. | Start the observer before the agents; re-check runs that span a signer restart. | [egress](egress.md#heartbeats-and-coverage) |
| A run an observer covers reaches `run.final` up to `observe_grace_s` (default 120 s) after it closes; an observation after that goes into the tenant's signer run as `effect_outside_run`, not reconciled. | The observer reports each minute once it ends, at its next heartbeat; a final run takes nothing more. | Raise `observe_grace_s` if `effect_outside_run` gaps name late runs; export them with `--run-set`. | [egress](egress.md#late-observations) |
| One attribution (a connection's identity, else its run) has at most 64 tunnels open through the egress proxy at once; one more is closed before it reaches its host. On Linux a loopback client's uid has at most 64 connections open, counted from accept, handshakes included, and every client sends what attributes it within 5 s. The proxy takes at most 256 connections per client address: in Linux system mode every agent connects from loopback, and each uid keeps the others' share; elsewhere (no uid to read, or one address for many sandboxes) one client's half-open connections can still take that address's 256, each for 5 s. | So one agent can't hold every connection its address may open, which would lock the other agents out. | Reuse connections (HTTP keep-alive, HTTP/2) in agents that open many at once; run agents that need more under identities of their own; give each untrusted client an address of its own. | [egress](egress.md#what-is-refused) |
| The proxy reads the server name from the ClientHello's first TLS record only, knows the public DNS-over-HTTPS endpoints by name only, and lets an `allow`ed host through with no SNI check (any protocol). | A split ClientHello is rare; DoH on another host is HTTPS to that host, which is recorded as such. | Keep `allow` to the hosts that need it; DoH elsewhere shows as traffic to its host. | [egress](egress.md#what-is-refused) |
| Only connections made with a run's `proxy_env` (`proxyEnv`) carry its token: the SDKs never set the agent process's own `HTTPS_PROXY`, so its in-process connections reach the observer only when the deployment proxies them, attributed by identity (see the next row). | Proxy settings are per process and its clients read them once; the run token is per run, and one process can hold several runs. | Give `run.proxy_env` to the commands each run starts and to the HTTP clients it uses; set the agent's `HTTPS_PROXY` in the deployment for the rest, with the signers and custody services in `NO_PROXY`. | [egress](egress.md#attribution) |
| The executor and the gateways reach their upstreams directly, not through the observer: their calls are recorded at T2 already, and the agents' NetworkPolicy doesn't restrict them. | Their records name each call's host; an MCP gateway's upstream sessions are shared by every run, so no one run token fits them. | Read their records for where they went; restrict their own egress with the cluster's network policy. | [egress](egress.md#attribution) |
| A host the observer's `allow` doesn't list must resolve to public addresses only: a name resolving to a loopback, private or link-local address is refused (403), internal services included. | Otherwise the proxy would open the agents' way to everything only it can reach (cluster services, cloud metadata). | Add internal hosts agents may reach to `allow`, or to the agent's `NO_PROXY` when it reaches them directly; the 403 names the address. | [egress](egress.md#what-is-refused) |
| An observer covers only the runs of its own tenant (signer.yaml `tenants`). | Its observations are written as its tenant's records. | Run one observer per tenant. | [egress](egress.md#configure) |
| An observer that heartbeats after a run closes and then dies before its next report leaves the run `network: observed` with no `observer_lost` gap: the totals of its last minute, before the close, are lost. | Coverage rests on heartbeats; a beat inside `observe_grace_s` counts as covering the run, and nothing marks that the last minute's report followed. | Keep `observe_grace_s` at 90 s or more (two report intervals), run the observer under a supervisor that restarts it, and read a short-lived run's `observed` network with this in mind. | [egress](egress.md) |
| Attempts the proxy refuses (a raw IP, a private or metadata address, a DNS-over-HTTPS endpoint, an SNI mismatch, a name that doesn't resolve) leave no signed record; only the agent sees the refusal. The proxy resolves every name an agent sends it, so a name can carry data out through DNS (`<data>.evil.example`) whether or not the connection is then refused. | The observer records the connections it opens; a refusal comes before any, and resolving the name is how it decides. | Treat the observer as a record of connections, not of attempts; to catch exfiltration through DNS, log or restrict the resolver the proxy uses. | [egress](egress.md) |
| The observer keeps at most 10,000 observation totals the signer refused, and sends them again with every heartbeat; past that it drops the oldest, says how many on stderr, and they leave no signed record. | A refusal that never changes would otherwise grow the queue without end. | Fix what the observer's stderr names (its grants, its tenant) before the queue fills, and watch that stderr for `dropped`. | [egress](egress.md#attribution) |
| Model calls explain only the public API host of their provider (`api.openai.com`, `api.anthropic.com`). | The record names the provider, not the endpoint. | Send other endpoints through the LLM gateway, which makes those connections itself. | [egress](egress.md#explains) |
| Over the network (the central chart, compose) a plain `HTTPS_PROXY` client without a run token is refused (407): attribution by identity there needs `Proxy-Authorization: Bearer <token>` or a client certificate, which proxy settings can't carry. Only system mode attributes by identity alone (the agent's uid). | The proxy knows a networked client only by what it sends. | Give the agent process a run's `proxy_env` URL as its proxy, or a client that sends a bearer token of the egress audience. | [egress](egress.md#attribution) |
| The proxy checks each run token with the signer before it opens a connection: one round trip per connection, which costs none of the observer's event rate, and while the signer can't be asked, requests with a run token are refused (503, saying so). | Only the signer can check a token (an HMAC with its secret); an unchecked one would let a client name any run. | Keep the signer reachable from the observer; clients retry a 503. | [egress](egress.md#attribution) |
| Totals the signer can't take, or refuses, wait in the proxy's memory (a refused one is sent again with every heartbeat); a killed proxy loses what it had not reported, up to a minute and a heartbeat's. Any restart of the proxy, even after SIGTERM sent everything (a rolling update too), is an `observer_lost` gap in each open run it covered, and so a second proxy process under the same identity: run one replica per observer identity. | It reports per minute to keep records few; the signer can't tell a clean restart from a crash, nor a second process from a restart. | Stop it with SIGTERM, which sends every total, and restart it while no run is open; read its stderr line on a refusal. | [egress](egress.md#heartbeats-and-coverage) |
| Linux system mode's firewall rule refuses the agent uid's DNS (port 53), so the local DNS stub (systemd-resolved's `127.0.0.53`) gets no query from it. But a resolver daemon still answers any local user over a Unix socket, and the lookups it makes can carry data out unobserved: systemd-resolved over D-Bus and varlink (`resolvectl query`, and glibc's `resolve` NSS module), nscd, and avahi-daemon (mDNS on the local network). Dropping `resolve` from `/etc/nsswitch.conf` doesn't stop `resolvectl`.
