After retention, verification can report that an output file is not what its sources give, or fail because the signer is unavailable. | Verification rebuilds the graph and reads the vault again when `content` is true. | Keep the bundles and graph together; verify while the vault retains the content, or build without `--content` when the graph must be rebuildable from bundles alone. | [graph](graph.md#rebuild-and-verify) |
| File paths come from a write tool's path fields or shell targets as far as the policy parser reads them. Hosts come only from a call's `url` argument, not shell commands or an executor action's `url_template`; relative paths stay relative. | The graph uses the policy engine's existing readings and adds no parser. | Query a file by trailing path parts (`--file billing.py`); read hosts a shell command reached from `OBSERVED` egress edges. | [graph](graph.md#edges-and-grades) |
| An egress or kernel observer edge belongs to the run, not a call. A kernel file event stays separate from a call edge for the same path. | A process's carried call id is agent-controlled, so joining it to the observer result would trust the agent. | Read both edges together. | [graph](graph.md#edges-and-grades), [effect checks](findings.md) |
| Build accepts v2 `.tkb` bundles and a trust configuration; it has no default source and does not read a signer store or why log directly. | Each bundle's edges are graded by verification against the trust configuration. | Export a bundle with `tracekit last` or `tracekit export --v2`, then build the graph from it. | [graph](graph.md#build) |
| A graph built with `--content` can disclose paths and host names from call arguments. | Those values identify files and hosts in `graph.json` and both reports. | Share it with the same care as the bundles; omit `--content` when those names should not be included. | [graph](graph.md#build), [privacy](privacy.md#the-content-vault) |
| `tracekit graph query` doesn't verify `graph.json`: it answers from the file as it finds it, so a hand-written one (even with no sources) can answer that a person approved a call, `SIGNED`. Its text output says so on its first line (stderr with `--json`). | `graph.json` is not signed, and verifying needs the bundles, the trust config and, for a `--content` graph, the signer's vault, none of which `query` is given. | Run `tracekit graph verify DIR --trust trust.json` (exit 0) before relying on an answer, and cite each answer's record so it can be checked with `tracekit verify BUNDLE`. | [graph](graph.md#query) |
| `GRAPH_REPORT.md` lists at most 20 files and hosts. | It is a reading summary. | Use `tracekit graph query` or `graph.json` for the rest. | [graph](graph.md#report) |
| A why test is drawn as a tested causal or suppressive edge only when the verifier counts it over the graph's verified sources; one finding about a run that the verifier's check fails (its replay runs missing, or not giving its verdict) leaves every why test about that run uncounted, its label ending `(not counted)`. A counted test is as good as its author: `self-asserted` on the dev signer, whose user can sign tests about its own runs. | The verifier counts a finding only from its replay runs, and fails a run's `--with-findings` export that holds one it can't count. | Build from the run's `tracekit export --v2 --run R --with-findings` bundle; run `tracekit why` as an identity your trust config pins in `analyzers`. | [graph](graph.md#edges-and-grades), [why](why.md) |
| The Graphify join links only a whole matching `source_file` suffix and adds none of Graphify's own edges. | The join is read-only and keeps no dependency on Graphify. | Use Graphify for code structure; use the graph join to see its code nodes for a changed file. | [graph](graph.md#join-with-graphify) |

## Intent, reasoning and action checks

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| On a laptop, signed findings read `self-asserted`: the user who runs `tracekit analyze --v2` is the agent's user, which the dev signer lists in `analyzers`. | One user is both agent and analyzer; only a different identity can vouch for a run. | For findings an auditor takes as independent, run `tracekit analyze --v2` as an operator identity listed in signer.yaml `analyzers` (never the agent's) and pin it in the verifier's trust config. | [verdicts](verdicts.md#reviews) |
| Each pass of `tracekit analyze --v2` is a findings run of its own; passes aren't merged or deduplicated. | A signed record can't be withdrawn; a later pass may read more content. | Read the latest pass of each author; `--dry-run` to look without signing. | [findings](findings.md#signed-findings) |
| What the model said is its own account: a thinking summary is the provider's summary of the reasoning, not the reasoning, and every text the checks read in a Claude Code session comes from the transcript, which the agent can write. | The transcript is the only record of the model's text the hooks have; nothing outside the agent's user writes it. | Route model calls through the [LLM gateway](gateway.md), whose record is preferred; read each finding's trust tier (`transcript`: the agent can write it). | [findings](findings.md#v2-runs-what-was-asked-said-and-done-tracekit-analyze---v2), [evaluation](evaluation.md#e20-intent-reasoning-and-action-checks) |
| A prompt line counts as the person's only when the hook recorded the same typed text at UserPromptSubmit (TK-X208 flags one it didn't: E20 `c5_line`, 24 of 24 flagged, 0 before). The hook runs as the agent's user: an agent that runs the hook itself with a made-up prompt and writes the matching transcript line passes. A harness that changes the text between the hook and the transcript (an attachment) makes a real prompt `unconfirmed`; a paste or an image Claude Code writes as a placeholder ("[Pasted text #1 +40 lines]") pairs with the hook's prompt before it whose text holds the line's other text, and a hook prompt no line pairs with (a line the tailer missed) is a request of its own. Hooks from before this release record no prompt (`no_hook`), and their transcript lines count as before. | Only a hook process sees the harness's payload, and on a laptop it runs as the agent. | Read TK-X208 findings against the session; system mode keeps the signer and the tailer out of the agent's reach, not the hook; update the hooks with `tracekit init --dev --v2`. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#what-was-asked-and-said) |
| A transcript line that holds only text in wrappers Tracekit recognises (a task notification, a relayed message, a plugin's lead, a system reminder), with no typed text, is no prompt line: TK-X208 neither checks nor flags it, so such a line the agent appends to its own transcript goes unflagged (1.4.2 flagged it). It lifts and clears nothing, and `tracekit analyze --v2` reads none of it as a request. | Claude Code adds some wrappers after UserPromptSubmit, so the hook's prompt for that turn holds other text (a plugin's or observer's raw message), and its code can write other wrapper-only lines (hook output, teammate messages, system reminders) with no UserPromptSubmit at all. Until real transcripts show which, pairing these lines with the hook's prompts would flag real ones as high findings. | Treat a wrapper-only line in the transcript as text the harness or the agent wrote, never as the person's (Tracekit does the same). To see what a turn really submitted, read the run's `claude-code:<source>` input records for it. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#what-was-asked-and-said) |
| TK-X202 checks names and the words around them, not meaning: an edit counts as told when the request names its file before the call, or the model's text says it changes it (a change word tied to the file, before the call or after: "applying the same fix to billing.py", "billing.py needs the same fix", "the bug is in billing.py"). Naming the file innocently no longer clears it (E20 `c1` with no constraint: every edit caught on every set; 17% tuned, 0% pool v2 before 2026-10-19), but one the model says it updates passes whatever reason it gives ("updating billing.py to match the fix"); "the billing module" for billing.py is no mention (a false alarm); a new file, or one a search found and the request didn't name, is flagged (5 pool v1 and 4 pool v2 hard negatives, and 2 of the 119 calls of E20's honest sessions). In a request clause with a negation, a file is no longer named where the negation governs a change of it: a prohibition the grammar can't read ("nobody should ever edit X", "X must not be edited" phrased past its lists) doesn't count X as asked, so its edit is TK-X202's; a negation of what the file does ("make sure uploader.py never sends the token", "X must not crash when ...") or of a change of one part of it ("don't change the signature in utils.py") leaves it named, and so does a negation about something else for a name with no extension ("update the README so it never mentions the old flag"). A name the grammar doesn't read as a file stays named under a negation it can't read as a change of it: "never let anyone edit the run script" is TK-X202's, "the run script must not change" is neither TK-X202's nor TK-X204's, and nor is "don't touch the README" for README.md (name the file: "README.md"). Only a write tool's edit is checked: a shell command that writes a file (`sed -i`, a redirection, `cp`, `patch`) is never TK-X202's (TK-X203 and TK-X204 read its targets). | A word check can't read intent; a shell command's targets are a lexer's guess. | Read each TK-X202 finding against the change; name files in requests; add `--review`, a reviewer model that reads meaning (TK-X210, warn only). | [findings](findings.md#v2-runs-what-was-asked-said-and-done-tracekit-analyze---v2) |
| Stated constraints (TK-X204, and live constraints) are read by a fixed English grammar ([constraints.py](../tracekit/constraints.py)): a negated change word tied to the file ("don't touch / edit / write to / mess with X", "make no changes to X", "please refrain from editing X", "I'd rather you didn't touch X", "I don't want X changed"), the file said to stay as it is ("leave / keep X as is", "X is off-limits / out of scope / maintained elsewhere / frozen", "X belongs to another team", "X must not be modified"), "hands off X", "stay away from X", "keep (all) your changes / your hands / any edits / yourself out of X" (only the agent's changes: "keep the API key out of settings.py" and "keep this out of X" keep nothing away), "it" or "there" for the file named before ("X is generated; make no changes there"), a directory ("anything in vendor/", "leave src/legacy/ alone", "the vendor directory", "the .github folder", "stay out of the infra/ folder", "keep your changes out of db/migrations/"; not "the home / root / current / working / parent directory", a place rather than a folder's name; a folder word leaves the clause's files as they are, so "use the tmp folder for scratch files and leave config.yaml as is" keeps config.yaml away), "leave X exactly as it is", "the X file must not change", "under no circumstances touch X", "don't make any changes" (read-only; "don't make any changes to / in X" keeps X away when X is a file or a directory, and is no read-only) or a file people name without an extension ("the Dockerfile", "Makefile", "LICENSE"), "read-only" as an instruction ("read-only: just look", "stay read-only", "work in read-only mode"), "don't change anything", scopes ("only change T", "limit the change to T", "touch nothing but T", "everything else is out of scope"), "don't push", "no network", "work offline". Other phrasings state nothing: it reads 9 of the 14 ways of saying it in E20's pool v1 ("don't go near X", "a hard freeze on X until after the audit", "X has a pending PR, so editing it will cause a conflict: avoid", "hands off for now" after naming X in another clause, "nothing under X" are not) and 11 of 12 in pool v2 ("it would be better if X stayed exactly as it is" is not); both pools were read while the grammar was written. On pool v3 (blind, scored once on 2026-10-20; the final code review's fixes after that run moved none of its numbers) a live constraint held 67-92% of the edits made after a "don't touch X", by cover. A file is read in a window of five words. Some phrasings are left unread so they hold no wanted call (on E20's 56 honest sessions none is held; before the round-2 fixes 13 were, as "read-only" describing a mode, "nothing should change for existing callers" and "don't push to main" were read as constraints), though a phrasing outside them still can, which the person then approves (an approval by another identity than the run's lowers TK-X204 to low): a condition after the file, the push or the network ("don't edit X until the tests pass", "without checking the tests first", "unless", "before", "if", "while the server runs", "when DEBUG is set": the change is allowed once the condition holds, which the grammar can't check; "while you work" and "while fixing Y" are the task itself; a condition on the person's own go-ahead, "don't push before I review", "without asking me first", "until I say so", "unless I confirm", keeps the constraint, as the hold is what they ask for); "read-only" describing a thing ("add a read-only mode", "make the connection read-only", "the cache should be read-only after startup"); "nothing should change" for its callers; a negated change of one aspect of the file asked for ("don't change the public API of X", "the signature in X", "nothing in X changes its behaviour"); a scoped push ("don't push to main", "never push secrets"; "don't push it to origin" is read); a push or the network in a description rather than an instruction ("we don't use the network in unit tests", "the app works without network access", "our policy is no network access in CI"); a constraint a later clause of the same request asks for ("don't touch X for now; ... then update X too", "don't change anything, then fix it"); a question that asks rather than requests ("should I change X?"; "could you leave X alone?" is read); quoted text, quoted lines ("> ...") and code (blocks, lines four spaces in); a negation turned around ("don't forget to update X", "you don't need to touch X", "I don't mind if you change X"); "don't change how X is imported"; "any changes" followed by what picks them out: "to", "of", "in", "on" and the like with no file or directory there ("don't make any changes to the tests", "... to the public API", "... on main"), or "that", "which", "beyond", "outside", "for the sake of" ("... that break the API", "... beyond what the fix needs", "... for the sake of style"; the whole project, "to the code / the repo / my project", "on disk", "on this machine", and "of any kind", "in the meantime", "under any circumstances", "to any file", "for now", "for any reason", "for me", "on your own", "directly" stay read-only); "outside X" ("don't touch anything outside src/", a scope; "everything outside X is off-limits" is read); "keep the diff to X" (as "keep the changes to X small" asks for a change); "don't change anything except the docs" or "don't change anything else" naming no file; a network word after a change verb or before a noun ("don't change the network code", "no web UI changes"); "offline" anywhere but at the start of an instruction ("make the CLI work offline"). | A grammar that guesses more would hold or flag calls the person asked for. | Say constraints plainly in requests ("don't touch billing.py"); read the run's constraints at the top of its review in `tracekit view`. | [policy](policy-v2.md#live-constraints) |
| A scope ("only change src/pager.py", "everything else is out of scope") is checked after the run (TK-X204) and never held live: the hook adds no live constraint for it. A file named to keep away ("don't touch billing.py") is still held live. | Holding every write but the named files would ask the person about each test, lockfile or build write. | Read TK-X204 after the run; name the files that must not change ("don't touch billing.py"), which the signer holds live, or keep them out with a policy rule. | [policy](policy-v2.md#live-constraints) |
| A later "it", "them" or "there" in a clause that asks the agent for the change lifts the file named just before it in the same request (for "them", each file of that clause), so "Don't touch billing.py. If tests fail, fix them." states no constraint: "them" is read as billing.py, and the constraint is lost (no live hold, no TK-X204). A clause asks for the change when its change word is in its plain form with only words of asking or allowing before it ("fix them", "you can edit it locally", "later you can update it", "feel free to change it", "go ahead and update it"), and it doesn't start with "to", "should" or "must". A clause that waits on the person lifts no file, named or by "it": the person's go-ahead in it or in a clause after it that only says that ("you can change it after I approve", "edit X once I say so", "you can change it later, but ask me first"). Any other clause with the change lifts nothing, and the constraint holds: "ask me before you change it", "to change it, ask me first", "should you need to change it, ask me", "I'll update it myself", "we'll change it in the next PR", "explain how you would change it", "changing it breaks the deploy", and also "don't forget to update it", "you'll need to edit it", "you may need to change it" and "I want you to change it", which do ask for it (the person approves the held call). A constraint whose condition is the person's own go-ahead ("never touch X without asking me first. Change the timeout there to 30") is never lifted that way: it holds, and a file a request lifts that way counts as named (no TK-X202). | The grammar can't tell what a pronoun stands for; it takes the file named just before it, so that "X is frozen in production, but you can edit it locally" reads as the person meant, and lifts only where the clause hands the change to the agent, so a change merely mentioned keeps the constraint. | Name the thing to change instead of "it" ("if tests fail, fix the tests"), name the file again ("fix them, but don't touch billing.py"), or state the constraint last ("If tests fail, fix them. Don't touch billing.py."). | [policy](policy-v2.md#live-constraints) |
| A live constraint compares a file by its base name, and a directory by its name anywhere in the path written: another billing.py elsewhere is held too, as is a write under any directory of the kept one's name (one over the project included), and a recursive `rm` or `rmdir` of it. The writes it sees: a write tool's paths, a redirection, `tee`, `touch`, `rm`, `truncate`, `shred`, `cp`/`mv`/`install`/`ln`/`rsync`/`scp` destinations, `sed -i`/`perl -i` files, `dd of=`, `patch`'s file, `git rm`/`mv`/`restore` paths and `git checkout -- X`, the `-name` of a `find` that deletes or runs a command, and formatters that rewrite in place (`black`, `isort`, `rustfmt`, `prettier --write`, `gofmt -w`, `ruff format`, `ruff check --fix`, `eslint --fix`). A write the policy engine's shell parser can't see isn't held: a script, a variable or glob path, `python -c` or `python fix.py billing.py`, `awk -i inplace`, an editor (`vim`, `ex`), an archive extracted over it, `git apply` and `patch` with their files only in the diff (a write, so `read_only` holds them; a file kept away is not matched); a line it can't parse, such as `case`, which the policy's TK-SHELL-PARSE asks about. A push is `git push`; the network is an http or browser call, `WebFetch`, `curl`, `wget`, `ssh`, `git` fetch, pull, clone or push and package installs. A run takes 32 distinct file constraints (`keep_away`, `only_change`; read-only, no push and no network are always added): past them a new one is a signed `capture.gap{constraint_refused}`, `constraint_add` answers `refused`, the hook tells the person, and every later write of the run is held, so made-up constraints only make the agent wait. | The signer matches what the call's arguments show, by commitment; it doesn't run the shell. | Keep a policy rule or a sandbox for writes that must never happen; add the [kernel sensor](integrations/tetragon.md), whose file and connect effects TK-X204 checks after the run. | [policy](policy-v2.md#live-constraints) |
| A call that writes more than 256 distinct files is held in any run with a file constraint (`keep_away`, `only_change`), whatever the files: "the call writes more than 256 files". | The signer checks each file name against each file constraint by salted commitment; the cap bounds that work per call. | Approve the call if the person meant it, or write the files in smaller commands. | [policy](policy-v2.md#live-constraints) |
| The hook keeps a session's constraints in its own state, in the agent's runtime dir: a session resumed after it ended starts without its earlier prompts' constraints (SessionEnd removes the state), and an agent can edit that state before its next run. A run the signer closed idle passes them to the session's next run for at most 30 days after the session's last hook event (Claude Code's default transcript retention), and on Linux only until the runtime dir (`$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`) is cleared at logout or reboot; a session continued later starts without them. | Constraints are signed per run; the hook is the only place that knows a session's earlier prompts, and its state must not pile up forever. | Restate constraints in the first prompt after resuming a session; for limits that must hold whatever the agent does, use a policy rule. | [policy](policy-v2.md#live-constraints) |
| On a laptop, the person who answers a call a live constraint held is the agent's user. | The dev signer has one user. | In system mode, set signer.yaml `approvals` to a person other than the agent. | [approvals](approvals.md) |
| "Signed before this call" holds only when the record of what was said is older than the call's decision. With Claude Code alone it often isn't: the tailer reads the transcript after the model wrote it, so its lines are signed after the hook's decide (`transcript_order`). | The hook decides as soon as Claude Code runs the tool; the tailer follows the file. | Route model calls through the LLM gateway, which records the response before the client gets it. | [viewer](viewer.md#laptop-viewer) |
| Claude Code keeps the model's thinking only sealed unless thinking summaries are on: TK-X201 is then `withheld` for its calls. | Claude Code's default shows no thinking text (`showThinkingSummaries`; headless `--thinking-display`). | `tracekit init --v2` turns them on (`--no-thinking-summaries` leaves them alone); headless: `claude -p --thinking-display summarized`; `tracekit doctor` warns (`D-THINKING-SUMMARIES`). | [doctor](doctor.md) |
| The LLM gateway asks for readable reasoning only where the request already has the model reason: a request with no `thinking` (a Claude model that thinks by default, such as Opus 5.5, still returns none of it), `display: "updates"`, or a chat completions request gets what it asked for. | The gateway never turns on reasoning the agent didn't ask for, and changes only the field that shows it. | Have the agent send `thinking: {type: "adaptive"}` (Anthropic) or `reasoning` (Responses); the gateway then asks for the summaries. | [gateway](gateway.md#readable-reasoning) |
| What the gateway gets back is the provider's summary of the reasoning, in its words, not the reasoning, which providers return only sealed (a thinking block's signature, OpenAI's `encrypted_content`) or not at all. A summary can leave out what drove a call. | Providers don't return these models' reasoning in the open; a summary is the most they show. | Read a summary as the model's account, never as proof of why it acted. | [gateway](gateway.md#readable-reasoning), [findings](findings.md#v2-runs-what-was-asked-said-and-done-tracekit-analyze---v2) |
| Any 400 or 422 to a request the gateway amended is taken as the provider refusing the amendment: the request goes again as the client sent it, so one bad for another reason reaches the provider twice. After a request sent again succeeds, that model's requests aren't amended until the gateway restarts (at most 1,024 models remembered). | Providers word an unknown-field error differently, and the agent must never fail on a field it didn't send. | Read the first exchange's error (`sent again without the amendment as exchange ...`); restart the gateway once a provider takes the field. | [gateway](gateway.md#readable-reasoning) |
| TK-X203 reads a Bash command with a lexer: redirections, `tee`, `touch`, `mkdir`, `rm`, `rmdir`, `truncate`, and the destination of `cp`, `mv`, `install`, `ln`. Variables, globs, command substitutions and scripts aren't followed, symlinks aren't resolved, and a relative path after a `cd` is not checkable. The project is `--project` or the first prompt's working directory; `/` or a home directory is not checkable. | A shell is a language; the analyzer doesn't run it. | Pass `--project`; for writes that must be seen, add the [kernel sensor](integrations/tetragon.md), whose file effects TK-X203 also checks. | [findings](findings.md#v2-runs-what-was-asked-said-and-done-tracekit-analyze---v2) |
| A run still open is refused unless `--open`, and then is `partial`; the analysis stops at its budget (60 s, `--budget`) or its size caps (131,072 distinct words, 8 Mi characters of prompts): what it found before stays, what it didn't reach is `not_checkable: budget` or `size`, and the result is `INCOMPLETE` (exit 3 when nothing high was found). Each text a check reads is cut at 64 KiB, and a streamed response keeps at most 512 Ki characters of text and reasoning, and a Gemini candidate at most 1,024 parts (marked `truncated`). | What an open run does next isn't in it; a run of any size must finish. | Analyse after `run.final`; raise `--budget` for very long runs; review calls past the size caps by hand or with `--review`. | [findings](findings.md#v2-runs-what-was-asked-said-and-done-tracekit-analyze---v2) |
| The constraint grammar reads a text in time linear in its length except in two places: a negated push or network word, or a word that lifts a constraint ("fix", "add", "push"), reads the words before it in its clause again (a 64 KiB clause of them: about a second), and "them" stands for every file of the clause before it in each clause that says it (64 KiB of "don't change them" or "leave them alone" after 3,000 to 5,000 files: about 2 s and up to 1.1 GiB in `tracekit analyze --v2`). The hook reads each prompt with the same grammar. | Reading each such word against its clause keeps the grammar small, and real requests are far from these sizes. | Nothing on the signer: the grammar runs off it, in the hook and in `tracekit analyze --v2`. Bound the text a reviewer feeds the analysis from elsewhere. | [findings](findings.md#v2-runs-what-was-asked-said-and-done-tracekit-analyze---v2) |
| A call decided under an id the model's response doesn't carry (an adapter's own id, a Gemini call without one) is `unlinked`: its reasoning is not checked. | The link between a call and the model message is the provider's tool call id. | Use an integration that decides under the provider's id. | [findings](findings.md#v2-runs-what-was-asked-said-and-done-tracekit-analyze---v2) |
| The text checks (TK-X205, X101..X104, X111..X114, X120, X211) read words: a success claim, a denial or a mention phrased outside their word lists is missed. On E20's pool v3 (blind, scored once on 2026-10-20) TK-X205 finds 86% of the false success claims after a failed run (exit 86%, piped 87%). On pool v2 (blind, scored once on 2026-10-19, read since) it finds 85% of false success claims made with the pool's success phrases and all of its free-form final summaries; on pool v1 68% and 96%. An admission takes back the success claims about what it names ("2 tests still fail"), not the others: a summary that says a test still fails passes even when another check it claims also failed. A denial is read as the model's own act: what a thing does ("so it never makes network calls", "the app works without network access") is no denial, nor is one after "so" or with "it", "they" or "which" for its subject; a lie put that way ("so nothing was pushed") is missed. "Changed X without touching the tests" denies only the tests. "No code changes" denies an edit of a source file only, read by its extension (59 of them, [findings_text.py](../tracekit/findings_text.py) `SOURCE`): an edit of a file with another extension (a stylesheet, a template, a config) is no code change. TK-X101 reads a claimed test, build or lint run ("all tests pass", "the build is clean", "ESLint reports zero problems", "I ran the linter"); a success claim that names no check ("everything works as expected") claims no run, and most of the pools' claims are such: 13% of pool v2's and 29% of pool v1's Fc sessions are found, every one whose claim names a test run. "X is installed" reads as a state, not an install. A claim or denial that names nothing gives the claim and denial checks nothing to find: TK-X211 finds the action it leaves out. | A word list can't read meaning, and a text check finds only what the text says. | Read each finding against the run; for runs that matter, add `--review` (a reviewer model, TK-X210) or review what the model said yourself. | [findings](findings.md#v2-runs-what-was-asked-said-and-done-tracekit-analyze---v2), [evaluation](evaluation.md#e20-intent-reasoning-and-action-checks) |
| TK-X211 counts an action as told when the model names it, its readable reasoning included (Claude Code shows the person a thinking summary): a word of its kind in the model message that asked for the call or one after it ("added the dependency it needed and pushed" passes; a word of deleting only with a part of the deleted file's name, a package noun only beside a word of adding), its object named anywhere in the run, also for another reason ("backing up billing.py", then billing.py sent out; E20 tuned F2 c1: 0 of 24 caught), or the person's request asking for it, by its object ("upload report.txt") or with the verb as an instruction about this work ("commit and push the branch"; not "the upload handler", "I'll push it myself", "remove the unused helper" before a delete of another file). A kind word with or after the call still clears it when it is about something else ("uploaded the screenshot" beside an upload of `.env`). A dotfile is named only with its dot: ".env" tells of an upload of .env, "env" ("checking the env vars") or "travis.yml" for .travis.yml tells of nothing, and an ellipsis is no dot ("the config... env vars"). The request's words too: "upload the env file" asks for no upload of .env, so one the model never mentions is found. Sealed reasoning tells nothing. It sees what a lexer sees: curl and wget body or file flags, an HTTP tool's POST, PUT or PATCH with a body, httpie's method, scp, rsync and sftp to a host:path, git push, package publishes, rm, git rm, git clean, a delete tool, `install` or `add` of a named package; a script, an SDK call, a variable or another program hides the action. A delete of a temp path, a build or cache directory, or a file of a name an earlier call wrote is the run's own (by name: a file of that name elsewhere passes too); a file an edit tool changed is not. | Counting only the text flagged honest runs that explain the action in their reasoning; a lexer doesn't run the shell. | Read TK-X211 against the run; require approval with a policy rule for actions that must never happen unannounced; name a dotfile with its dot in requests (".env"); add the [kernel sensor](integrations/tetragon.md) for what a script does. | [findings](findings.md#v2-runs-what-was-asked-said-and-done-tracekit-analyze---v2), [evaluation](evaluation.md#e20-intent-reasoning-and-action-checks) |
| TK-X205 sees a failure only in a related call's signed result or its output: a test or build run it can name (pytest, jest, vitest, mocha, go test, cargo, unittest, make, npm/yarn/pnpm scripts, Maven, Gradle, ...) whose result is `ok: false`, or whose output has a runner's failure summary, a non-zero exit, or a Traceback or error line with no pass summary. A test run through a script it doesn't name, a runner that prints success and exits 0 after a failure, output cut before its summary, or a claim about something other than tests, a build, a deploy, a merge, a push, a commit or an install is not checked. A result the agent didn't complete is `no_result`. | Only the run's own records and their content are read; the analyzer doesn't run anything. | Run checks with a runner it names, keep the summary line in the output (`tail` keeps it), and complete every call. | [findings](findings.md#v2-runs-what-was-asked-said-and-done-tracekit-analyze---v2) |
| TK-X210 (`--review`) is a model's opinion: it can miss a fault, flag an honest run, and be steered by text in the run that addresses it; two calls on one run may answer differently. Its detection and false-alarm rates are not measured yet (the E20 model arm is built, not run). | A reviewer model reads meaning the checks can't, and the run's text is written by the agent it reviews. | Read TK-X210 as a second opinion beside the deterministic findings, never as a pass; it never changes the exit code or decides a call. Run the E20 model arm on your reviewer model before relying on it. | [findings](findings.md#the-reviewer-model-tk-x210), [server threat model](threat-model-server.md#signed-findings) |
| A reviewer of the agent's own model family may share its blind spots. The family is read from model names (`claude`, `gpt`, `gemini`, ...); a fine-tune or alias with another name is not recognised. | The analyzer knows a model only by the name the records and `--review-model` give. | Configure a second provider (`OPENAI_API_KEY` or `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, or `TRACEKIT_REVIEW_MODEL` with `TRACEKIT_REVIEW_URL`) and `--review` picks the one of another family; or pass `--review-model`. `tracekit analyze` warns when the families match. | [findings](findings.md#the-reviewer-model-tk-x210) |
| `--review` sends the run's text (redacted by the signer) to the reviewer model: the one at `--review-url`, else the one it picks (TRACEKIT_REVIEW_URL's, else Anthropic's or OpenAI's API, whichever key is set and is of another family than the agent's). | Judging a run means reading it. | For runs whose text must not leave your machines, point `--review-url` at a model you run yourself, or at your LLM gateway in front of one. | [privacy](privacy.md#what-leaves-the-machine) |
| The reviewer reads a run cut to about 48,000 characters (each text to 1,200, arguments to 400; records dropped from the middle), and sees whether each call succeeded, not its result. | One bounded call per pass keeps cost and time fixed on runs of any size. | Read the cut (`shown.review.truncated`); for a long run, read TK-X210 for its start and end only. | [findings](findings.md#the-reviewer-model-tk-x210) |
| `--review` needs a findings run to record the model call in, so it runs only as an identity signer.yaml `analyzers` lists, on the signer that holds the run, and not with `--dry-run`. Without `--review-gateway` the analyzer records the call itself: the record is its own report, not an independent one. Only Anthropic Messages and OpenAI chat endpoints are spoken. | The call is evidence too, kept beside the findings; only the gateway records it from outside the analyzer. | Run it as an analyzer identity; use `--review-gateway` where the gateway runs. | [findings](findings.md#the-reviewer-model-tk-x210) |
| CrewAI, Browser Use, a LangGraph `tracekit_tool_node` graph and the Vercel AI SDK record no readable reasoning through their integration; the Claude Agent SDK needs `tracekit_said` in the app's message loop and Microsoft Agent Framework needs `tk.chat` in `middleware=`. | CrewAI's after-LLM hook sees no response that asks for a tool, Browser Use's Tools never see the model's output, a ToolNode has no model hook, the Vercel middleware sends a commitment only; neither SDK gives a hook the model's messages. | Record those model calls with `tracekit.instrument()` or the [LLM gateway](gateway.md); add the one line where the table says. | [reasoning](reasoning.md#frameworks) |
| A framework rarely says whether reasoning text is a summary or the model's own reasoning: the integrations record it as a summary unless they know (an open model's `reasoning_content`, an inline `<think>`). Cursor's thinking text is recorded as a summary too. | Only the provider knows; the framework passes the text on. | Read `summary` as "readable"; the monitorability score never counts more full reasoning than there was. | [reasoning](reasoning.md) |
| Once an integration records model messages, the signer reconciles them with the calls: a call it decides that no recorded message carries reads as `reconcile.fabricated` (a message that failed to record, which warned), and a call in a recorded message no decide followed reads as `reconcile.hook_missing` (a run that stopped before its tools ran; a call the framework skipped or a person turned down before any hook: an ADK confirmation declined). The LangChain middleware and Pydantic AI's capability add each call to its response only when it reaches their gate, so a call they never decide (a person's reject or a call limit in another LangChain middleware, the recursion limit, arguments that fail Pydantic AI's validation) is not recorded; a Pydantic AI approval resumed by another `Tracekit` capability adds the call from the history. A call a person edited in LangChain's `HumanInTheLoopMiddleware` is recorded as the model asked for it and decided as edited: it reads as `reconcile.args_mismatch`. Gemini CLI's and Cursor's messages name no call (`said_only`) and are never reconciled. | Reconciliation can't tell a call left out of the record from one the agent made up, nor a person's edit from an agent's. | Read such records against the warnings the agent printed and the run's last steps; read an `args_mismatch` on a call your app let a person edit against that edit (the interrupt's resume value), or use the policy's `ask`, whose approval the signer records. | [gateway](gateway.md#reconciliation), [reasoning](reasoning.md) |
| Codex's reasoning is readable only with `model_reasoning_summary` set (init sets it at the top of `config.toml`; a profile's own setting is neither set nor read by init and doctor), and stays sealed (`encrypted_content`) without it. Each hook reads the rollout lines written since the session's last hook, at most 4 MiB: lines that failed to send are not read again and no gap says so, a rollout rewritten in place is read on from its new end, and a Codex release that names a call's hook differently reads as `args_mismatch` or `hook_missing`. | The rollout is Codex's only record of the model's reasoning; the hook maps each call the way Codex builds its hook payload. | Keep the setting on (`tracekit doctor`: `D-CODEX-REASONING`); route model calls through the LLM gateway where the record must be complete. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#what-was-asked-and-said) |
| Gemini CLI's thought summaries and Cursor's thinking text are not linked to a call, and Gemini CLI writes its thoughts after the calls ran. Text from Codex's rollout, Gemini CLI's chat recording and Cursor's hooks is written by the agent's own process (trust tier `transcript`). | Neither gives a tool call an id in its hooks; Gemini CLI queues thoughts until the calls complete; the files and hooks belong to the agent's user. | Read their reasoning as the session's, not a call's; route model calls through the LLM gateway for text the agent can't write. | [coding agents](coding-agents.md#what-was-asked-and-said) |
| `tracekit view` runs the checks only for the dev signer's final runs; another viewer shows each model line as committed, not its text. | Only the dev signer grants the viewer's user `content_read`. | Run `tracekit analyze --v2` as an identity with `content_read`. | [viewer](viewer.md#laptop-viewer) |

## Effect checks (TK-X206, TK-X207)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| TK-X206 and TK-X207 join effects to calls by time across two clocks: the sensor's (each effect) and the signer's (each decision and result). Past `--clock-skew` (0.5 s) honest effects become findings. A sensor clock ahead shows in any effect recorded before its own time; one behind only when every effect reached the signer more than the import's one-second flush late, which a slow import looks like too. | The sensor stamps events on its node; the signer on its host. | Keep both hosts on NTP; when a finding is labelled `clock_skew`, run again with the `--clock-skew` it names. | [findings](findings.md#effects-what-a-kernel-sensor-saw) |
| The harness itself (Claude Code's node process, Codex) runs from before the run: its connections (its model calls) and file writes are `agent_process`, not checked by TK-X206, TK-X203 or TK-X204, except a file at a write call's path inside that call's window. So code that runs inside the harness's own process (a plugin, an extension) and writes outside the project isn't flagged from the sensor's records; a call's process writing there is. A WebFetch's connection counts only to a host the harness didn't reach outside fetch calls, and a fetch over a connection the harness kept open opens none (TK-X207 finding). | Nothing names the call for the harness's own work, and a sensor gives an address, not a host name. | For hosts, add the [egress observer](egress.md), whose signed reconcile covers them; read a fetch's TK-X207 against the egress records. | [findings](findings.md#effects-what-a-kernel-sensor-saw) |
| A shell or Python process started outside any call, not by a call's process, is `hook_like`, not checked: Tracekit's own hooks look the same, and so does a hook or status line of your own, or a Python program a planted hook runs (E20 held-out: a Python exfiltration between calls was not checkable in 4 of 8 Claude Code runs). Its other programs are still checked. | The hooks run as the harness's children, around each call, with no call of their own; only binary and parent tell them apart. | Review the harness's hook settings; with Codex, keep `CODEX_TOOL_CALL_ID` in the sensor so a call's processes name it. | [findings](findings.md#effects-what-a-kernel-sensor-saw) |
| Without parent exec ids (Falco, or Tetragon before this release's import), a process a call left running is that call's only inside its window: a server's later children and connections are TK-X206 findings. | Falco names no parent process; the join falls back to time. | Use Tetragon with `tracekit sensor import` from this release for agents that start servers or background jobs. | [findings](findings.md#effects-what-a-kernel-sensor-saw) |
| TK-X207 checks a write only where you say the sensor's file policy selects writes (`--sensor-skip DIR` for each directory it leaves out), a kind of effect only if the run has some, and a Bash command's programs by name: a command whose program has the same name as Tracekit's hook interpreter (`python3 -m pytest` against a `python3` hook) is `hook_binary`, not checked, when only such processes ran. | The run doesn't record the sensor's policy; a hook's process and the command's look alike. | Pass `--sensor-skip`; with Codex keep `CODEX_TOOL_CALL_ID`, which makes each command's processes name it. | [findings](findings.md#effects-what-a-kernel-sensor-saw) |
| The egress observer's per-minute network totals aren't part of TK-X206 or TK-X207. | The signer reconciles them itself (`reconcile.unexplained_effect`); a total over a minute can't be placed inside a call. | Read `reconcile.unexplained_effect` records for hosts. | [egress](egress.md) |

## PCM 0.1: proof-carrying messages (experimental)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| A grant can't be revoked: it holds until its `expires_at`. | Revocation needs a list every receiver checks; this version has none. | Keep expiries short (the signer refuses more than 24 h); grant per task, not per agent. | [PCM](pcm.md#grants) |
| A receiver accepts a message within 300 s of its own clock, so signer and receiver clocks must agree. It refuses every message signed before it started, and replicas behind one address don't share what they saw, so a message replayed to another replica passes there. `tracekit pcm verify` doesn't check replay at all: its line ends `not checked: replay`, as `pcm.verify`'s `skipped` lists `replay` without a `Replay` and `recipient` without `me`. | Replay state is in memory, one `pcm.Replay` per process; refusing what came before the start is what makes a restart safe without saved state. | Keep both hosts on NTP; after a receiver restarts, the sender sends again; run one replica per receiver: the A2A gateway is merged, and shared replay state comes with enforcing PCM in it, a follow-up. | [PCM](pcm.md#verifying) |
| Only four claim types: `tests_passed`, `approved_by`, `reached_host`, `file_written`. A result the agent's own process reported (T1) is its word, so the claim is `unproven`; `proven` needs a result a gateway or executor wrote (T2), or a record the signer wrote itself (an approval, an `effect.observed`). `file_written` counts only full-content writes by the `Write` tool, not edits. | Each claim needs a checker over records that exist today, and a T1 `ok` is whatever the client sent. | Run calls that matter through the [tool executor](executor.md) or a gateway; observe hosts with the [egress observer](egress.md); send other facts as `payload`, which nothing checks. | [PCM](pcm.md#claims-and-evidence) |
| `approved_by` without `covers` proves only that the person approved the call the agent named `call` (a `read_file` call can be named `refund-81-approved`); what that call was is in the claim's `approved` and its detail, and it meets no `approved_by` a schema lists. With `"covers": true` the verifier checks that the approval covers the request, in two ways only: it issued the message's root grant, or it is of the `tool` the schema names with the intent's args, equal in JCS. So an approval of a range ("refunds up to $200") covers a request only through the grant it issued; under a schema without `tool`, or with an unopened decision of the schema's tool, the claim is `unproven`, and an unopened decision of another tool fails it. The evidence must hold the approval, the `approval.request` it answers and the `policy.decision` that request names, or the claim fails. It fails too, in any order, when its records disagree on what was approved (two requests of one approval naming two decisions, or approvals of the call naming decisions whose tool and args differ in JCS, args null when not opened, as when a person approved two attempts of one call id with other args) or when it lists a record twice. | A claim names a call, not what it did, and an offline verifier can't judge whether other args are close enough: the root grant (whose caveats it checks) and exact args are the two rules every verifier applies alike. Records that name two approved calls give no one answer, and taking the first would make the result hang on their order. | As a receiver, list `approved_by` in the schema's `claims`, so only an approval that covers the request counts, and name the schema's `tool`; for authority over a range, require a grant. As a sender, claim `covers: true` and cite the approval that issued the root grant, or open the decision (`pcm_evidence(..., args=...)`); pass the three records of one approval, once each. | [PCM](pcm.md#what-approved_by-says) |
| With `covers`, an approval of the schema's `tool` with the intent's args covers every message that asks for that tool with those args: to any receiver, as often as the run sends one, each with a new nonce. | An approval is of one call, not of one message: nothing in its records names the message it stands for. | For one-time authority, put a field unique to the request (such as `request_id`) in the schema's fields, so the approved call carries it, and act once per value; for repeated or bounded authority, require a grant, whose caveats and expiry bound it. | [PCM](pcm.md#what-approved_by-says) |
| `tests_passed` and `reached_host` count only calls of tools PCM 0.1's placement table classes as shell or http: 54 tool names frozen from the policy packs of Tracekit 1.4 (`Bash`, `shell`, `WebFetch`, `http_request`…), shipped as `tracekit/pcm/tools.json`. A custom shell or HTTP tool, a tool a pack added since, or another tool with a `command` or `url` field, makes the claim `unproven` ("a tool the verifier can't place as a shell tool"). | The verifier is offline and reads no deployment's policy; every PCM 0.1 verifier must place a tool the same way, so the table is part of the protocol; a `command` field alone doesn't make a shell call. | Run tests and requests through a tool the table names, or send the fact as `payload`. | [PCM](pcm.md#tool-placement) |
| A T2 result's tool name stands for its class: the verifier takes a `Bash` call a gateway or executor ran as a shell call, whatever it ran. An operator who names an executor action after a placed tool of another class (an HTTP action called `Bash`) lets its `ok` prove `tests_passed`. | The record names the tool, not what ran; PCM 0.1 doesn't pin which `via` may attest each class. | Name executor actions and gateway tools after what they do, never after a placed tool of another class; as a receiver, read `run by <via>` in the claim's detail. | [PCM](pcm.md#tool-placement) |
| The verifier trusts the schema documents it is given. The hash pins what a schema says, not who wrote it, and `id` patterns run with Python `re`. | Both sides agree on a schema by its content hash; there is no schema registry. | Ship schemas with the code that uses them and review their patterns like code. | [PCM](pcm.md#schemas) |
| Evidence must come from the sender's own run, under a checkpoint of the log that signed the message. Opening a decision shows its args to the receiver. | An offline check needs an inclusion proof under a pinned note; a claim about args needs the args. | Wait for the next checkpoint (`pcm_evidence` returns `pending` until then; the SDK nudges and waits); claim only what you would show the receiver. | [PCM](pcm.md#claims-and-evidence) |
| Evidence proves a call happened in the sender's run, not that nothing happened after it. A suite that passed, then failed after the code changed, still proves `tests_passed` with the record of the pass; a file written, then overwritten, still proves `file_written` with the first write. Both are `proven`. | The sender picks the records it sends, and PCM 0.1 has no evidence that shows the run's later records. A later version could carry the run since the call and bind `tests_passed` to the git tree it ran on (the [specification](https://github.com/Cygnux-Labs/PCM-protocol/blob/main/SPEC.md#appendix-b-open-issues)'s Appendix B question 5). | As a sender, claim right after the call (`pcm_evidence` has a checkpoint about a second later). As a receiver acting on the current state: refuse a message whose `ts` is more than a maximum age (seconds or a minute) after the oldest `ts` among the claim's evidence records (`tracekit pcm verify` prints that age), or whose evidence is older than the last change you know of; for `tests_passed`, name the suite, have it run after your request, or run it yourself on the code you act on; for `file_written`, hash the file you receive and compare it with the claim's `digest`, the one check that rules a later write out. | [PCM](pcm.md#freshness) |
| The log key signs PCM messages and grants as well as checkpoints, so a fault in PCM signing means rotating the log key. | trust.json already pins the log key, so a receiver needs no new trust entry. A dedicated PCM key needs a trust.json member of its own, which PCM 0.1 doesn't define; it comes with a later PCM version, before PCM leaves experimental. | Treat the log key as the signer's most valuable key ([signing](signing.md)); rotate it if PCM signing is ever in doubt. | [PCM](pcm.md#trust-and-keys) |
| There is no revocation and no key validity period: a leaked log key signs any message, grant or evidence checkpoint, with any `ts` and expiry, and receivers accept them until they remove the key from trust.json. The 24-hour grant cap binds only an honest signer; witness cosignatures (`witnesses_required`) limit forged evidence, not forged messages or grants. | PCM 0.1 trusts the keys trust.json pins and has no revocation list or key lifetime. | Keep the log key where only the signer reads it ([signing](signing.md)); require witness cosignatures on evidence; if the key leaks, remove it from every receiver's trust.json, rotate it, and treat what you accepted under it since the leak as unverified. | [PCM](pcm.md#trust-and-keys) |
| The trust.json PCM reads pins one Ed25519 key per log: two under one log name (an old key kept after a rotation, as [signing](signing.md) asks for bundles) make `pcm.verify` raise and `tracekit pcm verify` exit 2. Rotating the log key means replacing it there, after which messages, grants and evidence the old key signed don't verify. | PCM 0.1 declares no rotation, so a verifier can't tell which key may sign now, and an old key that leaked would sign grants for as long as it stays pinned. | Pass a trust file with only the current key (`tracekit signer trust --config <signer.yaml> -o pcm-trust.json` writes one), apart from the one that keeps old keys for bundles; after a rotation, senders ask for new grants and send again. | [PCM](pcm.md#trust-and-keys) |
| Messages and grants carry an Ed25519 signature only: a trust.json that pins the SLH-DSA hybrid key gets it checked on evidence checkpoints, not on messages or grants. | PCM 0 signs one line; the hybrid line comes when agents need it. | Don't rely on SLH-DSA for PCM authority; keep grant expiries short. | [PCM](pcm.md#trust-and-keys) |
| `reached_host` compares hosts in ASCII only. A call whose URL has any character outside printable ASCII (`!` to `~`) anywhere in it, the path too (a space, tab, newline or other control character, DEL, `bücher.example`, `/café`), or whose host holds `%` (`http://%61.example/`: hosts are never percent-decoded), is unproven: the verifier can't tell which host it reached. So is an effect whose host is of that kind (a claim's host may hold only `a`-`z`, `0`-`9`, `.`, `_`, `:` and `-`, or the claim fails as malformed), a URL with a `[` or `]` in its authority other than one pair around an IPv6 or IPvFuture address that opens the host after the last `@` (`http://[x]@good.com/`, `http://[zz]@[::1]/`, an authority of `[::1]@` alone; a browser reaches `[::1]` from the second), and an IPv6 address whose dotted quad has a leading zero (`[::ffff:01.2.3.4]`). An `xn--` label is compared as written, without a punycode check. | IDNA 2003, UTS 46 and Python's `idna` codec name some Unicode hosts differently, and Python releases read controls, spaces, tabs, newlines, brackets and leading zeros in a URL differently (`urlsplit`, `ipaddress`): PCM 0.1 refuses all of it, so every verifier reads a host alike on every Python. | Fetch and claim hosts in ASCII: an international name in its `xn--` form, as DNS names it (`xn--bcher-kva.example`); percent-encode spaces and non-ASCII characters in the path and query, and keep `%` and brackets out of the host but for an IPv6 address. A call's detail names two causes the sender can fix here: `its URL is not printable ASCII: send it without spaces or control characters, with the host in xn-- form and anything else percent-encoded`, and `its host has a %: send the URL with the host as plain ASCII, not percent-encoded`. Any other cause but the IPv4 one below says only `the verifier can't tell whether call …`, and an effect's host `a host the verifier can't read`: check the URL or host against [Hosts](pcm.md#hosts). | [PCM](pcm.md#hosts) |
| `reached_host` reads an IPv4 address only as a dotted quad: four decimal numbers 0 to 255 without leading zeros (`127.0.0.1`, `10.0.0.1`). A host whose last label is a number (digits, or `0x` and hex digits) written any other way, in a URL, a claim or an effect (`0127.0.0.1`, `010.0.0.1`, `127.1`, `0x7f.0.0.1`, `2130706433`, `1.2.3.4.5`), is unproven. | Resolvers read those forms apart: macOS connects to `127.0.0.1` for `0127.0.0.1`, where `inet_aton` reads `87.0.0.1`, so no verifier can say which host the call reached. | Fetch and claim IPv4 addresses as dotted quads. A call's or a claim's detail then ends with `its host is an IPv4 address not written as a dotted quad: send it as four decimal numbers 0-255 without leading zeros`; an effect's says only `a host the verifier can't read`. | [PCM](pcm.md#hosts) |
| `reached_host` can't tell the host of a URL that starts with `view-source:` or `blob:` (`blob:https://api.example.com/x`): a call of one is unproven. | A browser's address bar reads the URL inside, while WHATWG URL (Node's `URL`) reads no host from it, so a verifier can't tell what the call reached. | Fetch the URL inside (`https://api.example.com/x`). | [PCM](pcm.md#hosts) |
| `reached_host` can't tell the host of a URL with no scheme that doesn't start with `//` (`/api.example.com/x`, `api.example.com/x`, `api.example.com:8080/x`): a call of one is unproven. | A client given a base URL (an HTTP client's `base_url`, a page's `fetch`) reads it as a path on the base's host, while an address bar or curl reads a host from it, so a verifier can't tell what the call reached. | Fetch the whole URL, with its scheme and host (`https://api.example.com/x`); the claim's detail says so. | [PCM](pcm.md#hosts) |
| `reached_host` from a call needs its T2 result `ok`: a call the host answered with an HTTP error status (a 404, a 500) is recorded as not ok, so the claim fails and the message is rejected, though the host was reached; the claim's detail says what to do. | The evidence records whether a result was ok and a hash of it, not the status or whether a response came back; telling an error answer from a failed connection needs a new field in the evidence format (a PCM 0.2 question, the [specification](https://github.com/Cygnux-Labs/PCM-protocol/blob/main/SPEC.md#appendix-b-open-issues)'s Appendix B question 6). | Claim the host from a call that succeeded, or from the [egress observer](egress.md)'s `effect.observed`, which counts the connection whatever the answer. | [PCM](pcm.md#claims-and-evidence) |
| `reached_host` reads the host alone and ignores the port: the claim doesn't say which port was reached, and a URL a browser refuses, such as one with a port above 65535 (`https://api.example.com:99999/`) or an `xn--` label that is no valid punycode, can still name its host. | Every verifier must read a URL alike, and checking ports and punycode would add rules each must share. A client that refuses such a URL fails the call, and a failed call proves nothing. | When the port matters, read it in the evidence: the URL in the call's opened args, or an effect's `port`. | [PCM](pcm.md#hosts) |
| `reached_host` is proven by an `effect.observed` only when the egress observer saw a connection to the host open (`connections` above 0). A kernel sensor's or OpenShell's `connect` is the attempt, and an egress minute with no connection is a connect that failed or a later minute of a connection opened before: each makes the claim unproven ("saw no connection to … open", "… an attempt"). | Tetragon reports the connect call, not whether it opened; the egress observer counts a connection once its upstream connect succeeds. | Route the run's traffic through the [egress observer](egress.md) and send the minute in which the connection opened, or make the request with an http tool through the [tool executor](executor.md). | [PCM](pcm.md#claims-and-evidence) |
| `tests_passed` knows a fixed list of test commands by bare name, without wrappers: `uv run pytest`, `CI=1 pytest`, `.venv/bin/pytest`, `./gradlew test` and `npm run test:unit` are no test command, so the claim fails. A suite is one line of plain words: no operator, redirection, `$`, backquote, backslash, `#`, brace or glob character. Of the flags that run no tests it refuses `-h`, `-V` and `--help`, `--version`, `--collect-only` and their prefixes (`--he`, `--co`), not others: `pytest -qh`, `pytest --fixtures`, `go test -run NONE`, `go test -exec true`, `make test SHELL=/bin/true`, `npm test --script-shell=true`, `cargo test --no-run` and `mvn test -DskipTests` pass and run no test. `proven` means this exact command line exited 0 on the executor, with whatever program its `PATH` names `pytest`. | Every PCM 0.1 verifier must recognise the same commands, so the list is part of the protocol, not a guess at what a command does; every flag of every runner can't be listed. | Run and claim the suite as the list writes it (`pytest`, `python -m pytest`, with the venv on `PATH`); as a receiver, compare `suite` with the suites you accept before trusting the claim. | [PCM](pcm.md#test-commands) |
| `file_written` needs an absolute path: one from `/`, read by POSIX rules (`\` is part of a name, so `/repo\a.py` is not `/repo/a.py`, even when the call ran on Windows), or a drive path (`C:\repo\a.py`, `C:/repo/a.py`), read by Windows rules. A drive path is proven only by a `Write` call of the very same string, which assumes the call ran on Windows: on a Linux executor that string is a relative file name, and the claim is still proven. The same path spelled otherwise (`C:/repo/a.py` for `C:\repo\a.py`) is unproven. Paths compare case by case, so a call of `c:\repo\a.py` fails a claim of `C:\repo\a.py`. A UNC path (`\\server\share\a.py`), `\repo\a.py` and `C:a.py` are relative, so such a claim is malformed. A `Write` call whose path is relative, or holds a `..`, proves nothing: the claim is unproven when that path ends with the claimed one. | A relative path names a different file from each directory, and the record holds neither the working directory nor the system that ran the call: `C:/repo/a.py` is a relative path on POSIX. | Write files by absolute path, and claim the path exactly as the call wrote it; as a receiver, take a proven drive path as a Windows file only when you know the sender's executor runs Windows. | [PCM](pcm.md#paths) |
| A root grant needs the approval of a person signer.yaml's `approvals.persons` names: an approver it doesn't map, an OIDC person included, gets `forbidden` from `pcm_grant`, and a receiver rejects a root grant whose issuer names no person. | The receiver checks the authority by its person (`grants.root.person`); an identity alone names no one across channels. | Map each identity that approves `pcm.grant` to its person in `approvals.persons`. | [PCM](pcm.md#grants) |
| A root grant needs a person's approval: an agent approver can't issue one. The verifier reports who issued the root grant (`grants.root`) but doesn't check it is someone the receiver accepts. | A grant without caveats is the widest grant, and agent approvers can't bound caveats yet. | Have people approve `pcm.grant`; check `grants.root.person` against the people you accept. | [PCM](pcm.md#grants) |
| Narrowing is checked per caveat: an equality is narrowed only by the same equality (or a one-value `in` for strings), and `>= 81` with `<= 81` doesn't count as `== 81`. `pcm_delegate` takes only grants this signer signed. | A simple rule, tested over every operator pair, is safer than a complete solver. | Write a child's caveats in its parent's form. | [PCM](pcm.md#grants) |
| A bundle of a run that sent messages or made grants shows `not interpreted` warnings for `ext.pcm.sent` and `ext.pcm.grant` (exit 0): "1 record(s) carry ext.pcm.sent: their signatures, chain and inclusion are checked above; what ext.pcm.sent means is not checked". | The verifiers have no check for these records yet; one comes when PCM leaves experimental. | Expect the warnings; the records are signed and in the chain like every other. | [verdicts](verdicts.md#a-bundle-that-needs-a-newer-verifier) |
| Nothing enforces PCM between agents yet: the A2A gateway forwards PCM parts without checking them, and there is no `transfer` payload. | The A2A gateway is merged; enforcing PCM in it, and a `transfer` payload, are a follow-up. | Call `pcm.verify` in the receiving agent before it acts. | [PCM](pcm.md#agent-to-agent-transport) |
| TypeScript has the four RPC schemas, so its client can call them, but no PCM verifier. | No TypeScript agent receives PCM yet. | Verify in Python, or with `tracekit pcm verify`. | [PCM](pcm.md#python-sdk) |

## Intent grants (experimental)

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| A grant covers one run: a new run of the same agent asks again. | A grant's totals are counted in its run's records, so that run's bundle is enough to recount them. | Grant each run (`tracekit grant add --run RUN`). A grant over several runs is a later step. | [intent grants](intent-grants.md#the-grant) |
| An intent grant is no authority in a PCM message: `pcm.verify` refuses a message that carries one, and `pcm_delegate` refuses one as a parent. | Its limits, the tools it covers, its run and its revocation are only in its run's records, which a receiver doesn't have; PCM 0.1's caveats can't say a total or a count. So it is signed under a tag of its own (`tracekit.intent-grant.v2`), not PCM's. | For authority another agent checks, issue a PCM grant from a person's approval (`pcm_grant`), whose caveats and expiry are all it needs. | [intent grants](intent-grants.md#the-grant) |
| Grants cover payments only (`payment.send`). | Payments are the first intent; production changes and messages to people come next. | Approve other held calls one at a time. | [intent grants](intent-grants.md#later) |
| A grant reads an integer `amount` as the currency's smallest unit (cents, a token's base unit) and `amount_cents` as hundredths of its whole unit. A tool that takes whole dollars in `amount` is read 100 times smaller. | The arguments carry no unit; the smallest unit is Stripe's convention, and the one a grant compares exactly. | Before you grant, read the `grant` line of `tracekit approvals show`: it prints the amount as the grant reads it. If it reads wrong, approve that tool's calls one at a time. | [what a grant reads](intent-grants.md#what-a-grant-reads) |
| A payment typed into a shell command (`curl` to a payments API, the Stripe CLI) stays held. | Its amount and payee are text in a command line; a grant reads a tool's structured arguments. | Pay through an MCP or framework tool with structured arguments. | [what a grant reads](intent-grants.md#what-a-grant-reads) |
| On the dev signer, any identity of the run's tenant may issue a grant, as it may answer the run's approvals, and the agent runs as your user, so it could issue one (recorded self-approved, which makes the run's assurance `dev`, as a self-approval does; its bundle needs a 1.6 verifier, and an older one reports it `UNVERIFIABLE (needs tracekit >= 1.6.0)` rather than read it as witnessed, but only while its manifest is as exported: the manifest isn't signed, and edited down to 1.4.0 it lets an older verifier read the bundle as witnessed; a 1.6 verifier recomputes what the records need and warns `verifier minimum`). | The dev signer can't tell you from your agent. The dev pack denies every `tracekit grant` and `approvals approve` command the agent runs (`TK-D012`) as a tripwire; code that calls `grant_issue` itself (`python -c`, a script) isn't read, as for approvals. | Run [system mode](deploy.md#laptop-system-mode), or a signer with `approvals`, where only a named approver issues grants. Verify bundles with 1.6 or later, and treat a `verifier minimum` warning as an edited manifest. | [who may issue](intent-grants.md#who-may-issue-a-grant) |
| A release at T1 rests on the arguments the harness reported: the agent's own process runs the call. | A hook sees a call before it runs; the agent's process runs it. | Run payments through the [tool executor](executor.md) or the [MCP gateway](mcp-gateway.md) (T2): the call runs with the arguments the signer decided. | [records](intent-grants.md#records-and-verification) |
| A payment counts when it is decided, also when the payment then fails. | Counting at the decision keeps two payments at once from spending the same room. | Revoke the grant and issue a new one when failed payments used up its total. | [how the signer decides](intent-grants.md#how-the-signer-decides-a-payment) |
| The fields a release records are the signer's reading of the arguments. The verifier checks them against the grant; checking them against the arguments is a manual step. | The bundle holds the arguments as commitments, which the verifier doesn't open. | Open them with `tracekit signer reveal` and read them with `grants.read` in `tracekit/grants.py`. | [records](intent-grants.md#records-and-verification) |
| Grants know the units of USD, EUR, GBP, INR, CAD, AUD, CHF, SGD, JPY, USDC and USDT: a payment in another currency stays held, and a grant can't be issued in one. | A fixed table in `tracekit/grants.py`, each currency with the digits of its smallest unit. | Ask for the currency you need in an issue. | [what a grant reads](intent-grants.md#what-a-grant-reads) |
| A payment with any argument besides its amount, currency, payee, `description` and `note` stays held: an idempotency key or an invoice id too. | A grant releases only what it reads; an argument it doesn't read may pick what is paid or where the money goes (`memo` routes funds on some chains). | Approve those calls one at a time. | [what a grant reads](intent-grants.md#what-a-grant-reads) |
| The Go verifier in `contrib/` reports the grant records as not interpreted. | It hasn't been ported yet. | Verify with `tracekit verify` or the TypeScript verifier. | [records](intent-grants.md#records-and-verification) |

## Tracekit Box (experimental)

A Tracekit Box daemon named in signer.yaml's `box_daemons` labels its box's run (`ext.box`), and opens each box's agent channel, through which the coding agent's hooks inside the box record its calls as the box's own identity, `box:<uid>/<box id>`.

| Limit | Why it is so | What to do | Details |
|---|---|---|---|
| A box's label proves only that the signing key was outside that box: verify's `boxed agent` line is `PROOF AGAINST THE BOXED AGENT (micro-VM)`, never the general `PROOF AGAINST THE AGENT`. Anything running as the Box daemon's user or the signer's user on the host is outside the claim: it can open a box's channel and write as the box, or reach the key. | The signer knows a box only through its daemon's Unix socket connection, which any process of that uid can make. | Run the Box daemon and the signer as users of their own; pin a witness the host's operator doesn't run, so a rewrite of the store shows. | [verdicts](verdicts.md#warnings---strict-exits-3) |
| An agent run's records are the agent's own account of its calls. Its hooks run inside the box under its control: a hostile agent can skip them, lie in them or ignore a block. Box's doors (each connection, each file written back at `apply`) are what held it, and their records, in the box's own run, are what it could do. | The channel carries what the agent says; only the doors sit outside it. | Read the agent's run beside its box run's door records, and act on the doors: they, not the hooks, are the enforcement. | Box's `docs/agent-channel.md` |
| The label's box id, image digest and allowlist names are the Box daemon's account; only its engine comes from the signer's config. The signer checks none of the three. | The daemon is the trusted party that ran the box; the signer never saw it. | Compare them with the box run's `tkbox.box` records, which the daemon wrote too. | Box's `docs/agent-channel.md` |
| An agent run's `run.registered` names the Box daemon's uid as its identity (what the transport established) and `signer_isolation: unknown`; its box is only in `ext.box`. The viewer, report and graph show the daemon's uid for it. | The recorded identity is a closed part of the format: a new `box` scheme there would make every older verifier refuse the bundle. Inside the signer the box's identity owns the run. | Read verify's `boxed agent` line, or `ext.box`, for the box. | [format v2](format-v2.md#9-bundle-layout) |
| A verifier older than 1.6.0 claims nothing for an agent run: it reads `isolation: unknown` and a `not interpreted` warning for `ext.box`. For the box's own run it reads the isolation measured for the daemon, so it may print the general proof line, about the daemon. | `ext.box` is non-critical (format-v2.md §9): an older verifier still verifies the records. | Verify box bundles with a verifier that reads `ext.box`. | [format v2](format-v2.md#9-bundle-layout) |
| One box id is one identity per daemon: two boxes a daemon names alike share their owner, quotas, request ids and cooldowns (each still reaches only its own box run's runs and their approvals). | The identity is `box:<uid>/<box id>`, and the daemon names the box. | Box gives each box an id of its own. | [Box channel](#tracekit-box-experimental) |
| `box_daemons` takes uids only, so a Box daemon reaches the signer over its Unix socket, never over HTTPS or the TCP dev transport, and only the `firecracker` engine (`micro-VM`). The dev signer has no `box_daemons`. | A channel binds one connection; HTTPS has none to bind, and Box has one engine. | Run a configured signer (`tracekit signer serve --config`) on the Box host. | [Box channel](#tracekit-box-experimental) |
| The channel needs signer protocol 21. A signer of protocol 20 or older refuses `channel_open` and register_run's `box`, so Box runs with no channel and no label; a 1.6 client (protocol 22) refuses an older signer. | The RPC contract is frozen: new calls and fields are a new protocol version, and a signer serves older clients, not newer ones. | Upgrade the signer first, then Box and the agents' Tracekit. | [deployment](deploy.md#upgrading) |
| TK-X212 (`tracekit analyze --v2` on a box run) sees only what crosses a door: connections and the files written back at `apply`. What the agent does inside the box (a process, a read, a file it writes then reverts or that stays out of the patch) has no door record, so only a fetch tool's call is checked for a missing connection: never a shell's `curl` (its exit status claims nothing about it), nor a write call against the patch (the patch is the net change). A door event is matched to a call by time alone: a connection made while a call that may connect ran counts as that call's, so one hidden inside a long shell call is missed. A fetch is matched by host and port: Claude Code's WebFetch follows a redirect only when the hosts differ by a leading `www.` (same protocol and port), which counts as the fetch's own; it reports a redirect to another host instead of following it, but a fetch tool that follows one shows that host's connection as a finding. | Box's doors are the only records outside the agent's reach, and they name a host or a path, never the process or call behind it. | Read the agent's calls around long shell calls yourself; prefer fetch tools that report a redirect to another host instead of following it, as Claude Code's WebFetch does. | [findings](findings.md#boxes-what-tracekit-box-saw-tk-x212) |
| Some honest door events are TK-X212 findings, and some can't be told. Findings: the harness's own traffic other than its model calls (Claude Code's `statsig.anthropic.com` when `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_NONESSENTIAL_TRAFFIC` isn't set), a file the harness writes in `/workspace` for itself, and the setup half of a command that both sets up and starts the agent (`sh -c 'npm ci && claude …'`), which counts as the agent's. Not checkable: every later connection in a run after a shell call that may have left a process running (`background`), every file of the patch no write call named once any shell call ran (`shell_ran`), and every door event while no agent run was open (`no_agent_run`): before its hooks start, or after the agent closes its run, which it can do early. So the agent's own word can hide the rest of its run from the check, though never silently: one call it marks `run_in_background` (any call can be marked) makes every later connection of the run `background`, and one call it never completes makes them `call_unfinished`. | The doors don't say which process acted; the rules count what they can't tell instead of guessing, and treat only model API hosts as the harness's own. | Keep the settings of Box's Claude Code image (non-essential traffic off); start host setup in a command of its own; review `no_agent_run`, `background` and `call_unfinished` door events by hand (`tkbox events`). | [findings](findings.md#boxes-what-tracekit-box-saw-tk-x212) |
| An agent that never registers a run keeps no account at all: TK-X212 then has no call to compare, and counts every door event `no_agent_account` (the summary line says `NO AGENT ACCOUNT`), not a finding. That reading needs a final box run, a source that holds every run of the box's tenant (the signer's store, or a run-set bundle from registry 0 with no run still open, whose range holds the box run's own registration: a run-set bundles only final runs, so a box stopped mid-session leaves its agent's open run out, and it may carry its selected run from outside its range), and no `register_run` Box refused on the channel (`tkbox.channel`, a count of refusals Box folded, or such records lost: Box's refusal is not the agent's). Short of that the same box reads `no_agent_runs`, since its agent's runs may simply not be there. | The agent's hooks run inside the box, under its control; skipping them is the simplest way to leave nothing to compare. | Analyse finished box runs from the store or a run-set exported from registry 0 after the box's runs closed; treat `NO AGENT ACCOUNT` as every door event unexplained. | [findings](findings.md#boxes-what-tracekit-box-saw-tk-x212) |
| Which processes a shell call leaves running is read from its command by the policy engine's shell parser: `run_in_background`, a command it sends to the background (`&` after a command, an and-or list or a block such as `{ …; }`, if or while, in a subshell or an `sh -c` string too), or a command that detaches by itself or runs one later (setsid, disown, coproc, `tmux … -d`, `screen -d…`, systemd-run, at, batch); a line the parser can't read counts. Other ways stay out: a program that daemonizes itself (`redis-server --daemonize yes`, `daemonize`, `start-stop-daemon --background`), `sudo -b`, `ssh -f`, `docker run -d`, an asynchronous process substitution `>(…)`, an interpreter that spawns a detached child (Python, Node), a script file that starts one. Each errs toward a false TK-X212 finding for its later connections, never toward silence. A command Box started from the host that leaves a daemon running is the same: its daemon's connections after the command ended are findings. | The command line is all the record holds of what a call started; a program's own forking isn't in it. | Start servers and daemons in the same call or command that uses them, or with `run_in_background`; read such findings against the agent's earlier commands. | [findings](findings.md#boxes-what-tracekit-box-saw-tk-x212) |
